:
Components of the displacement supermultiplet with their scaling dimensions and the masses of the dual
\text{AdS}_{2}
string excitations. The mass is obtained through the AdS/CFT dictionary
m^{2}=\Delta(\Delta-1)
for the bosons and
m^{2}=(\Delta-\frac{1}{2})^{2}
for the fermions.

3.2.1
Symmetry Breaking and Superconformal Algebra

The
1/2
BPS Wilson line in ABJM breaks the three-dimensional
\mathcal{N}=6
superconformal algebra
osp(6|4)
to the one-dimensional
\mathcal{N}=6
superconformal algebra
su(1,1|3)
. This algebra is generated by
\{D,P,K,R_{a}^{b},J;Q^{a},\bar{Q}_{a},S^{a},\bar{S}_{a}\}
, where
a=1,2,3
and the generators can be identified with
osp(6|4)
generators (see Appendix A of
[
1
]
and C of
[
163
]
). The bosonic generators
\{D,P,K\}
form the one-dimensional conformal algebra
su(1,1)\sim so(2,1)
, the traceless generators
{R_{a}}^{b}
(
a,b=1,2,3
) form SU
(3)
, and
J_{0}
forms the
u(1)
R-symmetry. Their commutation relations are

\displaystyle[P,K]

\displaystyle=-2D\,,

\displaystyle~~[D,P]

\displaystyle=P\,,

\displaystyle[D,K]

\displaystyle=-K

\displaystyle[{R_{a}}^{b},{R_{c}}^{d}]

\displaystyle=\delta_{a}^{d}{R_{c}}^{b}-\delta^{b}_{c}{R_{a}}^{d}\,,

(3.13)

and
J_{0}
commutes with all of them.
The anticommutation relations for the fermionic generators
Q^{a}
,
\overline{Q}_{a}
and the corresponding superconformal charges
S^{a},~\overline{S}_{a}
,
a=1,2,3
are

\displaystyle\{Q^{a},\bar{Q}_{b}\}

\displaystyle=2\delta^{a}_{b}P\,,

\displaystyle\{S^{a},\bar{S}_{b}\}

\displaystyle=2\delta^{a}_{b}K

(3.14)

\displaystyle\{Q^{a},\bar{S}_{b}\}

\displaystyle=2\delta_{b}^{a}(D+\tfrac{1}{3}J_{0})-2{R_{b}}^{a}

\displaystyle\{\bar{Q}_{a},S^{b}\}

\displaystyle=2\delta_{b}^{a}(D-\tfrac{1}{3}J_{0})+2{R_{a}}^{b}\,,

(3.15)

and the non-vanishing mixed commutators are

\displaystyle[D,Q^{a}]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}Q^{a}

\displaystyle[D,\overline{Q}_{a}]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\overline{Q}_{a}

\displaystyle[K,Q^{a}]

\displaystyle=S^{a}

\displaystyle[K,\overline{Q}_{a}]

\displaystyle=\overline{S}_{a}

(3.16)

\displaystyle[D,S^{a}]

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}S^{a}

\displaystyle[D,\overline{S}_{a}]

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\overline{S}_{a}

\displaystyle[P,S^{a}]

\displaystyle=-Q^{a}

\displaystyle[P,\overline{S}_{a}]

\displaystyle=-\overline{Q}_{a}

(3.17)

\displaystyle[{R_{a}}^{b},Q^{c}]

\displaystyle=\delta_{a}^{c}Q^{b}-\tfrac{1}{3}\delta_{a}^{b}Q^{c}

\displaystyle[{R_{a}}^{b},\overline{Q}_{c}]

\displaystyle=-\delta_{c}^{b}\overline{Q}_{a}+\tfrac{1}{3}\delta_{a}^{b}\overline{Q}_{c}

\displaystyle[J_{0},Q^{a}]

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}Q^{a}

\displaystyle[J_{0},\overline{Q}_{a}]

\displaystyle=-\tfrac{1}{2}\overline{Q}_{a}

(3.18)

\displaystyle[{R_{a}}^{b},S^{c}]

\displaystyle=\delta_{a}^{c}S^{b}-\tfrac{1}{3}\delta_{a}^{b}S^{c}

\displaystyle[{R_{a}}^{b},\overline{S}_{c}]

\displaystyle=-\delta_{c}^{b}\overline{S}_{a}+\tfrac{1}{3}\delta_{a}^{b}\overline{S}_{c}

\displaystyle[J_{0},S^{a}]

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}S^{a}

\displaystyle[J_{0},\overline{S}_{a}]

\displaystyle=-\tfrac{1}{2}\overline{S}_{a}.

(3.19)

The quadratic Casimir is

\displaystyle{\bf C}^{(2)}=D^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\,\{K,P\}+\frac{1}{3}J_{0}^{2}-\frac{1}{2}{R_{a}}^{b}\,{R_{b}}^{a}+\frac{1}{4}\,[\overline{S}_{a},Q^{a}]+\frac{1}{4}\,[S^{a},\overline{Q}_{a}]~,

(3.20)

and, when acting on a highest weight state
[\Delta,j_{0},j_{1},j_{2}]
of
su(1,1|3)
, it has eigenvalue

\displaystyle{\bf c_{2}}=\Delta(\Delta+2)+\frac{j_{0}^{2}}{3}\,-\frac{1}{3}(j_{1}^{2}+j_{1}j_{2}+j_{2}^{2}).

(3.21)

To compute the superconformal blocks, we must represent the above algebra as an action on superconformal primaries. For this purpose, we use the superspace
(t,\theta_{a},\bar{\theta}^{a})
introduced above.
3
3

3

The natural superspace to study correlation functions in the bulk was introduced in
[
168
]
, and the setup adopted here can be seen as the reduction of the one considered there.
We can then write the differential action of the
\mathcal{N}=6
generators as

\displaystyle P

\displaystyle=-\partial_{t}

(3.22)

\displaystyle D

\displaystyle=\textstyle{-t\partial_{t}-\frac{1}{2}\theta_{a}\partial^{a}-\frac{1}{2}\bar{\theta}^{a}\bar{\partial}_{a}-\Delta}

(3.23)

\displaystyle K

\displaystyle=-t^{2}\partial_{t}-(t+\theta\bar{\theta})\theta_{a}\partial^{a}-(t-\theta\bar{\theta})\bar{\theta}^{a}\bar{\partial}_{a}-(\theta\bar{\theta})^{2}\partial_{t}-2\,t\,\Delta+\frac{2}{3}j_{0}\,\theta\bar{\theta}

(3.24)

\displaystyle Q^{a}

\displaystyle=\partial^{a}-\bar{\theta}^{a}\partial_{t}

(3.25)

\displaystyle\bar{Q}_{a}

\displaystyle=\bar{\partial}_{a}-\theta_{a}\partial_{t}

(3.26)

\displaystyle S^{a}

\displaystyle=(t+\theta\bar{\theta})\partial^{a}-(t-\theta\bar{\theta})\bar{\theta}^{a}\partial_{t}-2\bar{\theta}^{a}\bar{\theta}^{b}\bar{\partial}_{b}-2(\Delta+\frac{1}{3}j_{0})\bar{\theta}^{a}

(3.27)

\displaystyle\bar{S}_{a}

\displaystyle=(t-\theta\bar{\theta})\bar{\partial}_{a}-(t+\theta\bar{\theta})\theta_{a}\partial_{t}-2\theta_{a}\theta_{b}\partial^{b}-2(\Delta-\frac{1}{3}j_{0})\theta_{a}

(3.28)

\displaystyle J_{0}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\theta_{a}\partial^{a}+\frac{1}{2}\bar{\theta}^{a}\bar{\partial}_{a}+j_{0}

(3.29)

\displaystyle R_{a}{}^{b}

\displaystyle=-\theta_{a}\partial^{b}+\bar{\theta}^{b}\bar{\partial}_{a}+\textstyle{\frac{1}{3}}\,\delta_{a}^{b}\,(\theta_{c}\partial^{c}-\bar{\theta}^{c}\bar{\partial}_{c})

(3.30)

where
\partial^{a}=\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta_{a}}
,
\bar{\partial}_{a}=\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{\theta}^{a}}
,
\theta\bar{\theta}=\theta_{a}\bar{\theta}^{a}
and we neglect the SU
(3)
charges
j_{1}
and
j_{2}
because we will only be interested in neutral superfields.
The superspace is also equipped with super covariant derivatives

\displaystyle\mathsf{D}^{a}=\partial^{a}+\bar{\theta}^{a}\partial_{t}\,,\qquad\qquad\bar{\mathsf{D}}_{a}=\bar{\partial}_{a}+\theta_{a}\partial_{t}\,.

(3.31)

3.2.2
Displacement Multiplet

This section is devoted to defining and constructing the displacement super multiplet for a line defect given by the
1/2
BPS Wilson line in ABJM. For completeness, we start by recapitulating some basic facts about this theory. The gauge sector consists of two gauge fields
A_{\mu}
and
\hat{A}_{\mu}
belonging respectively to the adjoint of
U(N)
and
\hat{U}(N)
. The matter sector instead contains the complex scalar fields
C_{I}
,
\bar{C}^{I}
, and the fermions
\psi_{I}
and
\bar{\psi}^{I}
. The fields
(C_{I},\bar{\psi}^{I})
transforms in the bifundamental
(N,\bar{N})
while the couple
(\bar{C}^{I},\psi_{I})
lives in the
(\bar{N},N)
. The additional capital index
I=1,2,3,4
labels the (anti)fundamental representation of the R-symmetry group SU
(4)
. The kinetic term for the gauge fields consists of two Chern-Simon actions of opposite level
(k,-k)
, while the ones for scalars and fermions take the standard form in terms of the usual covariant derivatives. To ensure super-conformality, the action is also endowed with a suitable sextic scalar potential and
\psi^{2}C^{2}
Yukawa-type interactions explicitly spelt out in
[
150
,
169
]
.

An infinitesimal variation of the Wilson line (
3.1
) translates into an operator insertion according to the identity

\frac{\braket{(\delta\mathcal{W})\dots}}{\braket{\mathcal{W}}}=-i\int_{\mathcal{C}}dt\,\langle\delta\mathcal{L}(t)\dots\rangle_{\mathcal{W}},

(3.32)

where on the left-hand side (l.h.s.) we are considering an arbitrary correlator with the deformed Wilson line while on the right-hand side (r.h.s.) we are using the definition (
1.50
). Notice that
\delta
could be any generator of a symmetry broken by the Wilson line. When
\delta
is the action of a broken fermionic or bosonic charge inside the parent superalgebra, the resulting operators can be related to an element of the
super-multiplet of the
displacement operator. Consider, for instance, the six broken R-symmetry generators
J_{1}{}^{a}\equiv\mathsf{J}^{a}
and
J_{a}{}^{1}\equiv\bar{\mathsf{J}}_{a}
(see Appendix
B.1
). Their action will yield six bosonic defect operators by the symbolic action

\displaystyle[\mathsf{J}^{a},\mathcal{W}]

\displaystyle\equiv i\delta_{\mathsf{J}^{a}}\mathcal{W}=\int dt~\mathcal{W}[\mathbb{O}^{a}(t)]

\displaystyle[\bar{\mathsf{J}}_{a},\mathcal{W}]

\displaystyle\equiv i\delta_{\bar{\mathsf{J}}_{a}}\mathcal{W}=\int dt~\mathcal{W}[\bar{\mathbb{O}}_{a}(t)],

(3.33)

where we indicate by
\mathcal{W}[\mathbb{O}^{a}(t)]
an operator that is inserted on the Wilson line. The equations (
3.33
) are Ward identities and need to be thought of as inserted in some correlation function. The defect operator
\mathbb{O}^{a}(t)
has conformal dimension
\Delta=1
since the line defect is dimensionless and the dilatations commute with
\mathsf{J}^{a}
. The
U(1)_{J_{0}}
charge is
2
and it can be read from the commutator
[J_{0},\mathsf{J}^{a}]=-2[J_{1}{}^{1},\mathsf{J}^{a}]=2\mathsf{J}^{a}
. Finally, it transforms in the fundamental representation of SU
(3)
. Namely, this operator is characterised by the following set of four quantum numbers
[1,2,1,0].
Similarly for
\bar{\mathbb{O}}_{a}(t)
we have
[1,-2,0,1].

The six broken supercharges
Q^{1a}_{-}\equiv i\bar{\mathsf{Q}}^{a}
and
Q^{ab}_{{+}}=\epsilon^{abc}\mathsf{Q}_{c}
define six fermionic defect operators

\displaystyle[\mathsf{Q}_{a},\mathcal{W}]

\displaystyle=\int dt~\mathcal{W}[\mathbb{\Lambda}_{a}(t)]

\displaystyle[\bar{\mathsf{Q}}^{a},\mathcal{W}]

\displaystyle=\int dt~\mathcal{W}[\bar{\mathbb{\Lambda}}^{a}(t)].

(3.34)

The quantum numbers of the line defect and the commutation relations of the broken and preserved charges fix the quantum numbers of the defect operators. We find
[\frac{3}{2},\frac{5}{2},0,1]
for
\mathbb{\Lambda}_{a}(t)
and
[\frac{3}{2},-\frac{5}{2},1,0]
for

\bar{\mathbb{\Lambda}}^{a}(t)
.
Finally, we can consider the two broken translations
\mathsf{P}
and
\bar{\mathsf{P}}
in the directions orthogonal to the defect. They define the displacement operators

\displaystyle[\mathsf{P},\mathcal{W}]

\displaystyle=\int dt\mathcal{W}[\mathbb{D}(t)]

\displaystyle[\bar{\mathsf{P}},\mathcal{W}]

\displaystyle=\int dt\mathcal{W}[\bar{\mathbb{D}}(t)]

(3.35)

with charges
[2,3,0,0]
and
[2,-3,0,0]
respectively.
The above construction does not uniquely determine the above set of operators. Since they are defined as objects inserted in the defect, and we integrate over the position along the loop, we can add a total derivative with respect to
t
to the integrand without altering the result.
The set of defect operators obtained through the action of the broken charges organizes itself as a supermultiplet. The action of the preserved supercharges
Q^{a}=Q^{1a}_{+}
and
\bar{Q}_{a}=i\,\frac{1}{2}\epsilon_{abc}Q_{-}^{bc}
on them is essentially dictated by the commutation relations between broken and preserved charges. For instance, to compute
[Q^{a},\mathbb{O}^{b}]
one has simply to consider the commutator
[Q^{a},\mathsf{J}^{b}]
acting on
\mathcal{W}
and we get

\displaystyle[Q^{a},\mathsf{J}^{b}]

\displaystyle=Q^{ab}_{+}=\epsilon^{abc}\mathsf{Q}_{c}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle[Q^{a},\mathbb{O}^{b}]

\displaystyle=\epsilon^{abc}\mathbb{\Lambda}_{c}.

(3.36)

From the commutation relation of
Q_{a}
with the broken supercharges and translation, we immediately find

\displaystyle\{Q^{a},\mathbb{\Lambda}_{b}\}

\displaystyle=-2\delta^{a}_{b}\mathbb{D}\qquad\text{and}\qquad[Q^{a},\mathbb{D}]=0.

(3.37)

Fixing the action of
\bar{Q}_{a}
on these defect operators requires more attention. The naïve application of the above procedure would give zero since
\bar{Q}_{a}
commutes with all the broken charges. This is inconsistent with
\{Q^{a},\bar{Q}_{b}\}=2\delta^{a}_{b}P
, where
P
generates the translations along the line. However, as stressed above, any result obtained from (
3.33
), (
3.34
), and (
3.35
) is
defined up to a derivative with respect to
t
, which yields zero when integrated. To fix the form of these derivatives in the commutation relations, it is more convenient to use the super-Jacobi identities. For instance, we can determine
[\bar{Q}_{c},\mathbb{D}]
as follows
4
4

4

We choose to represent
P
as
-\partial_{t}
and then
[P,\Phi]=\partial_{t}\Phi
, see the discussion in
[
170
]
.

\displaystyle 0=\{\bar{Q}_{c},[Q^{a},\mathbb{D}]\}-\{Q^{a},[\mathbb{D},\bar{Q}_{c}]\}+[\mathbb{D},\{\bar{Q}_{c},Q^{a}\}]=Q^{a},[\bar{Q}_{c},\mathbb{D}]+\partial_{t}\mathbb{\Lambda}_{c}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\qquad[\bar{Q}_{c},\mathbb{D}]=-\partial_{t}\mathbb{\Lambda}_{c}.

(3.38)

where we assumed that the action of the translation
P
is realised by
-\partial_{t}
to be consistent with (
3.22
).
Similarly we can show that
\{\bar{Q}_{a},\mathbb{\Lambda}_{b}\}=-2\epsilon_{abc}\partial_{t}\mathbb{O}^{c}
. Since we do not have an operator of lower dimension, it would be natural to set
[\bar{Q}_{c},\mathbb{O}^{b}]=0
. However, this choice is inconsistent with the super-Jacobi identity

\displaystyle 0=

\displaystyle\{\bar{Q}_{c},[Q^{a},\mathbb{O}^{b}]\}-\{Q^{a},[\mathbb{O}^{b},\bar{Q}_{c}]\}+[\mathbb{O}^{b},\{\bar{Q}_{c},Q^{a}\}]=-2\delta_{c}^{b}\partial_{t}\mathbb{O}^{a}+\{Q^{a},[\bar{Q}_{c},\mathbb{O}^{b}]\}.

(3.39)

The consistency of (
3.39
) suggests the existence of an additional fermionic operator
\mathbb{F}
, that is a singlet under SU
(3)
,
which obeys the anticommutation relation:

\displaystyle\{Q^{a},\mathbb{F}\}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{O}^{a},

(3.40)

which in turn implies
[\bar{Q}_{c},\mathbb{O}^{b}]=2\delta^{b}_{c}\partial_{t}\mathbb{F}
. The Jacobi identity for this new field immediately shows that
\{\bar{Q}_{a},\mathbb{F}\}
can be consistently chosen to vanish. Furthermore,
\mathbb{F}
has the correct quantum numbers to be the superprimary of the chiral multiplet
\bar{\mathcal{B}}^{\frac{1}{2}}_{\frac{3}{2},0,0}
(see Appendix
B.1
) with the structure

\displaystyle\bar{\mathcal{B}}^{\frac{1}{2}}_{\frac{3}{2},0,0}:

[\frac{1}{2},\frac{3}{2},0,0]
[1,2,1,0]
[\frac{3}{2},\frac{5}{2},0,1]
[2,3,0,0]

(3.41)

where this supermultiplet is realized in terms of the fundamental fields of ABJM theory
[
1
]
.
An analogous action of the supercharges can be derived for the conjugated operators leading to the barred version of the commutation relations in Table
3.2
.

Summary of supersymmetry transformations

\{Q^{a},\mathbb{F}\}=\mathbb{O}^{a}

\{\bar{Q}_{a},\mathbb{F}\}=0

[Q^{a},\mathbb{O}^{b}]=\epsilon^{abc}\mathbb{\Lambda}_{c}

[\bar{Q}_{a},\mathbb{O}^{b}]=2\delta^{b}_{a}\partial_{t}\mathbb{F}

\{Q^{a},\mathbb{\Lambda}_{b}\}=-2\delta^{a}_{b}\mathbb{D}

\{\bar{Q}_{a},\mathbb{\Lambda}_{b}\}=-2\epsilon_{abc}\partial_{t}\mathbb{O}^{c}

[Q^{a},\mathbb{D}]=0

[\bar{Q}_{c},\mathbb{D}]=-\partial_{t}\mathbb{\Lambda}_{c}

Table 3.2
:
Summary of the supersymmetry transformations of the displacement supermultiplet of a 1/2-BPS line-defect in
\mathcal{N}=6
supersymmetric theories in
d=3
.

The displacement supermultiplet (
3.41
) appearing in the dCFT
1
living along the Wilson line
should match the string transverse excitations via AdS/CFT.
The expansion of the Green-Schwarz string action around the minimal surface solution results in a multiplet of fluctuations transverse to the string
[
166
,
167
]
whose components match precisely the quantum numbers of the operators in (
3.41
), once the two standard relations between AdS
2
masses and the corresponding CFT
1
operator dimensions are taken into account,
m^{2}=\Delta(\Delta-1)
for scalars and
\Delta=\frac{1}{2}+|m|
for spinors
[
171
,
172
]
. In particular, in the scalar fluctuation sector, one finds one massive (with
m^{2}=2
) complex scalar field
X
in AdS
4
, corresponding to the
\Delta=2
displacement operator
\mathbb{D}
, and three massless
complex
scalar fluctuations
w^{a},~a=1,2,3
in
\mathbb{C}\textup{{P}}^{3}
, corresponding to the
\Delta=1
operators
\mathbb{O}^{a},~a=1,2,3
. In the fermionic sector, there are two massless fermions which should correspond to the
\Delta=\frac{1}{2}
fermionic superprimary
\mathbb{F}
of the multiplet and its conjugate, as well as six massive fermions (of which three with mass
m_{F}=1
and three with
m_{F}=-1
) corresponding to the
\Delta=\frac{3}{2}
fermionic operator
\mathbb{\Lambda}_{a}
and its conjugate.

3.2.3
Chiral Correlators

The representation theory analysis of the supergroup SU
(1,1|3)
reviewed in the previous section shows that the displacement operator belongs to a chiral multiplet with R-charge
j_{0}=\frac{3}{2}
and dimension
\Delta=\frac{1}{2}
(the conjugate operator belongs to an antichiral multiplet with opposite R-charge). For this section, we will consider a chiral multiplet with arbitrary R-charge
j_{0}
and dimension
\Delta=\frac{j_{0}}{3}
. A chiral superfield
\Phi_{j_{0}}
should respect the chirality condition

\bar{\mathsf{D}}_{a}\Phi_{j_{0}}=0\,,\qquad

(3.42)

for every value of
a
.
By defining the chiral coordinate
y=t+\theta_{a}\bar{\theta}^{a}
, such that
\bar{\mathsf{D}}_{a}y=0
, one simply has the component expansion

\displaystyle\Phi_{j_{0}}(y,\theta)=\phi(y)+\theta_{a}\psi^{a}(y)-\frac{1}{2}\theta_{a}\theta_{b}\,\epsilon^{abc}\,\eta_{c}(y)+\frac{1}{3}\theta_{a}\theta_{b}\theta_{c}\,\epsilon^{abc}\xi(y)\,,

(3.43)

where the numerical coefficients of each component are fixed by the consistency between the action of the supercharges on the superfield and the commutation relations in Table
3.2
.

Similarly, the antichiral field is expanded as

\displaystyle\bar{\Phi}_{j_{0}}(y,\theta)=\bar{\phi}(y)+\bar{\theta}^{a}\bar{\psi}_{a}(y)+\frac{1}{2}\bar{\theta}^{a}\bar{\theta}^{b}\,\epsilon_{abc}\,\bar{\eta}^{c}(y)-\frac{1}{3}\bar{\theta}^{a}\bar{\theta}^{b}\bar{\theta}^{c}\,\epsilon_{abc}\,\bar{\xi}(y)\,,

(3.44)

where the coefficients in the expansion are determined by the Ward identities relating the two-point functions of different super descendants.
5
5

5

Or equivalently, one can act explicitly on the superfield with the supercharges since the commutations with the component fields were computed using the same Ward identities. Writing the superfield as

\Phi(y)=\mathbb{F}+c_{1}\theta^{a}\mathbb{O}_{a}+\frac{1}{2}c_{2}\theta^{a}\theta^{b}\epsilon_{abc}\Lambda^{c}+\frac{1}{3}c_{3}\theta^{3}\mathbb{D},

(3.45)

one obtains the commutation relations

\displaystyle\{Q_{a},\mathbb{F}\}=c_{1}\mathbb{O}_{a}

(3.46)

\displaystyle[Q_{a},\mathbb{O}_{b}]=-\frac{c_{2}}{c_{1}}\epsilon_{abc}\Lambda^{c}

(3.47)

\displaystyle\{Q_{a},\Lambda^{c}\}=2\frac{c_{3}}{c_{2}}\delta_{a}^{c}\mathbb{D}

(3.48)

\displaystyle[Q_{a},\mathbb{D}]=0,

(3.49)

which when compared to (
3.43
) give the correct factors.
Using the commutation relations from above, we can relate the constants from the two-point functions

\langle\mathbb{F}(0)\bar{\mathbb{F}}(t)\rangle=\frac{c_{\mathbb{F}}}{t}\quad\quad\langle\mathbb{O}_{a}(0)\bar{\mathbb{O}}^{b}(t)\rangle=\frac{\delta_{a}^{b}c_{\mathbb{O}}}{t^{2}}\quad\quad\langle\Lambda(0)^{a}\bar{\Lambda}_{b}(t)\rangle=\frac{\delta^{a}_{b}c_{\Lambda}}{t^{3}}\quad\quad\langle\mathbb{D}(0)\bar{\mathbb{D}}(t)\rangle=\frac{c_{\mathbb{D}}}{t^{4}}\quad\quad

by using the vanishing of some of the two-point correlators such as
\langle\mathbb{F}\bar{\mathbb{O}}_{a}\rangle
. Acting on it with Q and using the previous commutation relations gives:

\displaystyle\quad\quad Q_{a}\langle\mathbb{F}\bar{\mathbb{O}}^{b}\rangle=0

(3.50)

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\langle\{Q_{a},\mathbb{F}\}\bar{\mathbb{O}}^{b}\rangle+\langle\mathbb{F}[Q_{a},\bar{\mathbb{O}}^{b}]\rangle=0

(3.51)

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\langle\mathbb{O}_{a}\bar{\mathbb{O}}^{b}\rangle+2\delta_{a}^{b}\langle\mathbb{F}\partial_{t}\bar{\mathbb{F}}\rangle=0

(3.52)

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\frac{\delta_{a}^{b}c_{\mathbb{O}}}{\tau^{2}}+2\delta_{a}^{b}\partial_{t}\frac{c_{\mathbb{F}}}{\tau}=0

(3.53)

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow c_{\mathbb{O}}=+2c_{\mathbb{F}}.

(3.54)

Doing the same process on
\langle\mathbb{O}_{a}\bar{\Lambda}_{b}\rangle\quad\langle\Lambda^{b}\bar{\mathbb{D}}\rangle
gives the equalities:

\displaystyle c_{\Lambda}=4c_{\mathbb{O}}

(3.55)

\displaystyle 2c_{\mathbb{D}}=3c_{\Lambda}.

(3.56)

The two-point function of a chiral and antichiral superfield must be expressed in terms of the chiral distance (
\bar{\mathsf{D}}_{i}\braket{i\bar{j}}=\mathsf{D}_{j}\braket{i\bar{j}}=(Q_{i}+Q_{j})\,\braket{i\bar{j}}=(\bar{Q}_{i}+\bar{Q}_{j})\,\braket{i\bar{j}}=0
)

\displaystyle\braket{i\bar{j}}=y_{i}-y_{j}-2{\theta_{a}}_{i}\bar{\theta^{a}}_{j}\,,

(3.57)

and can be written as

\displaystyle\braket{\Phi_{j_{0}}(y_{1},\theta_{1})\bar{\Phi}_{-j_{0}}(y_{2},\bar{\theta}_{2})}=\frac{c_{\Phi_{j_{0}}}}{\braket{1\bar{2}}^{\frac{2j_{0}}{3}}}\,.

(3.58)

For the case of the displacement supermultiplet, the normalization factor
C_{\Phi}
has an important physical interpretation. As shown in subsection
3.2.2
, most of the components in the displacement multiplet are obtained by the action of a broken symmetry generator. Such generators have a natural normalization in the bulk theory. Therefore the Ward identities (
3.35
) fix the physical normalization of the displacement operator, making its two-point function an important piece of dCFT data. There is concrete evidence that in the presence of a superconformal defect, this coefficient is related to the one-point function of the stress tensor operator
[
173
,
174
,
175
]
. Moreover, for the case of the Wilson line, this coefficient is particularly important as it computes the energy emitted by an accelerating heavy probe in a conformal field theory
[
59
,
176
]
, often called Bremsstrahlung function. The relation with the two-point function of the displacement operator in the context of superconformal theories in three and four dimensions has allowed for the exact computation of this quantity in a variety of examples
[
59
,
176
,
173
,
166
,
167
,
177
,
178
,
179
,
163
,
180
,
181
,
174
,
182
,
183
]
. In the present context we have

\displaystyle C_{\Phi}(\lambda)=2B_{1/2}(\lambda)\,,

(3.59)

where
B_{1/2}(\lambda)
is the Bremsstrahlung function associated to the 1/2-BPS Wilson line in ABJM theory (see
[
177
,
163
]
).
From the Graßmann expansion of the two-point function (
3.58
), we extract the following two-point functions

\displaystyle\langle\mathbb{F}(t_{1})\bar{\mathbb{F}}(t_{2})\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{C_{\Phi}}{t_{12}}

\displaystyle\langle\mathbb{O}^{a}(t_{1})\bar{\mathbb{O}}_{b}(t_{2})\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{2\,C_{\Phi}\,\delta^{a}_{b}}{t_{12}^{2}}

(3.60)

\displaystyle\langle\mathbb{\Lambda}_{a}(t_{1})\bar{\mathbb{\Lambda}}^{b}(t_{2})\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{8\,C_{\Phi}\,\delta_{a}^{b}}{t_{12}^{3}}

\displaystyle\langle\mathbb{D}(t_{1})\bar{\mathbb{D}}(t_{2})\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{12\,C_{\Phi}}{t_{12}^{4}}

(3.61)

which is consistent with the component expansion above.

The four-point function of 1/2-BPS (chiral) operators will, in general, be a function of four spacetime coordinates and twelve Graßmanian coordinates subject to the constraints from three bosonic generators
(P,K,D)
and twelve Graßmannian generators (
Q^{a},\bar{Q}_{a},S^{a},\bar{S}_{a}
) of
su(1,1|3)
. By simple counting, one expects the correlator to depend on a single super cross-ratio. For the two different four-point function configurations, we have

\displaystyle\!\!\braket{\Phi_{j_{0}}(y_{1},\theta_{1})\bar{\Phi}_{-j_{0}}(y_{2},\bar{\theta}_{2})\Phi_{j_{0}}(y_{3},\theta_{3})\bar{\Phi}_{-j_{0}}(y_{4},\bar{\theta}_{4})}

\displaystyle=\frac{C^{2}_{\Phi_{j_{0}}}}{\braket{1\bar{2}}^{\frac{2j_{0}}{3}}\braket{3\bar{4}}^{\frac{2j_{0}}{3}}}\,f(\mathcal{Z})\,,

(3.62)

\displaystyle\!\!\braket{\Phi_{j_{0}}(y_{1},\theta_{1})\bar{\Phi}_{-j_{0}}(y_{2},\bar{\theta}_{2})\bar{\Phi}_{-j_{0}}(y_{3},\bar{\theta}_{3})\Phi_{j_{0}}(y_{4},\theta_{4})}

\displaystyle=-\,\frac{C^{2}_{\Phi_{j_{0}}}}{\braket{1\bar{2}}^{\frac{2j_{0}}{3}}\braket{4\bar{3}}^{\frac{2j_{0}}{3}}}\,h(\mathcal{X})\,,

(3.63)

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}

\displaystyle=\frac{\braket{1\bar{2}}\braket{3\bar{4}}}{\braket{1\bar{4}}\braket{3{\bar{2}}}}

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\braket{1\bar{2}}\braket{4\bar{3}}}{\braket{1\bar{3}}\braket{2\bar{4}}}

(3.64)

are the two superconformal cross-ratios corresponding to the two different correlators. These two invariants are built out of the chiral distance (
3.57
). In the general case, one may have a set of additional superconformal invariants which are nilpotent due to their Graßmann nature. For long-multiplet four-point functions, using such nilpotent invariants guarantees a finite truncation in the superspace expansion (see
[
87
]
).
However, none of these invariants is compatible with the chirality condition (
3.42
). The absence of nilpotent invariants is expected for correlators of 1/2-BPS operators and, in general, for four-point functions containing two chiral and two anti-chiral operators
[
184
]
. It is important to notice that both correlators (
3.119
) and (
3.120
) are ordered such that
t_{1}<t_{2}<t_{3}<t_{4}
. These two correlators would be related by crossing in higher dimensions, but this is not the case in one dimension. To make this more concrete, let us consider the bosonic part of the cross-ratios (
3.64
)

\displaystyle z

\displaystyle=\frac{t_{12}t_{34}}{t_{14}t_{32}}

\displaystyle\chi

\displaystyle=\frac{t_{12}t_{34}}{t_{13}t_{24}}

(3.65)

where
z
is the bosonic part of
\mathcal{Z}
and
\chi
is the bosonic part of
\mathcal{X}
. With our ordering we have
z<0
and
0<\chi<1
. The transformation

\displaystyle z=\frac{\chi}{\chi-1}\,

(3.66)

relates the two cross-ratios.
The absence of nilpotent invariants implies that the superprimary correlators

\displaystyle\!\!\braket{\phi(t_{1})\bar{\phi}(t_{2})\phi(t_{3})\bar{\phi}(t_{4})}

\displaystyle=\braket{\phi(t_{1})\bar{\phi}(t_{2})}\braket{\phi(t_{3})\bar{\phi}(t_{4})}\,f(z)\,,

(3.67)

\displaystyle\!\!\braket{\phi(t_{1})\bar{\phi}(t_{2})\bar{\phi}(t_{3})\phi(t_{4})}

\displaystyle=\braket{\phi(t_{1})\bar{\phi}(t_{2})}\braket{\bar{\phi}(t_{3})\phi(t_{4})}\,h(\chi)\,,

(3.68)

fully determine the four-point functions of the whole superconformal multiplet, i.e. the correlators of superconformal descendants can be obtained by the action of differential operators on
f(z)
. Of course, one is free to express the function
f(z)
in terms of the cross-ratio
\chi
by considering
f(\frac{\chi}{\chi-1})
. One can also take the analytic continuation of
f(z)
for
0<z<1
, which would naively establish a relation between (
3.67
) and (
3.68
).
6
6

6

f(z)
has branch cut singularities at coincident points, i.e.
z=0,1,\infty
.
Nevertheless, this is not the case in one dimension, and
h(\chi)
is not the analytic continuation of
f(z)
. Still, we will see in subsection
3.2.4
that a relationship between these two functions exists through their
s
-channel block expansion.

Just as in the case of the two-point function, the correlators of the super descendants can be obtained by expanding the Graßmann coordinates of the four-point correlator in superspace.

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\braket{\mathbb{F}(t_{1})\bar{\mathbb{F}}(t_{2})\mathbb{F}(t_{3})\bar{\mathbb{F}}(t_{4})}=\frac{C_{\Phi}^{2}}{t_{12}t_{34}}f(z)

(3.69)

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\braket{\mathbb{O}^{a_{1}}(t_{1})\bar{\mathbb{O}}_{a_{2}}(t_{2})\mathbb{O}^{a_{3}}(t_{3})\bar{\mathbb{O}}_{a_{4}}(t_{4})}=\frac{4C_{\Phi}^{2}}{t_{12}^{2}t_{34}^{2}}\,\Big[\delta^{a_{1}}_{a_{2}}\delta^{a_{3}}_{a_{4}}\,\big(\,f(z)-zf^{\prime}(z)+z^{2}f^{\prime\prime}(z)\big)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad-\delta^{a_{1}}_{a_{4}}\delta^{a_{3}}_{a_{2}}\,\big(z^{2}f^{\prime}(z)+z^{3}f^{\prime\prime}(z)\big)\,\Big]

(3.70)

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\braket{\mathbb{D}(t_{1})\bar{\mathbb{D}}(t_{2})\mathbb{D}(t_{3})\bar{\mathbb{D}}(t_{4})}=\frac{(12C_{\Phi})^{2}}{t_{12}^{4}t_{34}^{4}}\,\frac{1}{36}\Big[36f(z)-36(z^{4}+z)f^{\prime}(z)+18z^{2}(-14z^{3}+3z^{2}+1)f^{\prime\prime}(z)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad-6z^{3}\left(55z^{3}-39z^{2}+3z+1\right)f^{(3)}(z)-3z^{4}\left(46z^{3}-63z^{2}+18z-1\right)f^{(4)}(z)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad-3(z-1)^{2}z^{5}(7z-1)f^{(5)}(z)-(z-1)^{3}z^{6}f^{(6)}(z)\,\Big]\,

(3.71)

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\braket{\mathbb{D}(t_{1})\bar{\mathbb{D}}(t_{2})\mathbb{O}^{a_{3}}(t_{3})\bar{\mathbb{O}}_{a_{4}}(t_{4})}=\frac{24C_{\Phi}^{2}}{t_{12}^{4}t_{34}^{2}}\delta^{a_{3}}_{a_{4}}\frac{1}{6}\Big[\,(\!1\!-z)\,z^{4}f^{(4)}\!(z)-(3z+\!1\!)\,z^{3}f^{(3)}\!(z)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+3z^{2}\,f^{\prime\prime}(z)-6zf^{\prime}(z)+6f(z)\,\Big]\,,

(3.72)

where for each correlator, we factorised the (squared) two-point function contribution (
3.60
) arising from the double OPE.
In the next section, we will use analytic bootstrap techniques to evaluate the function
f(z)
perturbatively to third-order at strong coupling.
In section
3.3
, the superspace analysis is used to find
f(z)
from the correlators of superconformal descendants evaluated directly at strong coupling using Witten diagrammatics. The consistency of these various correlators with the superspace analysis provides a non-trivial check of the relations above.

3.2.4
Symmetry and Superblocks

Selection rules for the 1/2-BPS operators

Even though we only consider half-BPS multiplets as external operators, more general multiplets can be exchanged when an OPE is applied to the correlator.
In our case, there are two qualitatively different OPE channels to consider, depending on whether we take the chiral-antichiral OPE
\Phi\times\bar{\Phi}
or the chiral-chiral OPE
\Phi\times\Phi
. Each has selection rules for superconformal representations appearing in these two channels and corresponding superconformal blocks. We start from the chiral-antichiral channel. In
[
181
]
, all the selection rules for the 1/6-BPS defect theory were derived, and it was found that only the identity and long multiplets can appear in the chiral-antichiral OPE for
su(1,1|1)
, a.k.a.
\mathcal{N}=2
supersymmetry. For the
\mathcal{N}=6
case of interest here, every pair
\{Q^{a},\bar{Q}_{a}\}
at fixed
a
generates a
su(1,1|1)
subgroup of
su(1,1|3)
, and a chiral multiplet of
su(1,1|3)
is also a chiral multiplet of all the three
su(1,1|1)
subgroups. This implies that the operators appearing in the
\Phi\times\bar{\Phi}
OPE must belong to long multiplets of all the
su(1,1|1)
subgroups, i.e. they must not be annihilated by any supercharge. Furthermore, three-point functions with one chiral, one antichiral and one long multiplet are non-vanishing only when the superprimary R-charges sum to zero, namely a long multiplet enters only when its superprimary can be exchanged. We conclude that

\displaystyle\bar{\mathcal{B}}_{j_{0}}\times\mathcal{B}_{-j_{0}}\sim\mathcal{I}+\mathcal{A}^{\Delta}_{0,0,0}\,,

(3.73)

where
\mathcal{I}
is the identity,
\mathcal{B}_{-j_{0}}
is the chiral multiplet, and
\mathcal{A}^{\Delta}_{0,0,0}
is an uncharged long multiplet.
7
7

7

We use the notation of Appendix
B.1
for the
su(1,1|3)
supermultiplets.

In this case, the unitarity bound (
B.24
) is
\Delta\geq 0
, and every positive dimension is allowed for long operators.

For the chiral-chiral channel, the situation is richer. We will borrow an argument from
[
185
]
to extract the selection rules. Consider the chiral superprimary operator
\phi
. chirality gives

\displaystyle[\bar{Q}_{a},\phi(t)]

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle[\bar{S}_{a},\phi(t)]

\displaystyle=0

(3.74)

for any
a
and any
t
. The first condition is simply the definition of chirality. In contrast, the second one comes from the requirement that
\phi
is a superprimary at the origin together with the commutation relation
[P,\bar{S}_{a}]=-\bar{Q}_{a}
. These two conditions imply that any operator
\mathcal{O}
appearing in the
\phi\times\phi
OPE must respect

\displaystyle[\bar{Q}_{a},\mathcal{O}(t)]

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle[\bar{S}_{a},\mathcal{O}(t)]

\displaystyle=0

(3.75)

for any
a
. It is then immediate to realize that the only superprimary operator which is allowed to appear is a chiral operator of dimension
\Delta_{\text{exc}}=\frac{2j_{0}}{3}
. All other multiplets will contribute with a single super descendant (and all its conformal descendants) generated by the repeated action of
\bar{Q}_{a}
. Concretely, a long multiplet will contribute with the operator generated by
\bar{Q}^{3}O
(here
\bar{Q}^{3}=\epsilon^{abc}\bar{Q}_{a}\bar{Q}_{b}\bar{Q}_{c})
, where
O
is the superprimary. The complete analysis yields

\displaystyle\bar{\mathcal{B}}_{j_{0}}\times\bar{\mathcal{B}}_{j_{0}}\sim\bar{\mathcal{B}}_{2j_{0}}+\bar{\mathcal{B}}_{2j_{0}+\frac{1}{2},1}+\bar{\mathcal{B}}_{2j_{0}+1,0,1}+\mathcal{A}^{\Delta}_{2j_{0}+\frac{3}{2},0,0}.

(3.76)

In particular, in the OPE of the superprimary operator
\phi
, every supermultiplet contributes with a single conformal family, whose conformal primary has quantum numbers
[\Delta,2j_{0},0,0]
. The dimension is fixed in terms of
j_{0}
for short multiples. In Table
3.3
, we summarize the schematic form of the only relevant super descendant operators and make explicit their conformal dimension, which can be easily obtained from the associated superprimary. The dimension of the long multiplet is unfixed, but it should respect the unitarity bound (
B.24
). For this specific case, we find that the dimension of the superprimary must be
\Delta>\frac{2j_{0}}{3}+\frac{1}{2}
, while the relevant super descendant, obtained by acting with all the
\bar{Q}
’s, must have dimension
8
8

8

Here the equality is excluded because in that case, the long multiplet decomposes as in Table
B.1
and the relevant super descendant falls back into the
\bar{\mathcal{B}}_{2j_{0}+1,0,1}
multiplet.

\Delta_{\text{exc}}^{\text{long}}>\frac{2j_{0}}{3}+2\,.

(3.77)

This bound will be very important in the following, where we will focus on the
j_{0}=\frac{3}{2}
case. In summary, the
\phi\times\phi
spectrum admits three protected conformal primaries of dimensions
\frac{2j_{0}}{3}
,
\frac{2j_{0}}{3}+1
and
\frac{2j_{0}}{3}+2
and infinitely many operators with unprotected dimensions strictly higher than the protected ones.

Multiplet

Exc.

\Delta_{\text{exc}}

\bar{\mathcal{B}}_{2j_{0}}

O

\frac{2j_{0}}{3}

\bar{\mathcal{B}}_{2j_{0}+\frac{1}{2},1}

\bar{Q}O

\frac{2j_{0}}{3}+1

\bar{\mathcal{B}}_{2j_{0}+1,0,1}

\bar{Q}^{2}O

\frac{2j_{0}}{3}+2

\mathcal{A}^{\Delta}_{2j_{0}+\frac{3}{2},0,0}

\bar{Q}^{3}O

\Delta+\frac{3}{2}

Table 3.3
:
The multiplets contributing to the chiral-chiral OPE with a schematic representation of the only superconformal descendant (but conformal primary) contributing to the OPE (in this Table
O
indicates the superprimary of each multiplet).

Superblocks

We now derive the superconformal blocks associated with the two channels. In the chiral-chiral channel, each supermultiplet only contributes with a single conformal family. Therefore one only needs to select the
sl(2)
conformal blocks with the appropriate dimensions
[
186
]
.

\displaystyle g_{h}(\chi)=\chi^{h}\,_{2}F_{1}(h,h;2h;\chi).

(3.78)

Each conformal primary listed in Table
3.3
contributes with an
sl(2)
block with
h=\Delta_{\text{exc}}
. Let us consider the specific correlator of interest here
9
9

9

The correlator (
3.67
) does not admit an expansion in this channel since one cannot take point
\phi(t_{1})
close to
\phi(t_{3})
.

\displaystyle\braket{\phi(t_{1})\bar{\phi}(t_{2})\bar{\phi}(t_{3})\phi(t_{4})}=\frac{C_{\Phi}^{2}}{t_{14}^{\frac{2j_{0}}{3}}t_{23}^{\frac{2j_{0}}{3}}}\hat{h}(\chi)

(3.79)

where, comparing with (
3.68
) we defined
\hat{h}(\chi)=(\frac{1-\chi}{\chi})^{\frac{2j_{0}}{3}}h(\chi)
. The
\phi\times\phi
channel corresponds to the
\chi\to 1
limit and we have

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\hat{h}(\chi)=\mathsf{c}_{\frac{2j_{0}}{3}}g_{\frac{2j_{0}}{3}}(1-\chi)+\mathsf{c}_{\frac{2j_{0}}{3}+1}g_{\frac{2j_{0}}{3}+1}(1-\chi)+\mathsf{c}_{\frac{2j_{0}}{3}+2}g_{\frac{2j_{0}}{3}+2}(1-\chi)+\sum_{\Delta}\mathsf{c}_{\Delta}g_{\Delta}(1-\chi)

(3.80)

where the sum runs over unprotected operators with dimension
\Delta>\frac{2j_{0}}{3}+2
, and
\mathsf{c}_{\Delta}
’s are the squared moduli of the OPE coefficients.
10
10

10

We use a different font to distinguish the coefficients from the chiral-chiral and the chiral-antichiral channel.

In the chiral-antichiral channel, long multiplets with quantum numbers
[\Delta,0,0,0]
contribute with all the allowed super descendants, and we can decompose the four-point correlation function in terms of superconformal blocks. The latter are always expressed as a linear combination of ordinary
sl(2)
blocks with shifted dimensions. Conformal blocks can be seen as eigenfunctions of the Casimir differential operator
[
186
]
. Analogously, superconformal blocks can be computed by considering the differential equation generated by the action of the superconformal Casimir
[
184
]
. We start by the
s
-channel OPE expansion of the superspace four-point function (
3.119
), insert a resolution of the identity between points
t_{2}
and
t_{3}
, and act on each term of the sum with the quadratic Casimir
{\bf C}_{1}+{\bf C}_{2}
, where
{\bf C}_{i}
is the differential operator (
3.20
) acting on the supercoordinates
(t_{i},\theta_{ai},\bar{\theta}^{a}_{i})
. This leads to the block expansion

\displaystyle f(z)=1+\sum_{\Delta}\,c_{\Delta}\,G_{\Delta}(z)

(3.81)

where the sum runs over the dimensions
\Delta>0
of the superprimary operators exchanged in the chiral-antichiral channel, and each conformal block satisfies the differential equation

\displaystyle\big(-z^{2}(z-1)\partial_{z}^{2}-z(z-3)\partial_{z}\big)\,G_{\Delta}(z)=\Delta(\Delta+2)\,G_{\Delta}(z)\,,

(3.82)

where
\Delta(\Delta+2)
is the Casimir eigenvalue (
3.21
) with zero R-charges. The equation above is solved by the hypergeometric function
11
11

11

The appearance of a minus sign in (
3.81
) is due to our use of
z
, which takes real negative values, rather than the more standard
\chi
, see (
3.65
).

\displaystyle G_{\Delta}(z)=(-z)^{\Delta}{}_{2}F_{1}(\Delta,\Delta,2\Delta+3;z)\,.

(3.83)

G_{\Delta}(z)
can be decomposed in terms of a finite sum of
sl(2)
blocks (
3.78
).
12
12

12

Here we use
\hat{g}(z)=g(\frac{z}{z-1})=(-z)^{\Delta}\,_{2}F_{1}(\Delta,\Delta;2\Delta;z)
, which implements the change of variable from
\chi
to
z
.

\displaystyle G_{\Delta}(z)

\displaystyle=\hat{g}_{\Delta}(z)+\frac{3\Delta}{2(2\Delta+3)}\hat{g}_{\Delta+1}(z)+\frac{3\Delta^{2}(\Delta+1)}{4(2\Delta+3)(2\Delta+1)(\Delta+2)}\hat{g}_{\Delta+2}(z)

\displaystyle+\frac{\Delta^{2}(\Delta+1)}{8(2\Delta+3)^{2}(2\Delta+5)}\hat{g}_{\Delta+3}(z)

(3.84)

To find a connection between the two correlators (
3.67
) and (
3.68
), it is important to consider the
s
-channel expansion of (
3.68
)

\displaystyle h(\chi)

\displaystyle=1+\sum_{\Delta}\,\tilde{c}_{\Delta}\,\tilde{G}_{\Delta}(\chi)

(3.85)

where

\displaystyle\tilde{G}_{\Delta}(\chi)=\chi^{\Delta}{}_{2}F_{1}(\Delta,\Delta,2\Delta+3;\chi)\,.

(3.86)

The similarity between this block expansion and (
3.81
) is apparent, especially when considering the relation between
\tilde{c}_{\Delta}
and
c_{\Delta}
. Denoting by
\mathcal{O}_{\Delta}
the exchanged operator of dimension
\Delta
, the expressions of
c_{\Delta}
and
\tilde{c}_{\Delta}
are given by

\displaystyle c_{\Delta}

\displaystyle=f_{\phi\bar{\phi}\mathcal{O}_{\Delta}}f_{\phi\bar{\phi}\mathcal{O}_{\Delta}}

\displaystyle\tilde{c}_{\Delta}=f_{\phi\bar{\phi}\mathcal{O}_{\Delta}}f_{\bar{\phi}\phi\mathcal{O}_{\Delta}}

(3.87)

where
f_{\phi\bar{\phi}\mathcal{O}_{\Delta}}
is the three-point coefficient determining
\braket{\phi\bar{\phi}\mathcal{O}_{\Delta}}
. In one-dimensional CFT, the OPE coefficients depend on the signature of the permutation. In particular, despite there being no continuous group of rotation, there is a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
parity transformation
t\to-t
.
13
13

13

This symmetry was called S-parity in
[
92
]
.
operators are charged under this symmetry, and one has

\displaystyle\braket{\mathcal{O}_{1}(t_{1})\mathcal{O}_{2}(t_{2})\mathcal{O}_{3}(t_{3})}=(-1)^{T_{1}+T_{2}+T_{3}}\braket{\mathcal{O}_{3}(-t_{3})\mathcal{O}_{2}(-t_{2})\mathcal{O}_{1}(-t_{1})}

(3.88)

where
T_{1}
,
T_{2}
and
T_{3}
are the charges of the operators under parity. For our example, if
\phi
is a bosonic operator, we have

\displaystyle f_{\phi\bar{\phi}\mathcal{O}_{\Delta}}=(-1)^{T_{\mathcal{O}}}f_{\bar{\phi}\phi\mathcal{O}_{\Delta}},

(3.89)

whereas for a fermionic
\phi

\displaystyle f_{\phi\bar{\phi}\mathcal{O}_{\Delta}}=(-1)^{1+T_{\mathcal{O}}}f_{\bar{\phi}\phi\mathcal{O}_{\Delta}}.

(3.90)

Therefore the two coefficients
c_{\Delta}
and
\tilde{c}_{\Delta}
, for a fermionic
\phi
are related by

\displaystyle c_{\Delta}=(-1)^{T_{\mathcal{O}}+1}\tilde{c}_{\Delta}.

(3.91)

As an example, which will be useful in the following, operators of the schematic form
\mathcal{O}_{n}=\phi\partial_{t}^{n}\bar{\phi}
have charge
T_{\mathcal{O}}=n
. In section
3.5
, these relations will allow us to establish a precise connection between the correlators (
3.67
) and (
3.68
) in a perturbative expansion around the free theory result.

3.3
Diagrammatic Results

In this section, we use the superconformal symmetry to relate the four-point correlation function of the massless bosonic excitations
\{w^{a}{\bar{w}}_{a}\}
to that of the fundamental operator
\{\mathbb{F},\bar{\mathbb{F}}\}
.
The Type IIA background
\text{AdS}_{4}\times\mathbb{C}\textup{{P}}^{3}
is defined by

\displaystyle ds^{2}=R^{2}\left(ds^{2}_{\textrm{AdS}_{4}}+4ds^{2}_{\mathbb{C}\textup{{P}}^{3}}\right)\,,\qquad e^{\phi}=\frac{2R}{k}\,,\qquad R^{2}\equiv{\widetilde{R}^{3}\over 4k}=\frac{k^{2}}{4}e^{2\phi}\,,

(3.92)

\displaystyle F_{2}=2\,k\,J_{\mathbb{C}\textup{{P}}^{3}}\,,\qquad F_{4}=\frac{3}{8}R^{3}\,\vol(\text{AdS}_{4})\,,

(3.93)

where
R
is the
\text{AdS}_{4}
radius,
\phi
the dilaton,
k
results from the compactification of the original M-theory on an
\text{AdS}_{4}\times\textup{{S}}^{7}/Z_{k}
background.
14
14

14

k
coincides in the dual theory with the Chern-Simons level number
[
150
]
.
Above,
F_{2}
,
F_{4}
are the two-form and four-form field strengths with
J_{\mathbb{C}\textup{{P}}^{3}}
the Kähler form on
\mathbb{C}\textup{{P}}^{3}
. As they only play a role in the fermionic part of the Lagrangian we only report them here for completeness.
15
15

15

At first order in perturbation, it is sufficient to consider the bosonic part of the action to compute bosonic correlators.

Using the Poincaré patch for the AdS
4
metric

\displaystyle ds^{2}_{\text{AdS}_{4}}=\frac{dz^{2}+dx^{r}dx^{r}}{z^{2}}\,,

(3.94)

where
x^{r}=(x^{0},x^{1},x^{2})
parametrize the Euclidean three-dimensional boundary of
\text{AdS}_{4}
and
z
is the radial coordinate,
the bosonic part of the superstring action in AdS
{}_{4}\times\,\mathbb{CP}^{3}
reads

S_{B}=\frac{1}{2}T\int d^{2}\sigma\sqrt{h}\,h^{\mu\nu}\Big[\frac{1}{z^{2}}\left(\partial_{\mu}x^{r}\partial_{\nu}x^{r}+\partial_{\mu}z\partial_{\nu}z\right)+4\,G_{MN}^{\mathbb{C}\textup{{P}}^{3}}\,{\partial_{\mu}Y^{M}\partial_{\nu}Y^{N}}\Big]\ .

(3.95)

Here,
\sigma^{\mu}=(t,s)
are Euclidean world-sheet coordinates, and
T
is the effective string tension. In its original “dictionary” proposal
[
150
]
it is related to the effective ’t Hooft
coupling
\lambda
of the dual
\mathcal{N}=6
superconformal Chern-Simons theory (realized in the limit of
k
and
N
large with their ratio fixed) by equation
1.34
which we remind here:

\displaystyle T=\frac{R^{2}}{2\pi\alpha^{\prime}}=\sqrt{\frac{\lambda}{2}}\,,\qquad\qquad\lambda=\frac{N}{k}\,.

(3.96)

As we are interested in the leading and tree-level orders in perturbation theory, we may disregard the corrections to the effective string tension
T
due to the geometry of the background
[
150
,
187
]
, which start at order
\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lambda}}

[
157
]
.
The classical solution to (
3.95
), which is relevant here, is the minimal surface corresponding to the straight Wilson line at the boundary

\displaystyle z

\displaystyle=s

\displaystyle x^{0}

\displaystyle=t

\displaystyle x^{i=1,2}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle Y^{M}

\displaystyle=0.

(3.97)

This is the embedding of AdS
2
in the AdS
4
background of the solution of
[
188
,
33
]
.
The induced AdS
2
metric is

\displaystyle g_{\mu\nu}d\sigma^{\mu}d\sigma^{\nu}={1\over s^{2}}(dt^{2}+ds^{2})\,.

(3.98)

We will consider correlators of small fluctuations of ‘‘transverse” string coordinates near this minimal surface.
16
16

16

The transverse coordinates are
x^{i},\,i=1,2
and the
\mathbb{C}\textup{{P}}^{3}
coordinates.
The bosonic symmetry of the defect conformal field theory associated with the 1/2-BPS Wilson line is SU
(1,1)\times
SU
(3)\times
U
(1)_{J_{0}}
, and it turns out to be the manifest symmetry of the bosonic string action (
3.95
).
The SU
(1,1)\simeq
SO
(2,1)
symmetry
can be made manifest by fixing a static gauge where
z
and
x^{0}
do not fluctuate. AdS
2
is embedded
[
33
]
into AdS
4
using the following parametrization

\displaystyle ds^{2}_{4}=\frac{(1+\frac{1}{2}|X|^{2})^{2}}{(1-\frac{1}{2}|X|^{2})^{2}}ds^{2}_{2}+\frac{dXd\bar{X}}{(1-\frac{1}{2}|X|^{2})^{2}}\ ,\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ ds^{2}_{2}=\frac{1}{z^{2}}(dx_{0}^{2}+dz^{2})\ .

(3.99)

The complex combination
X=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(x^{1}+ix^{2})
was introduced in terms of the transverse AdS coordinates
x^{i}
.
X
and
\bar{X}
have opposite charge under
U(1)_{J_{0}}
.
Finally, adopting the following parametrization of the
\mathbb{C}\textup{{P}}^{3}
metric

\displaystyle ds^{2}_{\mathbb{C}\textup{{P}}^{3}}=\frac{d\bar{w}_{a}\,dw^{a}}{1+|w|^{2}}-\frac{d\bar{w}_{a}\,w^{a}\,d{w}^{b}\,\bar{w}_{b}}{(1+|w|^{2})^{2}}\,,\qquad|w|^{2}=\bar{w}_{a}\,w^{a}\,,\qquad a,b=1,2,3\,,

(3.100)

the preserved SU
(3)
subgroup of the SU
(4)
global symmetry of
\mathbb{C}\textup{{P}}^{3}
is manifest.

The Nambu-Goto action with fixed static gauge reads then
reads

S_{B}=T\int d^{2}\sigma\sqrt{\det\Big[\frac{(1+1/2|X|^{2})^{2}}{(1-1/2|X|^{2})^{2}}g_{\mu\nu}+2\frac{\partial_{\mu}X\partial_{\nu}\bar{X}}{(1-1/2|X|^{2})^{2}}+4\Big(\,\frac{\partial_{\mu}\bar{w}_{a}\partial_{\nu}w^{a}}{1+\lvert w\rvert^{2}}-\frac{\partial_{\mu}\bar{w}_{a}w^{a}\bar{w}_{b}\partial_{\nu}w^{b}}{(1+\lvert w\rvert^{2})^{2}}\Big)\,\Big]}.

(3.101)

Above,
g_{\mu\nu}={1\over s^{2}}\delta_{\mu\nu}
is the background AdS
2
metric. Along the lines of
[

]
, (

) can be interpreted as the action of a straight fundamental string in AdS

stretched from the boundary towards the AdS

Expanding the action above in powers of

and
w^{a}
one gets

\displaystyle S_{B}\equiv

\displaystyle T\int d^{2}\sigma\sqrt{g}\,L_{B}\,,\qquad L_{B}=\,L_{2}+L_{4X}+L_{2X,2w}+L_{4w}+...\,

(3.102)

\displaystyle L_{2}=

\displaystyle\textstyle g^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}X\partial_{\nu}\bar{X}+2|X|^{2}+g^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}w^{a}\partial_{\nu}{\bar{w}}_{a}\ ,

(3.103)

\displaystyle L_{4X}=

\displaystyle\ \textstyle 2|X|^{4}+|X|^{2}\,(g^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}X\partial_{\nu}\bar{X})-\frac{1}{2}\,(g^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}X\partial_{\nu}X)\,(g^{\rho\kappa}\partial_{\rho}\bar{X}\partial_{\kappa}\bar{X})\,

(3.104)

\displaystyle L_{2X,2w}=

\displaystyle\ \textstyle(g^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}X\partial_{\nu}\bar{X})\,(g^{\rho\kappa}\partial_{\rho}w^{a}\partial_{\kappa}{\bar{w}}_{a})-(g^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}X\partial_{\nu}w^{a})\;(g^{\rho\kappa}\partial_{\rho}\bar{X}\partial_{\kappa}{\bar{w}}_{a})

\displaystyle-(g^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\bar{X}\partial_{\nu}w^{a})\;(g^{\rho\kappa}\partial_{\rho}X\partial_{\kappa}{\bar{w}}_{a})\,

(3.105)

\displaystyle L_{4w}=

\displaystyle\ -\textstyle\frac{1}{2}\textstyle(w^{a}{\bar{w}}_{a})(g^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}w^{b}\partial_{\nu}{\bar{w}}_{b})-\frac{1}{2}\textstyle(w^{a}{\bar{w}}_{b})(g^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}w^{b}\partial_{\nu}{\bar{w}}_{a})+\frac{1}{2}\,(g^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}w^{a}\partial_{\nu}{\bar{w}}_{a})^{2}

\displaystyle\textstyle-\frac{1}{2}(g^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}w^{a}\partial_{\nu}{\bar{w}}_{b})\;(g^{\rho\kappa}\partial_{\rho}{\bar{w}}_{a}\partial_{\kappa}w^{b})-\frac{1}{2}(g^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}w^{a}\partial_{\nu}w^{b})\;(g^{\rho\kappa}\partial_{\rho}{\bar{w}}_{a}\partial_{\kappa}{\bar{w}}_{b})\ .

(3.106)

There is one massive (
X
with

) and three massless (

) complex scalar fields propagating in AdS

that correspond to the bosonic elementary CFT

Below, we will use these vertices of the AdS

{t_{n}}
on the boundary.
As in the AdS

Four-point Function of Massless Fluctuations in
\mathbb{C}\textup{{P}}^{3}

Here we compute the tree-level four-point Witten diagram of the
\mathbb{C}\textup{{P}}^{3}
fluctuations
w,{\bar{w}}
appearing in the AdS
2
action in (
3.102
)-(
3.106

As discussed above, these are AdS/CFT dual to the scalar operator insertions
\mathbb{O}^{a},\bar{\mathbb{O}}_{a},~a=1,2,3
with protected dimension
\Delta=1
. Due to the
SO(2,1)
conformal invariance, the four-point function is expected to take the form

\langle w^{a_{1}}(t_{1})\,{\bar{w}}_{a_{2}}(t_{2})\,w^{a_{3}}(t_{3})\,{\bar{w}}_{a_{4}}(t_{4})\rangle=\frac{\big[C_{w}(\lambda)\big]^{2}}{t_{12}^{2}t_{34}^{2}}G^{a_{1}\,a_{3}}_{a_{2}\,a_{4}}(\chi).

(3.107)

Here,
\chi
is the conformally invariant cross-ratio defined in (
3.65
), and we used for the two-point function

\langle w^{a_{1}}(t_{1}){\bar{w}}_{a_{2}}(t_{2})\rangle=\delta^{a_{1}}_{a_{2}}\frac{C_{w}(\lambda)}{t_{12}^{2}}\,.

(3.108)

The function
G^{a_{1}\,a_{3}}_{a_{2}\,a_{4}}(\chi)
in (

w^{a}
fields, and thus on
C_{w}(\lambda)
. One can of course choose

, so as to realise a direct identification of

with the
\mathbb{O}^{a}
in view of (

).

This is the formal choice of
[

]

[

,

,

]
. see also a related discussion in
[

]
.
This would correspond to an overall rescaling of the fields.
20
20

20

At tree level, this amounts to the rescaling
w^{a}\rightarrow\sqrt{2T}w^{a}
. This can be found from the leading strong coupling value of the Bremsstrahlung function
B_{1/2}(\lambda)=\frac{\sqrt{2\lambda}}{4\pi}\equiv\frac{T}{2\pi}
, and the canonical choice (
1.42
) of the propagator.

By perturbatively evaluating the two-point function (

are protected, as well as reproduce the strong coupling expansion of the corresponding 1/2-BPS Bremsstrahlung function (

).The disconnected part of the four-point function (

\displaystyle\langle w^{a_{1}}(t_{1})\,{\bar{w}}_{a_{2}}(t_{2})\,w^{a_{3}}(t_{3})\,{\bar{w}}_{a_{4}}(t_{4})\rangle_{\text{disconn.}}

\displaystyle=\big[C_{w}(\lambda)\big]^{2}\,\Big[\frac{\delta^{a_{1}}_{a_{2}}\delta^{a_{3}}_{a_{4}}}{t_{12}^{2}t_{34}^{2}}+\frac{\delta^{a_{1}}_{a_{4}}\delta^{a_{3}}_{a_{2}}}{t_{14}^{2}t_{23}^{2}}\Big]

\displaystyle=\frac{\big[C_{w}(\lambda)\big]^{2}}{t_{12}^{2}t_{34}^{2}}\,\Big[\delta^{a_{1}}_{a_{2}}\delta^{a_{3}}_{a_{4}}+\frac{\chi^{2}}{(1-\chi)^{2}}\,\delta^{a_{1}}_{a_{4}}\delta^{a_{3}}_{a_{2}}\,\Big]\,.

(3.109)

This leads to the expression

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!\langle w^{a_{1}}(t_{1})\,{\bar{w}}_{a_{2}}(t_{2})\,w^{a_{3}}(t_{3})\,{\bar{w}}_{a_{4}}(t_{4})\rangle_{\text{conn.}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4\pi T}\,\frac{\big[\mathcal{C}_{\Delta=1}\big]^{2}}{t_{12}^{2}t_{34}^{2}}\,\Big[\delta^{a_{1}}_{a_{2}}\delta^{a_{3}}_{a_{4}}\,G_{1}(\chi)+\delta^{a_{1}}_{a_{4}}\delta^{a_{3}}_{a_{2}}\,G_{2}(\chi)\Big]\,

(3.113)

where, according to (

, and

\displaystyle G_{1}(\chi)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-3+\frac{1}{(\chi-1)}-\frac{\chi^{2}}{(1-\chi)^{2}}\log\chi+\left(1-\frac{4}{\chi}\right)\log(1-\chi)

(3.114)

\displaystyle G_{2}(\chi)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{\chi(3\chi+1)}{(1-\chi)^{2}}+\frac{\chi^{2}(\chi+3)}{(\chi-1)^{3}}\,\log\chi-\log(1-\chi).

(3.115)

Identifying these to the differential equation in (
3.70
) leads to the following system of second-order differential equations
22
22

22

The functions above can be expressed in terms of the invariant
z
using (
3.66
).

\displaystyle f(z)-zf^{\prime}(z)+z^{2}f^{\prime\prime}(z)

\displaystyle=1+\,\frac{1}{4\pi T}\big[-4+z-z^{2}\,\log(-z)+\big(z^{2}-\frac{4}{z}+3\big)\,\log(1-z)\,\big]

(3.116)

\displaystyle-z^{2}f^{\prime}(z)-z^{3}f^{\prime\prime}(z)

\displaystyle=z^{2}\,+\frac{1}{4\pi T}\,\big[\,z-4\,z^{2}-z^{2}(3-4z)\,\log(-z)+(1+3z^{2}-4z^{3})\,\log(1-z)\,\big]\,

(3.117)

whose unique solution agrees with the bootstrap solution (
3.166
)

\displaystyle f(z)=1-z+\frac{1}{4\pi T}\,\big[z-1+z(3-z)\log(-z)-\frac{(1-z)^{3}}{z}\log(1-z)\big]\,.

(3.118)

One can now repeat the analysis for the correlator
\langle w^{a_{1}}(t_{1})\,{\bar{w}}_{a_{2}}(t_{2})\,{\bar{w}}^{a_{3}}(t_{3})\,w_{a_{4}}(t_{4})\rangle
. The result coincides with the one obtained using on the
f(z)
above with the replacement
