\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{B}=\frac{2\pi i}{k}\begin{pmatrix}l_{B}&0\\
0&\hat{l}_{B}\end{pmatrix}

(B.69)

Finally, we can list the transformation properties of the gauge fields

\displaystyle Q^{a}A_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{2\pi i}{k}(\bar{\psi}_{+}\bar{Y}^{a}-\bar{\chi}^{a}_{+}\bar{Z}-\epsilon^{abc}Y_{b}\chi_{c}^{-})

\displaystyle\bar{Q}_{a}A_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{2\pi}{k}(Z\chi_{a}^{+}-Y_{a}\psi^{+}+\epsilon_{abc}\bar{\chi}^{b}_{-}\bar{Y}^{c})

(B.70)

\displaystyle Q^{a}A

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\bar{Q}_{a}A

\displaystyle=-\frac{4\pi i}{k}(Y_{a}\psi^{-}-Z\chi_{a}^{-}+\epsilon_{abc}\bar{\chi}^{b}_{+}\bar{Y}^{c})

(B.71)

\displaystyle Q^{a}\bar{A}

\displaystyle=\frac{4\pi}{k}(\bar{\psi}_{-}\bar{Y}^{a}-\bar{\chi}^{a}_{-}\bar{Z}+\epsilon^{abc}Y_{b}\chi_{c}^{+})

\displaystyle\bar{Q}_{a}\bar{A}

\displaystyle=0

(B.72)

\displaystyle Q^{a}\hat{A}_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{2\pi i}{k}(\bar{Y}^{a}\bar{\psi}_{+}-\bar{Z}\bar{\chi}^{a}_{+}-\epsilon^{abc}\chi_{c}^{-}Y_{b})

\displaystyle\bar{Q}_{a}\hat{A}_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{2\pi}{k}(\chi_{a}^{+}Z-\psi^{+}Y_{a}+\epsilon_{abc}\bar{Y}^{c}\bar{\chi}^{b}_{-})

(B.73)

\displaystyle Q^{a}\hat{A}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\bar{Q}_{a}\hat{A}

\displaystyle=-\frac{4\pi i}{k}(\psi^{-}Y_{a}-\chi_{a}^{-}Z+\epsilon_{abc}\bar{Y}^{c}\bar{\chi}^{b}_{+})

(B.74)

\displaystyle Q^{a}\bar{\hat{A}}

\displaystyle=\frac{4\pi}{k}(\bar{Y}^{a}\bar{\psi}_{-}-\bar{Z}\bar{\chi}^{a}_{-}+\epsilon^{abc}\chi_{c}^{+}Y_{b})

\displaystyle\bar{Q}_{a}\bar{\hat{A}}

\displaystyle=0

(B.75)

To check the closure of these transformations and use them on local operators, it is important to keep in mind the equations of motion.
For the gauge field we are interested in the components
\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{F}_{21}-i\mathcal{F}_{31}
and
\bar{\mathcal{F}}=\mathcal{F}_{21}+i\mathcal{F}_{31}
of the field strength. In particular, we focus on the first one, which respects the equation

\mathcal{F}=\frac{2\pi i}{k}\begin{pmatrix}Z\overleftrightarrow{D}\bar{Z}+Y_{a}\overleftrightarrow{D}\bar{Y}^{a}+\bar{\psi}_{+}\psi^{-}+\bar{\chi}_{+}^{a}\chi^{-}_{a}&0\\
0&-\bar{Z}\overleftrightarrow{D}Z-\bar{Y}^{a}\overleftrightarrow{D}Y_{a}-\psi^{-}\bar{\psi}_{+}-\chi^{-}_{a}\bar{\chi}_{+}^{a}\end{pmatrix}

(B.76)

where the operator
\overleftrightarrow{D}
has the usual definition
Z\overleftrightarrow{D}\bar{Z}\equiv ZD\bar{Z}-DZ\bar{Z}
.
For the fermions, we need the equation

\not{D}\psi_{J}=\frac{2\pi}{k}\left(\bar{C}^{I}C_{I}\psi_{J}-\psi_{J}C_{I}\bar{C}^{I}+2\psi_{I}C_{J}\bar{C}^{I}-2\bar{C}^{I}C_{J}\psi_{I}+2\epsilon_{ILKJ}\bar{C}^{I}\bar{\psi}^{L}\bar{C}^{K}\right)

(B.77)

whose projection yields (listing only the components needed for our computations)

\displaystyle D\psi^{+}=iD_{1}\psi^{-}

\displaystyle+\frac{2\pi i}{k}\left(\hat{l}_{B}\psi^{-}-\psi^{-}l_{B}+2\bar{Y}^{a}Z\chi^{-}_{a}-2\chi^{-}_{a}Z\bar{Y}^{a}-2\bar{Y}^{a}\bar{\chi}_{+}^{b}\bar{Y}^{c}\epsilon_{abc}\right)

(B.78)

\displaystyle D\chi_{a}^{+}=iD_{1}\chi^{-}_{a}

\displaystyle+\frac{2\pi i}{k}\left(\chi^{-}_{b}\Omega_{a}^{b}-\hat{\Omega}_{a}^{b}\chi^{-}_{b}-2\bar{Z}Y_{a}\psi^{-}+2\psi^{-}Y_{a}\bar{Z}+\right)

(B.79)

\displaystyle+\frac{4\pi i}{k}\epsilon_{acd}\left(\bar{Y}^{c}\bar{\psi}_{+}\bar{Y}^{d}+\bar{Y}^{d}\bar{\chi}_{+}^{c}\bar{Z}-\bar{Z}\bar{\chi}_{+}^{c}\bar{Y}^{d}\right)

(B.80)

with

\Omega_{a}^{b}=\Theta_{a}^{b}+\Lambda_{a}^{b}-\frac{1}{2}\delta_{a}^{b}l_{B}.

(B.81)

Weak Coupling Representation

In the following, we will also find it convenient to accommodate the original scalar and fermionic fields of ABJM theory according to the new R-symmetry pattern

\displaystyle C_{I}

\displaystyle=(Z,Y_{a})

\displaystyle\bar{C}^{I}

\displaystyle=(\bar{Z},\bar{Y}^{a})

(B.82)

\displaystyle\psi_{I}^{\pm}

\displaystyle=(\psi^{\pm},\chi_{a}^{\pm})

\displaystyle\bar{\psi}^{I}_{\pm}

\displaystyle=(\bar{\psi}_{\pm},\bar{\chi}^{a}_{\pm})

(B.83)

where
Y^{a}
(
\bar{Y}_{a}
) and
\chi^{a\pm}
(
\bar{\chi}_{a\pm}
) change in the
\mathbf{3}
(
\mathbf{\bar{3}}
) of SU
(3)
, whereas
Z
and
\psi^{\pm}

(\bar{\psi}_{\pm})
are singlet. Moreover, we have expressed them in the basis of eigenvectors of
\gamma_{1}=\sigma_{1}
, e.g.

\psi_{+}=(\psi_{1}+\psi_{2})\qquad\psi_{-}=(\psi_{1}-\psi_{2})

(B.84)

with the rules
\psi^{-}=-\psi_{+}
and
\psi^{+}=\psi_{-}
.
The two gauge fields and, consequently, the covariant derivative can be instead split according to the new spacetime symmetry pattern, namely

A_{\mu}=(A_{1},A=A_{2}-iA_{3},\bar{A}=A_{2}+iA_{3})\qquad\hat{A}_{\mu}=(\hat{A}_{1},\hat{A}=\hat{A}_{2}-i\hat{A}_{3},\hat{\bar{A}}=\hat{A}_{2}+i\hat{A}_{3})

(B.85)

and
D_{\mu}=(D_{1},D=D_{2}-iD_{3},\bar{D}=D_{2}+iD_{3}).
In terms of these new fields, the superconnection (
3.2
) for the case of the straight line takes the following form

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(t)

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}A_{1}&0\\
0&\hat{A}_{1}\end{pmatrix}+\frac{2\pi i}{k}\begin{pmatrix}Z\bar{Z}-Y_{a}\bar{Y}^{a}&0\\
0&\bar{Z}Z-\bar{Y}^{a}Y_{a}\end{pmatrix}+\sqrt{\frac{2\pi}{k}}\begin{pmatrix}0&-i\bar{\psi}_{+}\\
\psi^{+}&0\end{pmatrix}.

(B.86)

This subsection aims to provide an explicit realization of the ABJM displacement supermultiplet in terms of the fundamental fields of the underlying theory. Since these fields live along the Wilson lines and, in most cases, are obtained by varying the superconnection (
3.2
), they naturally possess the structure of a supermatrix.
The lowest component of the multiplet
\mathbb{F}
has quantum numbers
[\frac{1}{2},\frac{3}{2},0,0]
and thus we expect it to be a merely fermionic object. On the other hand, the only field or combination of elementary fields with these quantum numbers, which can appear in the
entries of the supermatrix, is the bosonic complex scalar
Z
. Therefore we shall write the following Ansatz for
\mathbb{F}

\displaystyle\mathbb{F}=i\sqrt{\frac{2\pi}{k}}\,\tilde{\epsilon}\begin{pmatrix}0&Z\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}\,,

(B.87)

where
\tilde{\epsilon}
is a fermionic parameter endowing
\mathbb{F}
with its anticommuting nature.
1
1

1

We remark that
\tilde{\epsilon}
is just a bookkeeping device to keep the memory of the Graßmann nature of the operator insertions when constructing their explicit representation in terms of super matrices, and it appears in the definitions (
B.87
), (
B.91
), (
B.93
), (
B.94
) of
\mathbb{F},\mathbb{O},\mathbb{\Lambda},\mathbb{D}
below. In any explicit gauge theory computation of four-point functions, the presence of such fermionic parameters (each of the fields in the correlator should have a different one) will result in an overall factor, which should be dropped when comparing the final result with any alternative calculation. In particular, there is no
\tilde{\epsilon}
dependence in the correlators of the quantum fluctuations of the fundamental dual string evaluated in section
3.3
. The fermionic/bosonic nature of these fluctuations is indeed already explicit in the corresponding fluctuation Lagrangian.
To construct the next element of the multiplet, we have to act with
\theta_{a}\,Q^{a}
on the operator inserted in the Wilson line, where
\theta_{a}
is a Graßmann-odd parameter that we can identify with one of the fermionic coordinates of the superspace constructed in subsection
3.2.2
. In the four-dimensional case, the only contribution would come from the action of
\theta_{a}\,Q^{a}
directly on the operator
since the connection and the (open) Wilson line are invariant. This is not the case for operators inserted in the fermionic Wilson loop of ABJM theory. There is an additional contribution coming from the variation of the Wilson line. However, this amounts to “covariantize” the usual action of the supersymmetry on the operator as follows

\displaystyle\delta_{\text{cov}}\,\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\bullet$}}}}

\displaystyle=\theta_{a}\,Q^{a}\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\bullet$}}}}+2\,\sqrt{\frac{2\pi}{k}}\,\left[\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
\,\theta_{a}\,\bar{Y}^{a}&0\end{pmatrix}\,,\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\bullet$}}}}\right]\,,

(B.88)

\displaystyle\bar{\delta}_{\text{cov}}\,\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\bullet$}}}}

\displaystyle=\bar{\theta}^{a}\,\bar{Q}_{a}\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\bullet$}}}}+2\,\sqrt{\frac{2\pi}{k}}\,\left[\begin{pmatrix}0&\bar{\theta}^{a}Y_{a}\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}\,,\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.75}{$\bullet$}}}}\right]\,,

(B.89)

where
\theta_{a}
and
\bar{\theta}^{a}
are Graßmann odd parameters associated to supertranslations. The commutators in (
B.88
) and (
B.89
) compensates the super-gauge transformation of the Wilson line induced by the supersymmetry transformation (
3.3
) with the matrix
\mathcal{G}
given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}=2\,\sqrt{\frac{2\pi}{k}}\begin{pmatrix}0&\bar{\theta}^{a}Y_{a}\\
\,\theta_{a}\,\bar{Y}^{a}&0\end{pmatrix}\,.

(B.90)

The action of
\delta_{\text{cov}}
on
\mathbb{F}
defined in (
B.87
) is quite straightforward to evaluate once we use the transformations in Appendix
B.3
. The final result is

\displaystyle\delta_{\text{cov}}\mathbb{F}=\,i\sqrt{\frac{2\pi}{k}}\theta_{a}\,\tilde{\epsilon}\begin{pmatrix}2\sqrt{\frac{2\pi}{k}}\,Z\,\bar{Y}^{a}&-\bar{\chi}^{a}_{+}\\
0&2\sqrt{\frac{2\pi}{k}}\,\bar{Y}^{a}\,Z\end{pmatrix}\equiv\theta_{a}\,\mathbb{O}^{a}\,.

(B.91)

Namely, we have obtained the second component of our supermultiplet (
3.41
), the one associated to the
R
-symmetry breaking. An identical expression can be obtained by exploiting the transformation under the action of
\mathsf{J}^{a}
appearing in the superconnection (
B.86
)

{\delta_{\mathsf{J}^{a}}(Z,{Y_{b}})=(0,i\delta^{a}_{b}Z),\qquad{\delta_{\mathsf{J}^{a}}(\bar{Z},\bar{Y}^{b}})=(-i\bar{Y}^{a}},0),\qquad{\delta_{\mathsf{J}^{a}}}\psi^{+}=0,\qquad{\delta_{\mathsf{J}^{a}}}\bar{\psi}_{+}=-i\bar{\chi}^{a}_{+},

(B.92)

Using (
3.33
), one can apply the action of these broken generators on the superconnection (
B.86
), recovering the same result for
\mathbb{O}^{a}
.
Similarly, the form of
\bar{\mathbb{O}}_{a}(t)
can be obtained by looking at the explicit action of
\bar{\mathsf{J}}_{a}
on the fields.
Applying once more
\delta_{\text{cov}}
, we reach the third component

\displaystyle\delta_{\text{cov}}\,\big(\,\theta_{a}\,\mathbb{O}^{a}\,\big)

\displaystyle=2i\sqrt{\frac{2\pi}{k}}\,\epsilon^{nrs}\,\theta_{r}\theta_{s}\,\tilde{\epsilon}\begin{pmatrix}\sqrt{\frac{2\pi}{k}}\,(\epsilon_{abn}\,\bar{\chi}^{a}_{+}\,\bar{Y}^{b}-Z\,\chi^{-}_{n})&-D\,Y_{n}\\
\frac{4\pi}{k}\,\epsilon_{abn}\bar{Y}^{a}\,Z\bar{Y}^{b}&\sqrt{\frac{2\pi}{k}}\,(\epsilon_{abn}\,\bar{Y}^{b}\,\bar{\chi}^{a}_{+}-\chi^{-}_{n}\,Z\,)\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle\equiv-\epsilon^{nrs}\,\theta_{r}\,\theta_{s}\,\mathbb{\Lambda}_{n}\,.

(B.93)

Also, in this case, the expression (
B.93
) for
\mathbb{\Lambda}_{n}
perfectly agrees with the one obtained by acting with the broken generator on the superconnection (
B.86
) according to (
3.34
).

Finally, applying again
\delta_{\text{cov}}
to (
B.93
) we obtain the top component
\mathbb{D}
of the supermultiplet (
3.41
), namely the displacement operator

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\delta_{\text{cov}}\big(-\epsilon^{nrs}

\displaystyle\theta_{r}\theta_{s}\mathbb{\Lambda}_{n}\big)\!=\!2\sqrt{\frac{2\pi}{k}}\epsilon^{nrs}\theta_{n}\theta_{r}\theta_{s}\,\tilde{\epsilon}\!~~\times

\displaystyle\times\begin{pmatrix}\!\sqrt{\frac{2\pi}{k}}\,i(2Z\,D\bar{Z}-2DY_{a}\bar{Y}^{a}+\bar{\chi}^{a}_{+}\chi^{-}_{a})&-i\,D\bar{\psi}_{+}\\
D\psi_{+}-i\,\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{t}\psi^{-}&\sqrt{\frac{2\pi}{k}}\,i(2D\bar{Z}\,Z-2\bar{Y}^{a}\,DY_{a}-\chi^{-}_{a}\,\bar{\chi}^{a}_{+})\!\end{pmatrix}\equiv

\displaystyle\equiv 2\epsilon^{nrs}\,\theta_{n}\,\theta_{r}\,\theta_{s}\,\mathbb{D}\,.

(B.94)

where
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{t}
is the covariant derivative constructed only with the bosonic part of the superconnection.
The
\mathbb{D}
insertion can also be constructed out of its abstract definition (
3.35
) by acting with a broken translation on the superconnection, as was done in equation (5.18) of
[
163
]
. In this case, the two expressions are not identical, and the difference between the two definitions is proportional to the matrix

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\sqrt{\frac{2\pi}{k}}\bar{\psi}_{+}\,\psi^{-}&0\\
\,\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{t}\psi^{-}&-\sqrt{\frac{2\pi}{k}}\psi^{-}\,\bar{\psi}_{+}\end{pmatrix}{\propto}\,\mathcal{D}_{t}\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
\psi^{-}&0\end{pmatrix}\,

(B.95)

This difference is compatible with the construction (
3.35
), which is blind to total derivatives.
For the case of Wilson lines insertions, total derivatives are implemented
[
163
]
as the following modification for an operator
\mathcal{O}(t)
inserted into the loop

\displaystyle\int dt~\mathcal{W}[\mathcal{O}(t)]=

\displaystyle\int dt~[\mathcal{W}[\mathcal{O}(t)]+\partial_{t}(\mathcal{W}[\Sigma(t)])]=

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int dt~(\mathcal{W}[\mathcal{O}(t)]+\mathcal{W}[\mathcal{D}_{t}\Sigma(t)])=\int dt~\mathcal{W}[\mathcal{O}(t)+\mathcal{D}_{t}\Sigma(t)]\,,

(B.96)

where
\mathcal{D}_{t}
is the covariant derivative defined in (
3.3
).
We conclude that our supermatrix construction perfectly agrees with the abstract structure outlined in subsection
3.2.2
.

B.4
Solving the Leading Mixing in ABJM

The following section presents the details of the solving of the mixing for the first-order anomalous dimension. This is done through the consideration of another correlator that exchanges four-particle operators in GFF. This leads to the conclusion that the degeneracy of the dimension is not lifted at this order and allows for an easier bootstrap in section
3.5
.
Mixing cannot occur between operators of different
U(1)
-charge so we focus on the uncharged operators.
2
2

2

The mixing between operators with different
U(1)
charges does not occur. This can be seen from the vanishing of the relevant three-point functions and the symmetry under the
U(1)
reflection
\mathbb{F}\rightarrow\bar{\mathbb{F}}
.

Length-2 Operators

The only length-2 operators are

\displaystyle F\partial^{(n)}\bar{F}

\displaystyle F\partial^{(n)}F

\displaystyle\bar{F}\partial^{(n)}\bar{F}

(B.97)

where
n
is an integer (odd in the second and third case). For even
n
, there is only one operator at each dimension, so no mixing occurs. However, for odd
n
, there is generically mixing. We divide the weight matrix into these three sectors, which we denote
\{0,-1,1\}
for the operator
\mathcal{O}_{0}
,
\mathcal{O}_{-1}
, and
\mathcal{O}_{1}
.
We look at the set of vanishing three-point functions

\displaystyle<FF\mathcal{O}_{1}>

\displaystyle=<FF\mathcal{O}_{0}>=0

(B.98)

\displaystyle<F\bar{F}\mathcal{O}_{1}>

\displaystyle=<F\bar{F}\mathcal{O}_{-1}>=0

(B.99)

These appear in the OPE expansion of the vanishing four-point function, which at first order gives

\displaystyle<FFF\bar{F}>^{(1)}|_{\log(\chi)}=\frac{1}{x_{12}x_{34}}\sum_{\Delta}c_{FF\mathcal{O}}\gamma_{\mathcal{O}\mathcal{O}^{\prime}}c_{\mathcal{O}^{\prime}F\bar{F}}G_{\Delta}(\chi).

(B.100)

From the analysis of the three-point functions, we know that the only non-vanishing terms are

\displaystyle<FFF\bar{F}>^{(1)}|_{\log(\chi)}=\frac{1}{x_{12}x_{34}}\sum_{\Delta}c_{FF\mathcal{O}_{-1}}\gamma^{(1)}_{\mathcal{O}_{-1}\mathcal{O}^{\prime}_{0}}c_{\mathcal{O}^{\prime}_{0}F\bar{F}}G_{\Delta}(\chi).

(B.101)

Since only one operator of these types appears at each weight, the vanishing of the correlator implies the vanishing of the anomalous dimension. Therefore, there is no mixing at this order between the operators
\mathcal{O}_{0}
and
\mathcal{O}_{1}
. Likewise, with the
U(1)
conjugate. We are left between the mixing of
\mathcal{O}_{\pm 1}
.

I would expect the
U(1)
symmetry to identify the anomalous dimension of the operators
\mathcal{O}_{1}
and
\mathcal{O}_{-1}
. Therefore we have a structure

\displaystyle\gamma=\begin{pmatrix}0&\gamma_{1}\\
\gamma_{1}&0\end{pmatrix}

(B.102)

Therefore the square has
\gamma_{\mathcal{O}_{1}\mathcal{O}_{-1}}^{2}
in the diagonal components, so we can safely ignore the mixing from length-2 operators at this order.

Length-4 Operators

The anomalous dimension
\gamma^{(1)}_{\Delta}
of exchanged operators computed at first order, such as in
[
1
]
is a weighted average of anomalous dimensions from operators which have the same weight in free theory, such as

\displaystyle\mathbb{F}\partial^{3}\bar{\mathbb{F}}

\displaystyle\mathbb{F}\partial\mathbb{F}\bar{\mathbb{F}}\partial\bar{\mathbb{F}}

(B.103)

which have conformal weight
\Delta=4
. This is especially relevant in the bootstrap process, where these contributions must be disentangled to use the data from the previous order, such as in
[
39
]
.

\displaystyle\sum_{\Delta}\left(\gamma^{(1)}_{\Delta}\right)^{2}c^{(0)}_{\Delta}G_{\Delta}(\chi)

(B.104)

At first order, since the relevant vertices at strong coupling have four legs, the solving of the mixing only needs to include two-particle and four-particle operators. In particular, the protected operator
\mathcal{O}_{2}=\mathbb{F}\partial\mathbb{F}
exchanges both two-particle and four-particle operators in free theory.
The strong coupling free theory result is obtained by Wick contracting the elementary fields.
3
3

3

In this case, the elementary field is
\mathbb{F}
. This differs from the generalised free field result since
\mathcal{O}_{2}
is a composite operator, not an elementary excitation. In the free theory, both length-two and length-four operators will be exchanged.

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{O}_{2}\bar{\mathcal{O}}_{2}\bar{\mathcal{O}}_{2}\mathcal{O}_{2}\rangle

\displaystyle=\lim_{\{t_{2},t_{4},t_{6},t_{8}\}\rightarrow\{t_{1},t_{3},t_{5},t_{7}\}}\partial_{t_{2}}\partial_{t_{4}}\partial_{t_{6}}\partial_{t_{8}}\langle\mathbb{F}\mathbb{F}\bar{\mathbb{F}}\bar{\mathbb{F}}\bar{\mathbb{F}}\bar{\mathbb{F}}\mathbb{F}\mathbb{F}\rangle

(B.105)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(t_{13}t_{24})^{4}}\frac{(\chi-1)^{4}}{\chi^{4}}

(B.106)

This gives

\displaystyle f(\chi)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(1-\chi)^{4}}

\displaystyle\hat{f}(\chi)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(1-\chi)^{4}\chi^{4}}

(B.107)

\displaystyle h(\chi)

\displaystyle=(1-\chi)^{4}

\displaystyle\hat{h}(\chi)

\displaystyle=\frac{(1-\chi)^{8}}{\chi^{4}}

(B.108)

In particular, notice that even though the unitarity bound is
h>4
in the charged channel, the lightest operator exchanged in the free theory has dimension
\Delta_{L}=8
and that this weight is the same as the operator
\mathbb{F}\partial\mathbb{F}\partial^{2}\mathbb{F}\partial^{3}\mathbb{F}
.

In the
s
-channel of
f
, the free spectrum is
h^{(0)}=1+n
and the OPE coefficients are

\displaystyle\tilde{c}_{h}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{\pi}2^{-2h-3}(h+1)^{2}(h+2)(h(h+2)+9)\Gamma(h+3)}{9\Gamma\left(h+\frac{3}{2}\right)}

(B.109)

In the
s
-channel of
h
, the free spectrum is the same (
h^{(0)}=1+n
) and the OPE coefficients are

\displaystyle c_{h^{(0)}}=(-1)^{h^{(0)}}\tilde{c}_{h^{(0)}}

(B.110)

as expected from arguments of S-parity argued in
[
92
,
1
]

In the
t
-channel of
h
, then free spectrum is
h^{(0)}=2+2n
with OPE coefficients:

\displaystyle\mathbb{c}_{h}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{\pi}4^{-h}\left((-1)^{h}+1\right)(h-6)(h-4)(h-2)(h+1)(h+3)(h+5)\Gamma(h)}{9\Gamma\left(h-\frac{1}{2}\right)}

(B.111)

Notice that the difference between the free solution and the GFF solution will be from the exchange of two-particle operators since the exchanged fields in GFF for
\mathcal{O}_{2}\sim\mathbb{F}\partial\mathbb{F}
will be of the form
\mathbb{F}\partial\mathbb{F}\partial^{(n)}\mathbb{F}\partial\mathbb{F}
.
Since the GFF for chiral fields of dimension
\Delta_{\phi}
is given by

\displaystyle\frac{\sqrt{\pi}2^{-2\Delta-1}\Gamma(\Delta)\Gamma(\Delta+2\Delta\phi+2)}{\Delta\Gamma\left(\Delta+\frac{3}{2}\right)\Gamma(2\Delta\phi)^{2}\Gamma(\Delta-2\Delta\phi+1)}

(B.112)

One can dissociate the two-particle and the four-particle contributions.

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{O}_{2}\bar{\mathcal{O}}_{2}\bar{\mathcal{O}}_{2}\mathcal{O}_{2}\rangle^{(0)}

\displaystyle=\lim_{\{t_{2},t_{4},t_{6},t_{8}\}\rightarrow\{t_{1},t_{3},t_{5},t_{7}\}}\partial_{t_{2}}\partial_{t_{4}}\partial_{t_{6}}\partial_{t_{8}}\langle\mathbb{F}\mathbb{F}\bar{\mathbb{F}}\bar{\mathbb{F}}\bar{\mathbb{F}}\bar{\mathbb{F}}\mathbb{F}\mathbb{F}\rangle^{(0)}

(B.113)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(t_{13}t_{24})^{4}}\frac{(\chi-1)^{4}}{\chi^{4}}

(B.114)

Bootstrap of
\mathbb{F}\partial\mathbb{F}

Using the same constraints as for
\mathbb{F}
, the specific ones for the chiral ring detailed in
B.4
, and the CFT data for the exchanged operator
\mathbb{F}\bar{\mathbb{F}}
the first-order perturbation can be fixed entirely. This gives

\displaystyle f^{(1)}_{\mathcal{O}_{2}}(\chi)=4\epsilon\frac{(\chi-4)(\chi-1)^{2}\log(1-\chi)-\chi(-4\chi+(\chi+3)\chi\log(\chi)+4)}{(\chi-1)^{5}\chi}

(B.115)

where

\displaystyle\epsilon=-\frac{1}{2\pi\sqrt{2\lambda}}

(B.116)

is the strong-coupling expansion parameter and
\lambda
the ’t Hooft coupling for ABJM.
This gives the following CFT data:

\displaystyle\gamma_{s}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\epsilon\Delta(\Delta+2)

(B.117)

\displaystyle\gamma_{t}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\epsilon(\Delta(\Delta-1)-20)

(B.118)

In particular, this would mean that both the two-particle and the four-particle operators with vanishing
U(1)
charge have anomalous dimensions proportional to the quadratic Casimir and we have

\displaystyle\langle(\gamma^{(1)})^{n}\rangle=\langle\gamma^{(1)}\rangle^{n}

(B.119)

Chiral Ring on the Line

The OPE expansion of two protected chiral operators reveals a tower of such operators organised by their charge under the
U(1)
symmetry and the shortening condition of the associated superprimary. This is similar to the case of
\mathcal{N}=4
SYM, where one can form higher dimension protected operators by considering the totally antisymmetric states
\phi^{[a_{1}}..\phi^{a_{n}]}
built from combinations of the displacement multiplet, these form a chiral ring.

The
\phi\times\phi
Chiral OPE

It is useful to review the chiral OPE presented in
[
1
]
which uses similar arguments to
[
253
,
254
,
185
]
before generalising this to chiral operators with larger charge.
The Displacement multiplet is a protected chiral operator satisfying

\displaystyle[\bar{Q},\phi]=[\bar{S},\phi]=0.

(B.120)

Combining this with the commutation relation
[\bar{Q},P]=\bar{S}
, this implies that
\bar{Q}
and
\bar{S}
also annihilate all the operators in the the OPE

\displaystyle\phi_{j_{0}}\times\phi_{j_{0}}

\displaystyle\sim\sum\mathcal{O}_{2j_{0}}

\displaystyle\qquad[\bar{Q},\mathcal{O}_{2j_{0}}]

\displaystyle=[\bar{S},\mathcal{O}_{2j_{0}}]=0

(B.121)

The chiral condition implies that the exchanged operator is either a chiral superprimary or a chiral superdescendent. We separate these cases as

\displaystyle\phi_{j_{0}}\times\phi_{j_{0}}

\displaystyle\sim\mathcal{O}^{1}_{2j_{0},0,0}+\bar{Q}(\mathcal{O}^{2}_{2j_{0}+\frac{1}{2},1,0})+\bar{Q}^{2}(\mathcal{O}^{3}_{2j_{0}+1,0,1})+\bar{Q}^{3}(\mathcal{L}_{2j_{0}+\frac{3}{2},0,0})

(B.122)

where the first three operators are short multiplets and satisfy the shortening conditions

\displaystyle\bar{Q}\mathcal{O}^{1}_{2j_{0},0,0}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\bar{Q}^{2}(\mathcal{O}^{2}_{2j_{0}+\frac{1}{2},1,0})

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\bar{Q}^{3}(\mathcal{O}^{3}_{2j_{0}+1,0,1})

\displaystyle=0

(B.123)

The highest weight super descendent
\bar{Q}^{3}(\mathcal{L}_{2j_{0}+\frac{3}{2},0,0})
does not have any shortening conditions and is, therefore, generically a long operator. Additionally, the long operators satisfy the unitarity bound

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathcal{L}}>\frac{2j_{0}}{3}+\frac{1}{2}

(B.124)

where the equality was ruled out thanks to the recombination of the long operator at the equality. This corresponds to the following bound for the exchanged operator

\displaystyle\Delta_{\bar{Q}^{3}\mathcal{L}}>\frac{2j_{0}}{3}+2

(B.125)

Chiral
O_{j_{r}}\times O_{j_{s}}
OPE and Chiral Ring

The three operators exchanged in the
\phi\times\phi
OPE are protected chiral operators satisfying

\displaystyle[\bar{Q},\mathcal{O}]

\displaystyle=[\bar{S},\mathcal{O}]=0

(B.126)

despite not all being superprimary operators. This is sufficient to generalise use the argument above in the OPE of two such chiral fields:

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{j_{r}}\times\mathcal{O}_{j_{s}}\sim\mathcal{O}^{1}_{j_{r}+j_{s},0,0}+\bar{Q}(\mathcal{O}^{2}_{j_{r}+j_{s}+\frac{1}{2},1,0})+\bar{Q}^{2}(\mathcal{O}^{3}_{j_{r}+j_{s}+1,0,1})+\bar{Q}^{3}(\mathcal{L}_{j_{r}+j_{s}+\frac{3}{2},0,0})

(B.127)

with a following unitarity bound for the first long exchanged operator

\displaystyle\Delta_{\bar{Q}^{3}\mathcal{L}}>\frac{j_{r}+j_{s}}{3}+2,

(B.128)

where the equality was again excluded thanks to the recombination rule at the bound. Additionally, since only one super descendent will contribute at each weight, the block expansion becomes the usual 1dconformal block expansion.

By iterating this analysis, we have a family of operators with integer multiples of
j_{0}

U(1)
charge.

\displaystyle\{\bar{\mathcal{B}}_{nj_{0}}\}=\{\bar{\mathcal{B}}_{n_{0}},\bar{\mathcal{B}}_{2j_{0}},..\}

(B.129)

\displaystyle\{\bar{\mathcal{B}}_{nj_{0}+\frac{1}{2},1}\}=\{\bar{\mathcal{B}}_{j_{0}+\frac{1}{2},1},\bar{\mathcal{B}}_{2j_{0}+\frac{1}{2},1},..\}

(B.130)

\displaystyle\{\bar{\mathcal{B}}_{nj_{0}+1,0,1}\}=\{\bar{\mathcal{B}}_{j_{0}+1,0,1},\bar{\mathcal{B}}_{2j_{0}+1,0,1},..\}

(B.131)

These operators with increasing
U(1)
charge have protected dimensions and form the chiral ring generated by the chiral field
\phi
. Since they were obtained by iterating the chiral OPE, they will be considered as operators of length
n
. For example

\displaystyle\bar{Q}\bar{\mathcal{B}}_{2j_{0}+\frac{1}{2},1}\sim\Phi\partial\Phi

(B.132)

is an operator of length two.
And the correlation functions will be of the same type, with

\displaystyle\frac{\langle\mathcal{O}_{\Delta}\bar{\mathcal{O}}_{\Delta}\mathcal{O}_{\Delta}\bar{\mathcal{O}}_{\Delta}\rangle}{\langle\mathcal{O}_{\Delta}\bar{\mathcal{O}}_{\Delta}\rangle^{2}}

\displaystyle=f(\chi)=\chi^{2\Delta}\hat{f}(\chi)

(B.133)

\displaystyle\frac{\langle\mathcal{O}_{\Delta}\bar{\mathcal{O}}_{\Delta}\bar{\mathcal{O}}_{\Delta}\mathcal{O}_{\Delta}\rangle}{\langle\mathcal{O}_{\Delta}\bar{\mathcal{O}}_{\Delta}\rangle\langle\bar{\mathcal{O}}_{\Delta}\mathcal{O}_{\Delta}\rangle}

\displaystyle=h(\chi)=\left(\frac{\chi}{1-\chi}\right)^{2\Delta}\hat{h}(\chi)

(B.134)

And the OPE expansions are

\displaystyle f(\chi)

\displaystyle=_{\chi\rightarrow 0}1+\sum_{h}\tilde{c}_{h}\chi^{h}{}_{2}F_{1}(h,h+3,2h+3;\chi)

(B.135)

\displaystyle\hat{f}(\chi)

\displaystyle=\hat{f}(1-\chi)

(B.136)

\displaystyle h(\chi)

\displaystyle=_{\chi\rightarrow 0}1+\sum_{h}c_{h}\chi^{h}{}_{2}F_{1}(h,h,2h+3;\chi)

(B.137)

\displaystyle\hat{h}(\chi)

\displaystyle=_{\chi\rightarrow 1}\sum_{h_{L}>2+\frac{2j_{R}}{3}}\mathbb{c}_{h}(1-\chi)^{h}{}_{2}F_{1}(h,h,2h;1-\chi)

(B.138)

Appendix C
\mathcal{N}=4
SYM Multipoint Superconformal Blocks

This section contains information that was not essential to the understanding of the structure and computation of correlators in Chapter
4
. Section
C.1
details some information about the 1/2-BPS multiplets. Sections
C.2
and
C.3
list the superconformal blocks corresponding to the OPE expansion in the symmetric and asymmetric channels respectively. These are then used to extract the CFT data in section
4.4
and to constrain the correlator in section
4.5
.

C.1
Multiplets

The 1/2-BPS multiplets have a straightforward structure since they can only be acted upon with one family of supercharges

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{k}:\qquad[0,k]_{s=0}^{h=k}\rightarrow[1,k-1]_{s=1}^{k+\frac{1}{2}}\rightarrow\begin{matrix}[0,k-1]_{s=2}^{h=k+1}\\
\\
[2,k-2]_{s=0}^{h=k+2}\end{matrix}\rightarrow[1,k-2]_{s=1}^{h=k+\frac{3}{2}}\rightarrow[0,k-2]_{s=0}^{h=k+2}

(C.1)

For the blocks of protected operators, the normalisation corresponding to having a unit topological sector gives free OPE coefficients consistent with the literature
[
37
]
where

\displaystyle c_{i,j,k}=\frac{\sqrt{i!j!k!}}{(ij,k)!(ik,j)!(jk,i)!}

(C.2)

where we used the shorthand
(ij,k)=\frac{i+j-k}{2}
.

The generic long operators have a full family of descendants from all the supercharges acting on the superconformal primary which can be found in the long version of
[
39
]
.

C.2
Symmetric Superconformal Blocks

This section consists of a list of the superconformal blocks arising from the OPE expansions between points 1 and 2 and points 3 and 4. Since these two channels
are
related by the crossing
\chi_{1}\rightarrow 1-\chi_{2}
, we refer to this as the
symmetric
blocks. The Figure
4.1
illustrates this OPE where the operators
\mathcal{I},\mathcal{D}_{2}
and
\mathcal{L}^{h}_{0,[0,0]}
are exchanged in both channels.These correspond to the identity
\mathcal{I}
, the 1/2-BPS operator of weight
2
,
\mathcal{D}_{2}
and the uncharged long operator
\mathcal{L}^{h}_{0,[0,0]}
.
It is useful to use variables that vanish in the OPE limit. Therefore, we define

\displaystyle\nu_{2}=1-\chi_{2}.

(C.3)

The superconformal blocks
\mathcal{G}_{A,B}
will correspond to the one with operator
A
being exchanged in the
\chi_{1}\rightarrow 0
limit and
B
being exchanged in the
\nu_{2}\rightarrow 0
limit. These can be written as a sum of five-point scalar blocks
g_{a,b}
, weighted by the appropriate R-symmetry block
h_{[a_{1},a_{2}],[b_{1},b_{2}]}
. The R-symmetry block is found by applying the
SU(4)
quadratic Casimir to points 12 and 34 and uniquely determines the form of
h_{[a_{1},a_{2}],[b_{1},b_{2}]}
from the representation of
A
and
B
. The full
(r_{i},s_{i},t,\chi_{i})-
dependant superblock can then be decomposed in the same way as the correlator, in the Ward identity channels described in
4.15
. The corresponding blocks decomposition will be denoted
f^{i}_{A,B}
to avoid confusion with the asymmetric super blocks.

\bullet\quad\mathchoice{\raisebox{-5.86177pt}{\resizebox{3197285}{917504}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.8611pt}{$\displaystyle\mathbf{\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{D}_{2}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-5.86177pt}{\resizebox{3197285}{917504}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.8611pt}{$\textstyle\mathbf{\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{D}_{2}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-7.31702pt}{\resizebox{3929502}{917504}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.49998pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathbf{\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{D}_{2}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-13.08455pt}{\resizebox{5254669}{917504}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.17656pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{D}_{2}}}$}}}}}

The selection rules allow for only one term

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{D}_{2}}=h_{[0,0][0,2]}g_{0,2}=\frac{r_{1}}{\chi_{1}^{2}}

(C.4)

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{D}_{2},\mathcal{I}}=h_{[0,2][0,0]}g_{2,0}=\frac{s_{2}}{\nu_{2}^{2}}

(C.5)

where the crossing symmetry above is clear. In terms of the functions
f_{i}
described above, this gives

\displaystyle f^{0}_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{D}_{2}}

\displaystyle=1

\displaystyle f^{1}_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{D}_{2}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\chi_{1}}

\displaystyle f^{2}_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{D}_{2}}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle f^{3}_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{D}_{2}}

\displaystyle=0

(C.6)

\displaystyle f^{0}_{\mathcal{D}_{2},\mathcal{I}}

\displaystyle=1

\displaystyle f^{1}_{\mathcal{D}_{2},\mathcal{I}}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle f^{2}_{\mathcal{D}_{2},\mathcal{I}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\nu_{2}}

\displaystyle f^{3}_{\mathcal{D}_{2},\mathcal{I}}

\displaystyle=0

(C.7)

\bullet\quad\mathchoice{\raisebox{-5.86177pt}{\resizebox{4194036}{917504}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.8611pt}{$\displaystyle\mathbf{\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{D}_{2},\mathcal{D}_{2}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-5.86177pt}{\resizebox{4194036}{917504}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.8611pt}{$\textstyle\mathbf{\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{D}_{2},\mathcal{D}_{2}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-7.31702pt}{\resizebox{5241545}{917504}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.49998pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathbf{\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{D}_{2},\mathcal{D}_{2}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-13.08455pt}{\resizebox{7101186}{917504}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.17656pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{D}_{2},\mathcal{D}_{2}}}$}}}}}

The selection rules restrict the superconformal block to the following expansion (where we have already eliminated the vanishing contributions)

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{D}_{2},\mathcal{D}_{2}}=\beta_{1}h_{[0,0][0,2]}g_{4,2}+\beta_{2}h_{[0,2][0,0]}g_{4,2}+\beta_{3}h_{[0,2][2,0]}g_{2,3}

\displaystyle+\beta_{4}h_{[2,0][0,2]}g_{3,2}+\beta_{5}h_{[0,2][0,2]}g_{2,2}+\beta_{6}h_{[2,0][2,0]}g_{3,3}

(C.8)

The Ward identities constrain the coefficients up to an overall constant which is normalised to
\beta_{5}=\frac{-1}{5}
for the lowest conformal weight
g_{2,2}
to match the normalisation conventions

\displaystyle\beta_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{3}{70}\beta_{5}

\displaystyle\beta_{2}

\displaystyle=-\frac{3}{70}\beta_{5}

\displaystyle\beta_{3}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{4}\beta_{5}

\displaystyle\beta_{4}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}\beta_{5}

\displaystyle\beta_{6}

\displaystyle=\frac{3}{40}\beta_{5}

(C.9)

In terms of the Ward identity channels, this gives

\displaystyle f^{0}_{\mathcal{D}_{2},\mathcal{D}_{2}}

\displaystyle=1

(C.10)

\displaystyle f^{1}_{\mathcal{D}_{2},\mathcal{D}_{2}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k,l}\frac{2(k+1)(l+1)(2(k+1)l+3k+2)}{\nu_{2}^{2}\chi_{1}^{2}(k+l+1)}G_{\mathcal{D}_{2},\mathcal{D}_{2}}^{k,l}

(C.11)

\displaystyle f^{2}_{\mathcal{D}_{2},\mathcal{D}_{2}}

\displaystyle=-\sum_{k,l}\frac{(k+1)(l+1)(2k(l+1)+3l+2)}{\nu_{2}^{2}\chi_{1}^{2}(k+l+1)}G_{\mathcal{D}_{2},\mathcal{D}_{2}}^{k,l}

(C.12)

\displaystyle f^{3}_{\mathcal{D}_{2},\mathcal{D}_{2}}

\displaystyle=-\sum_{k,l}\frac{(k+1)(k+2)(l+1)(l+2)}{2\nu_{2}^{2}\chi_{1}^{2}}G_{\mathcal{D}_{2},\mathcal{D}_{2}}^{k,l}

(C.13)

One can define the block

\displaystyle G_{\mathcal{D}_{2},\mathcal{D}_{2}}^{k,l}=\frac{3(2)_{k+l}}{2(5)_{k}(5)_{l}}\chi_{1}^{k+2}\nu_{2}^{l+2}

(C.14)

\displaystyle\sum_{k,l}G_{\mathcal{D}_{2},\mathcal{D}_{2}}^{k,l}=3\chi_{1}^{2}\nu_{2}^{2}F_{2}\left(2,1,1;5,5\,|\,\chi_{1},\nu_{2}\right)

(C.15)

and the function
f^{3}_{\mathcal{D}_{2},\mathcal{D}_{2}}
is then the derivative of this Appell function.

\bullet\quad\mathchoice{\raisebox{-10.69751pt}{\resizebox{6016906}{1179648}{\hbox{\raisebox{4.06108pt}{$\displaystyle\mathbf{\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{D}_{2},\mathcal{L}_{h_{2}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-10.69751pt}{\resizebox{6016906}{1179648}{\hbox{\raisebox{4.06108pt}{$\textstyle\mathbf{\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{D}_{2},\mathcal{L}_{h_{2}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-15.99289pt}{\resizebox{7682751}{1179648}{\hbox{\raisebox{4.24997pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathbf{\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{D}_{2},\mathcal{L}_{h_{2}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-26.09088pt}{\resizebox{10458111}{1179648}{\hbox{\raisebox{4.92654pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{D}_{2},\mathcal{L}_{h_{2}}}}$}}}}}

The selection rules restrict the superconformal block to the following expansion (where we have already eliminated the vanishing contributions)

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{D}_{2},\mathcal{L}_{h_{2}}}=

\displaystyle\beta_{1}h_{[0,2][0,0]}g_{2,h_{2}}+\beta_{2}h_{[0,2][0,0]}g_{2,h_{2}+2}+\beta_{3}h_{[0,2][0,0]}g_{2,h_{2}+4}

\displaystyle+\beta_{4}h_{[0,2][2,0]}g_{2,h_{2}+1}+\beta_{5}h_{[0,2][2,0]}g_{2,h_{2}+3}+\beta_{6}h_{[0,2][0,2]}g_{2,h_{2}+2}

\displaystyle+\beta_{7}h_{[2,0][2,0]}g_{3,h_{2}+1}+\beta_{8}h_{[2,0][2,0]}g_{3,h_{2}+3}+\beta_{9}h_{[2,0][0,2]}g_{3,h_{2}+2}

\displaystyle+\beta_{10}h_{[0,0][0,2]}g_{4,h_{2}+2}

(C.16)

The Ward identities constrain the constants (normalised to
g_{2,h_{2}}
)

\displaystyle\beta_{1}

\displaystyle=1

\displaystyle\beta_{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{3h_{2}(h_{2}+1)(h_{2}+3)}{10(h_{2}-1)(2h_{2}+1)(2h_{2}+5)}

\displaystyle\beta_{3}

\displaystyle=\frac{(h_{2}+1)(h_{2}+2)(h_{2}+3)^{2}(h_{2}+4)}{16(h_{2}-1)(2h_{2}+3)(2h_{2}+5)^{2}(2h_{2}+7)}

\displaystyle\beta_{4}

\displaystyle=\frac{h_{2}}{2(h_{2}-1)}

\displaystyle\beta_{5}

\displaystyle=\frac{(h_{2}+1)(h_{2}+2)(h_{2}+3)}{8(h_{2}-1)(2h_{2}+3)(2h_{2}+5)}

\displaystyle\beta_{6}

\displaystyle=\frac{h_{2}+1}{5(1-h_{2})}

\displaystyle\beta_{7}

\displaystyle=-\frac{h_{2}(h_{2}+1)}{20(h_{2}-1)}

\displaystyle\beta_{8}

\displaystyle=-\frac{(h_{2}+1)(h_{2}+2)(h_{2}+3)^{2}}{80(h_{2}-1)(2h_{2}+3)(2h_{2}+5)}

\displaystyle\beta_{9}

\displaystyle=-\frac{(h_{2}+1)(h_{2}+2)}{40(h_{2}-1)}

\displaystyle\beta_{10}

\displaystyle=\frac{(h_{2}+1)(h_{2}+2)(h_{2}+3)}{700(h_{2}-1)}

(C.17)

\displaystyle f^{0}_{\mathcal{D}_{2},\mathcal{L}_{\Delta_{2}}}

\displaystyle=0

(C.18)

\displaystyle f^{1}_{\mathcal{D}_{2},\mathcal{L}_{\Delta_{2}}}

\displaystyle=-\sum_{k,l}\frac{(k+1)(\Delta_{2}+l)\left(-2\Delta_{2}(\Delta_{2}+1)+k\left(-\Delta_{2}^{2}+\Delta_{2}+4l+2\right)+4l\right)}{\nu_{2}\chi_{1}^{2}(\Delta_{2}+l+1)(\Delta_{2}+k+l)}G_{\mathcal{D}_{2},\mathcal{L}_{\Delta_{2}}}

(C.19)

\displaystyle f^{2}_{\mathcal{D}_{2},\mathcal{L}_{\Delta_{2}}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k,l}\frac{(k+1)}{\nu_{2}\chi_{1}^{2}(\Delta_{2}+l+1)(\Delta_{2}+k+l)}P_{\mathcal{D}_{2},\mathcal{L}_{\Delta_{2}}}G_{\mathcal{D}_{2},\mathcal{L}_{\Delta_{2}}}

(C.20)

\displaystyle f^{3}_{\mathcal{D}_{2},\mathcal{L}_{\Delta_{2}}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k,l}\frac{(k+1)(k+2)(\Delta_{2}+l)}{\nu_{2}\chi_{1}^{2}}G_{\mathcal{D}_{2},\mathcal{L}_{\Delta_{2}}}

(C.21)

Where

\displaystyle P_{\mathcal{D}_{2},\mathcal{L}_{\Delta_{2}}}^{k,l}

\displaystyle=-\Delta_{2}(\Delta_{2}+1)(\Delta_{2}(k+2)-3k-4)+(4k+6)l^{2}+l(\Delta_{2}(-\Delta_{2}(k+2)+5k+6)-2)

(C.22)

\displaystyle G_{\mathcal{D}_{2},\mathcal{L}_{\Delta_{2}}}^{k,l}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\chi_{1}^{2}\nu_{2}^{\Delta_{2}}}{4(\Delta_{2}-1)}\frac{(1)_{k}\chi_{1}^{k}\nu_{2}^{l}(\Delta_{2}+2)_{l}(\Delta_{2}+1)_{k+l}}{(5)_{k}\Gamma(k+1)\Gamma(l+1)(2\Delta_{2}+4)_{l}}

(C.23)

and the sum is

\displaystyle\sum_{k,l}G_{\mathcal{D}_{2},\mathcal{L}_{\Delta_{2}}}^{k,l}=-\frac{\chi_{1}^{2}\nu_{2}^{\Delta_{2}}}{4(\Delta_{2}-1)}F_{2}\left(1+\Delta_{2},1,\Delta_{2}+2;5,2(\Delta_{1}+2)\,|\,\chi_{1},\nu_{2}\right)

(C.24)

and
f^{3}_{\mathcal{D}_{2},\mathcal{L}_{\Delta_{2}}}
is related to the direct derivative of this Appell function.

\bullet\quad\mathchoice{\raisebox{-10.69751pt}{\resizebox{6016906}{1179648}{\hbox{\raisebox{4.06108pt}{$\displaystyle\mathbf{\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{L}_{h_{1}},\mathcal{D}_{2}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-10.69751pt}{\resizebox{6016906}{1179648}{\hbox{\raisebox{4.06108pt}{$\textstyle\mathbf{\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{L}_{h_{1}},\mathcal{D}_{2}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-15.99289pt}{\resizebox{7682751}{1179648}{\hbox{\raisebox{4.24997pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathbf{\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{L}_{h_{1}},\mathcal{D}_{2}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-26.09088pt}{\resizebox{10458111}{1179648}{\hbox{\raisebox{4.92654pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{L}_{h_{1}},\mathcal{D}_{2}}}$}}}}}

This is given by the crossing symmetric of
\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{D}_{2},\mathcal{L}_{h_{2}}}

\bullet\quad\mathchoice{\raisebox{-10.69751pt}{\resizebox{6641479}{1179648}{\hbox{\raisebox{4.06108pt}{$\displaystyle\mathbf{\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{L}_{h_{1}},\mathcal{L}_{h_{2}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-10.69751pt}{\resizebox{6641479}{1179648}{\hbox{\raisebox{4.06108pt}{$\textstyle\mathbf{\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{L}_{h_{1}},\mathcal{L}_{h_{2}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-15.99289pt}{\resizebox{8626372}{1179648}{\hbox{\raisebox{4.24997pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathbf{\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{L}_{h_{1}},\mathcal{L}_{h_{2}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-26.09088pt}{\resizebox{11786126}{1179648}{\hbox{\raisebox{4.92654pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{L}_{h_{1}},\mathcal{L}_{h_{2}}}}$}}}}}

The selection rules restrict the terms to the following:

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{L}_{h_{1}},\mathcal{L}_{h_{2}}}

\displaystyle=\beta_{1}h_{[0,0][0,2]}g_{\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2}+2}+\beta_{2}h_{[0,0][0,2]}g_{\Delta_{1}+2,\Delta_{2}+2}+\beta_{3}h_{[0,0][0,2]}g_{\Delta_{1}+4,\Delta_{2}+2}

\displaystyle+\beta_{4}h_{[2,0][2,0]}g_{\Delta_{1}+1,\Delta_{2}+1}+\beta_{5}h_{[2,0][2,0]}g_{\Delta_{1}+1,\Delta_{2}+3}+\beta_{6}h_{[2,0][2,0]}g_{\Delta_{1}+3,\Delta_{2}+1}

\displaystyle+\beta_{7}h_{[2,0][2,0]}g_{\Delta_{1}+3,\Delta_{2}+3}+\beta_{8}h_{[2,0][0,2]}g_{\Delta_{1}+1,\Delta_{2}+2}+\beta_{9}h_{[2,0][0,2]}g_{\Delta_{1}+3,\Delta_{2}+2}

\displaystyle+\beta_{10}h_{[0,2][0,0]}g_{\Delta_{1}+2,\Delta_{2}}+\beta_{11}h_{[0,2][0,0]}g_{\Delta_{1}+2,\Delta_{2}+2}+\beta_{12}h_{[0,2][0,0]}g_{\Delta_{1}+2,\Delta_{2}+4}

\displaystyle+\beta_{13}h_{[0,2][2,0]}g_{\Delta_{1}+2,\Delta_{2}+1}+\beta_{14}h_{[0,2][2,0]}g_{\Delta_{1}+2,\Delta_{2}+3}+\beta_{15}h_{[0,2][0,2]}g_{\Delta_{1}+2,\Delta_{2}+2}

\displaystyle+\beta_{16}h_{[0,1][0,1]}g_{\Delta_{1}+2,\Delta_{2}+2}

(C.25)

The Ward identities fix all the coefficients (except for
\beta_{16}
which multiplies
h_{[0,1][0,1]}=0
) up to a normalisation factor. We set
\beta_{4}=1
since it is the lowest weight term which is crossing symmetric

\{\Delta_{1},\chi_{1},h_{[a][b]}\}\leftrightarrow\{\Delta_{2},1-\chi_{2},h_{[b][a]}\}

This gives the coefficients consistent with crossing symmetry

\displaystyle\beta_{4}

\displaystyle=1

\displaystyle\beta_{1}

\displaystyle=\tilde{\beta}_{10}

\displaystyle\beta_{2}

\displaystyle=\tilde{\beta}_{11}

\displaystyle\beta_{3}

\displaystyle=\tilde{\beta}_{12}

\displaystyle\beta_{5}

\displaystyle=\tilde{\beta}_{6}

\displaystyle\beta_{8}

\displaystyle=\tilde{\beta}_{13}

\displaystyle\beta_{9}

\displaystyle=-\tilde{\beta}_{14}

\displaystyle\beta_{16}

\displaystyle=0

(C.26)

Where the tilde indicates the replacement
\Delta_{1}\leftrightarrow\Delta_{2}
and

\displaystyle\beta_{1}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\Delta_{1}-1}{\Delta_{2}}

(C.27)

\displaystyle\beta_{5}

\displaystyle=\frac{\left(\Delta_{2}+3\right)\left(\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}\right)\left(\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}+1\right)}{4\Delta_{2}\left(2\Delta_{2}+3\right)\left(2\Delta_{2}+5\right)}

(C.28)

\displaystyle\beta_{2}

\displaystyle=-\frac{3\left(\Delta_{1}+3\right)\left(\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}\right)\left(\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}+1\right)}{10\left(2\Delta_{1}+1\right)\left(2\Delta_{1}+5\right)\Delta_{2}}

(C.29)

\displaystyle\beta_{3}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\left(\Delta_{1}+3\right)\left(\Delta_{1}+4\right)\left(\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}\right)\left(\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}+1\right)\left(\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}+2\right)\left(\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}+3\right)}{16\Delta_{1}\left(2\Delta_{1}+3\right)\left(2\Delta_{1}+5\right){}^{2}\left(2\Delta_{1}+7\right)\Delta_{2}}

(C.30)

\displaystyle\beta_{8}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{\Delta_{1}}{\Delta_{2}}+1\right)

(C.31)

\displaystyle\beta_{9}

\displaystyle=\frac{\left(\Delta_{1}+3\right)\left(\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}\right)\left(\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}+1\right)\left(\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}+2\right)}{8\Delta_{1}\left(2\Delta_{1}+3\right)\left(2\Delta_{1}+5\right)\Delta_{2}}

(C.32)

The corresponding blocks in the Ward identity channels are then:

\displaystyle f^{0}_{\mathcal{L}_{\Delta_{1}},\mathcal{L}_{\Delta_{2}}}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle f^{1}_{\mathcal{L}_{\Delta_{1}},\mathcal{L}_{\Delta_{2}}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k,l}\frac{\Delta_{2}+l}{\nu_{2}\chi_{1}(\Delta_{1}+k+1)(\Delta_{2}+l+1)(\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}+k+l-1)}(P^{1})^{k,l}_{\mathcal{L}_{\Delta_{1}},\mathcal{L}_{\Delta_{2}}}G^{k,l}_{\mathcal{L}_{\Delta_{1}},\mathcal{L}_{\Delta_{2}}}

\displaystyle f^{2}_{\mathcal{L}_{\Delta_{1}},\mathcal{L}_{\Delta_{2}}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k,l}\frac{\Delta_{1}+k}{\nu_{2}\chi_{1}(\Delta_{1}+k+1)(\Delta_{2}+l+1)(\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}+k+l-1)}(P^{2})^{k,l}_{\mathcal{L}_{\Delta_{1}},\mathcal{L}_{\Delta_{2}}}G^{k,l}_{\mathcal{L}_{\Delta_{1}},\mathcal{L}_{\Delta_{2}}}

\displaystyle f^{3}_{\mathcal{L}_{\Delta_{1}},\mathcal{L}_{\Delta_{2}}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k,l}\frac{(\Delta_{1}+k)(\Delta_{2}+l)}{\nu_{2}\chi_{1}}G^{k,l}_{\mathcal{L}_{\Delta_{1}},\mathcal{L}_{\Delta_{2}}}

(C.34)

Where

\displaystyle G^{k,l}_{\mathcal{L}_{\Delta_{1}},\mathcal{L}_{\Delta_{2}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{(\Delta_{1}+2)_{k}(\Delta_{2}+2)_{l}(\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2})_{k+l}}{\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Gamma(k+1)\Gamma(l+1)(2\Delta_{1}+4)_{k}(2\Delta_{2}+4)_{l}}\chi_{1}^{k+\Delta_{1}}\nu_{2}^{l+\Delta_{2}}

(C.35)

\displaystyle(P^{1})^{k,l}_{\mathcal{L}_{\Delta_{1}},\mathcal{L}_{\Delta_{2}}}

\displaystyle=-k^{2}\left(-\Delta_{2}^{2}+\Delta_{2}+4l+2\right)

\displaystyle\quad+k\left((\Delta_{2}+1)(\Delta_{2}+2)+\Delta_{1}^{2}(\Delta_{2}+l+1)+\Delta_{1}\left(2\Delta_{2}^{2}+\Delta_{2}-5l-1\right)\right)

\displaystyle\quad+\Delta_{1}(\Delta_{1}+1)(\Delta_{2}(\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2})+\Delta_{1}+(\Delta_{1}-3)l-1)

(C.36)

\displaystyle(P^{2})^{k,l}_{\mathcal{L}_{\Delta_{1}},\mathcal{L}_{\Delta_{2}}}

\displaystyle=-\Delta_{2}(\Delta_{2}+1)((\Delta_{1}+1)(\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}-1)+(\Delta_{2}-3)k)

\displaystyle\quad+l^{2}\left(-\Delta_{1}^{2}+\Delta_{1}+4k+2\right)

\displaystyle\quad-l\left(\Delta_{1}^{2}(2\Delta_{2}+1)+\Delta_{1}\left(\Delta_{2}^{2}+\Delta_{2}+3\right)+\Delta_{2}(\Delta_{2}+(\Delta_{2}-5)k-1)+2\right)

(C.37)

Notice that

\displaystyle\sum_{k,l}G^{k,l}_{\mathcal{L}_{\Delta_{1}},\mathcal{L}_{\Delta_{2}}}=\frac{\chi_{1}^{\Delta_{1}}\nu_{2}^{\Delta_{2}}}{\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}}F_{2}\left(\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2},\Delta_{1}+2,\Delta_{2}+2;2(\Delta_{1}+2),2(\Delta_{2}+2)\,|\,\chi_{1},\nu_{2}\right)

(C.38)

So we have a situation very similar to the four-point case where the superblocks and scalar blocks are not that dissimilar. Note that
f^{3}_{\mathcal{L}_{\Delta_{1}},\mathcal{L}_{\Delta_{2}}}
is just a derivative of the Appell function.

C.3
Blocks in the Asymmetric Channel

This section consists of a list of the superconformal blocks arising from the OPE expansions between points 1 and 2 and points 4 and 5. Since these two channels are
not
related by the crossing
\chi_{1}\rightarrow 1-\chi_{2}
, we refer to this as the
asymmetric
blocks. The Figure
4.2
illustrates this OPE where the operators
\mathcal{I},\mathcal{D}_{1},\mathcal{D}_{2},\mathcal{D}_{3},\mathcal{L}^{h}_{0,[0,0]}
and
\mathcal{L}^{h}_{0,[0,1]}
are exchanged. These correspond to the identity
\mathcal{I}
, the 1/2-BPS operator of weight
k
,
\mathcal{D}_{k}
, the uncharged long operator
\mathcal{L}^{h}_{0,[0,0]}
, and the charged long Operator
\mathcal{L}^{h}_{0,[0,1]}
.
It is useful to use variables that vanish in the OPE limit. Therefore, we define

\displaystyle\nu_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{\chi_{1}-\chi_{2}}{1-\chi_{2}}

\displaystyle\nu_{2}

\displaystyle=1-\chi_{2}

(C.39)

The superconformal blocks
\mathcal{G}_{A,B}
will correspond to the one with operator
A
being exchanged in the
\nu_{1}\rightarrow 0
limit and
B
being exchanged in the
\nu_{2}\rightarrow 0
limit. These can be written as a sum of five-point scalar blocks
g_{a,b}
, weighted by the appropriate R-symmetry block
h_{[a_{1},a_{2}],[b_{1},b_{2}]}
. The R-symmetry block is found by applying the
SU(4)
quadratic Casimir to points 12 and 45 and uniquely determines the form of
h_{[a_{1},a_{2}],[b_{1},b_{2}]}
from the representation of
A
and
B
. The full
(r_{i},s_{i},t,\chi_{i})-
dependant superblock can then be decomposed in the same way as the correlator, in the Ward identity channels described in
4.15
. The corresponding blocks decomposition will be denoted
g^{i}_{A,B}
to avoid confusion with the symmetric super blocks.

\bullet\quad\mathchoice{\raisebox{-5.86177pt}{\resizebox{3197285}{917504}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.8611pt}{$\displaystyle\mathbf{\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{D}_{1}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-5.86177pt}{\resizebox{3197285}{917504}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.8611pt}{$\textstyle\mathbf{\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{D}_{1}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-7.31702pt}{\resizebox{3929502}{917504}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.49998pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathbf{\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{D}_{1}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-13.08455pt}{\resizebox{5254669}{917504}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.17656pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{D}_{1}}}$}}}}}

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{D}_{1},\mathcal{I}}=\frac{r_{1}s_{2}}{\chi_{1}^{2}(1-\chi_{2})^{2}}

(C.40)

\displaystyle g^{0}_{\mathcal{D}_{1},\mathcal{I}}

\displaystyle=1

\displaystyle g^{1}_{\mathcal{D}_{1},\mathcal{I}}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle g^{2}_{\mathcal{D}_{1},\mathcal{I}}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle g^{3}_{\mathcal{D}_{1},\mathcal{I}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{(\nu_{1}\nu_{2})^{2}}

(C.41)

\bullet\quad\mathchoice{\raisebox{-5.86177pt}{\resizebox{4194036}{917504}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.8611pt}{$\displaystyle\mathbf{\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{D}_{2},\mathcal{D}_{1}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-5.86177pt}{\resizebox{4194036}{917504}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.8611pt}{$\textstyle\mathbf{\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{D}_{2},\mathcal{D}_{1}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-7.31702pt}{\resizebox{5241545}{917504}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.49998pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathbf{\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{D}_{2},\mathcal{D}_{1}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-13.08455pt}{\resizebox{7101186}{917504}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.17656pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{D}_{2},\mathcal{D}_{1}}}$}}}}}

\displaystyle g^{0}_{\mathcal{D}_{2},\mathcal{D}_{1}}

\displaystyle=1

(C.42)

\displaystyle g^{1}_{\mathcal{D}_{2},\mathcal{D}_{1}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\nu_{2}}\sum_{k_{2}}-\frac{6(k_{2}-2)\nu_{1}^{k_{2}}}{(k_{2}+2)(k_{2}+3)(k_{2}+4)}

(C.43)

\displaystyle g^{2}_{\mathcal{D}_{2},\mathcal{D}_{1}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\nu_{2}}\sum_{k_{2}}\frac{12(1+k_{2})\nu_{1}^{k_{2}}}{(k_{2}+2)(k_{2}+3)(k_{2}+4)}

(C.44)

\displaystyle g^{3}_{\mathcal{D}_{2},\mathcal{D}_{1}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\nu_{2}^{2}}\sum_{k_{2}}\frac{12(k_{2}-2)\nu_{1}^{k_{2}}}{(k_{2}+3)(k_{2}+4)(k_{2}+5)}

(C.45)

Note that the normalisation was chosen so that the OPE coefficient of the protected operator
c_{112}
corresponds to the localisation result.

\bullet\quad\mathchoice{\raisebox{-5.86177pt}{\resizebox{4194036}{917504}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.8611pt}{$\displaystyle\mathbf{\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{D}_{2},\mathcal{D}_{3}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-5.86177pt}{\resizebox{4194036}{917504}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.8611pt}{$\textstyle\mathbf{\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{D}_{2},\mathcal{D}_{3}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-7.31702pt}{\resizebox{5241545}{917504}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.49998pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathbf{\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{D}_{2},\mathcal{D}_{3}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-13.08455pt}{\resizebox{7101186}{917504}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.17656pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{D}_{2},\mathcal{D}_{3}}}$}}}}}

\displaystyle g^{0}_{\mathcal{D}_{2},\mathcal{D}_{3}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{21}{2}

(C.46)

\displaystyle g^{1}_{\mathcal{D}_{2},\mathcal{D}_{3}}

\displaystyle=-\sum_{k_{1},k_{2}}\frac{(k_{1}-2)(k_{1}+1)}{(k_{2}+5)}G_{\mathcal{D}_{2},\mathcal{D}_{3}}^{k_{1},k_{2}}

(C.47)

\displaystyle g^{2}_{\mathcal{D}_{2},\mathcal{D}_{3}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k_{1},k_{2}}\frac{2(k_{1}+1)^{2}}{(k_{2}+5)(k_{2}+6)}G_{\mathcal{D}_{2},\mathcal{D}_{3}}^{k_{1},k_{2}}

(C.48)

\displaystyle g^{3}_{\mathcal{D}_{2},\mathcal{D}_{3}}

\displaystyle=-\sum_{k_{1},k_{2}}\frac{2(k_{1}+1)(k_{1}+2)(k_{2}+1)}{(k_{1}+5)(k_{2}+6)(k_{2}+7)}G_{\mathcal{D}_{2},\mathcal{D}_{3}}^{k_{1},k_{2}}

(C.49)

where

\displaystyle G_{\mathcal{D}_{2},\mathcal{D}_{3}}^{k_{1},k_{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\Gamma(8)\Gamma(k_{2}+1)}{4\Gamma(k_{1}+5)\Gamma(-k_{1}+k_{2}+1)}(-\nu_{1})^{k_{1}}\nu_{2}^{k_{2}}

(C.50)

\bullet\quad\mathchoice{\raisebox{-14.84203pt}{\resizebox{7906271}{1179648}{\hbox{\raisebox{5.63446pt}{$\displaystyle\mathbf{\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{L}^{h}_{0,[0,0]},\mathcal{D}_{1}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-14.84203pt}{\resizebox{7906271}{1179648}{\hbox{\raisebox{5.63446pt}{$\textstyle\mathbf{\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{L}^{h}_{0,[0,0]},\mathcal{D}_{1}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-21.76294pt}{\resizebox{10381832}{1179648}{\hbox{\raisebox{5.78331pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathbf{\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{L}^{h}_{0,[0,0]},\mathcal{D}_{1}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-34.27663pt}{\resizebox{14256690}{1179648}{\hbox{\raisebox{6.4722pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{L}^{h}_{0,[0,0]},\mathcal{D}_{1}}}$}}}}}

\displaystyle g^{0}_{\mathcal{L}^{h}_{0,[0,0]},\mathcal{D}_{1}}

\displaystyle=0

(C.51)

\displaystyle g^{1}_{\mathcal{L}^{h}_{0,[0,0]},\mathcal{D}_{1}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k_{1}}h^{3}+h^{2}(k_{1}+1)+hk_{1}-(k_{1}-3)k_{1}G_{\mathcal{L}^{h}_{0,[0,0]},\mathcal{D}_{1}}^{k_{1},k_{2}}

(C.52)

\displaystyle g^{2}_{\mathcal{L}^{h}_{0,[0,0]},\mathcal{D}_{1}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k_{1}}-((h+h^{2}-2k1)(h+k1))G_{\mathcal{L}^{h}_{0,[0,0]},\mathcal{D}_{1}}^{k_{1},k_{2}}

(C.53)

\displaystyle g^{3}_{\mathcal{L}^{h}_{0,[0,0]},\mathcal{D}_{1}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k_{1}}-\frac{\left(h^{2}+h-2k_{1}\right)(h+k_{1}-1)}{\nu_{1}\nu_{2}}G_{\mathcal{L}^{h}_{0,[0,0]},\mathcal{D}_{1}}^{k_{1},k_{2}}

(C.54)

where

\displaystyle G_{\mathcal{L}^{h}_{0,[0,0]},\mathcal{D}_{1}}^{k_{1},k_{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{(-1)^{k_{1}+1}\Gamma(2h+4)\Gamma(h+k_{1})\Gamma(h+k_{1}+1)}{\left(h^{2}-1\right)\Gamma(h+1)^{2}\Gamma(k_{1}+1)\Gamma(2h+k_{1}+4)}(-\nu_{1})^{h+k_{1}-1}\nu_{2}^{-1}

(C.55)

\bullet\quad\mathchoice{\raisebox{-14.84203pt}{\resizebox{7906271}{1179648}{\hbox{\raisebox{5.63446pt}{$\displaystyle\mathbf{\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{L}^{h}_{0,[0,0]},\mathcal{D}_{3}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-14.84203pt}{\resizebox{7906271}{1179648}{\hbox{\raisebox{5.63446pt}{$\textstyle\mathbf{\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{L}^{h}_{0,[0,0]},\mathcal{D}_{3}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-21.76294pt}{\resizebox{10381832}{1179648}{\hbox{\raisebox{5.78331pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathbf{\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{L}^{h}_{0,[0,0]},\mathcal{D}_{3}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-34.27663pt}{\resizebox{14256690}{1179648}{\hbox{\raisebox{6.4722pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{L}^{h}_{0,[0,0]},\mathcal{D}_{3}}}$}}}}}

This block vanishes from selection rules since the uncharged long does not appear in the
\mathcal{D}_{1}\times\mathcal{D}_{3}
OPE
[
35
]
.

\bullet\quad\mathchoice{\raisebox{-14.84203pt}{\resizebox{7906271}{1179648}{\hbox{\raisebox{5.63446pt}{$\displaystyle\mathbf{\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{D}_{2},\mathcal{L}^{h}_{0,[0,1]}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-14.84203pt}{\resizebox{7906271}{1179648}{\hbox{\raisebox{5.63446pt}{$\textstyle\mathbf{\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{D}_{2},\mathcal{L}^{h}_{0,[0,1]}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-21.76294pt}{\resizebox{10381832}{1179648}{\hbox{\raisebox{5.78331pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathbf{\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{D}_{2},\mathcal{L}^{h}_{0,[0,1]}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-34.27663pt}{\resizebox{14256690}{1179648}{\hbox{\raisebox{6.4722pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{D}_{2},\mathcal{L}^{h}_{0,[0,1]}}}$}}}}}

\displaystyle g^{0}_{\mathcal{D}_{2},\mathcal{L}^{h}_{0,[0,1]}}

\displaystyle=0

(C.56)

\displaystyle g^{1}_{\mathcal{D}_{2},\mathcal{L}^{h}_{0,[0,1]}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k_{1},k_{2}}(k_{1}-2)G_{\mathcal{D}_{2},\mathcal{L}^{h}_{0,[0,1]}}^{k_{1},k_{2}}

(C.57)

\displaystyle g^{2}_{\mathcal{D}_{2},\mathcal{L}^{h}_{0,[0,1]}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k_{1},k_{2}}(-2(k_{1}+1))G_{\mathcal{D}_{2},\mathcal{L}^{h}_{0,[0,1]}}^{k_{1},k_{2}}

(C.58)

\displaystyle g^{3}_{\mathcal{D}_{2},\mathcal{L}^{h}_{0,[0,1]}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k_{1},k_{2}}\frac{2(k_{1}+2)(h-k_{1}+k_{2}-2)}{(k_{1}+5)\nu_{2}}G_{\mathcal{D}_{2},\mathcal{L}^{h}_{0,[0,1]}}^{k_{1},k_{2}}

(C.59)

where

\displaystyle G_{\mathcal{D}_{2},\mathcal{L}^{h}_{0,[0,1]}}^{k_{1},k_{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{(k_{1}+1)(h+k_{2}-1)(h-1)_{k_{2}}(h+3)_{k_{2}}}{2(h-2)(h-1)(5)_{k_{1}}(1)_{k_{2}}(2h+4)_{k_{2}}(h+k_{2}-1)_{-k_{1}}}(-\nu_{1})^{k_{1}}\nu_{2}^{h+k_{2}-2}

(C.61)

\bullet\quad\mathchoice{\raisebox{-14.84203pt}{\resizebox{10420210}{1179648}{\hbox{\raisebox{5.63446pt}{$\displaystyle\mathbf{\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{L}^{h}_{0,[0,0]},\mathcal{L}^{h}_{0,[0,1]}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-14.84203pt}{\resizebox{10420210}{1179648}{\hbox{\raisebox{5.63446pt}{$\textstyle\mathbf{\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{L}^{h}_{0,[0,0]},\mathcal{L}^{h}_{0,[0,1]}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-21.76294pt}{\resizebox{14024535}{1179648}{\hbox{\raisebox{5.78331pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathbf{\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{L}^{h}_{0,[0,0]},\mathcal{L}^{h}_{0,[0,1]}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-34.27663pt}{\resizebox{19383285}{1179648}{\hbox{\raisebox{6.4722pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{L}^{h}_{0,[0,0]},\mathcal{L}^{h}_{0,[0,1]}}}$}}}}}

\displaystyle g^{0}_{\mathcal{L}^{h_{1}}_{0,[0,0]},\mathcal{L}^{h_{2}}_{0,[0,1]}}

\displaystyle=0

(C.62)

\displaystyle g^{1}_{\mathcal{L}^{h_{1}}_{0,[0,0]},\mathcal{L}^{h_{2}}_{0,[0,1]}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k_{1},k_{2}}\left(-\left(h_{2}^{2}+h_{2}+3\right)k_{1}-h_{2}^{2}(h_{2}+1)+k_{1}^{2}\right)G_{\mathcal{L}^{h}_{0,[0,0]},\mathcal{L}^{h}_{0,[0,1]}}^{k_{1},k_{2}}

(C.63)

\displaystyle g^{2}_{\mathcal{L}^{h_{1}}_{0,[0,0]},\mathcal{L}^{h_{2}}_{0,[0,1]}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k_{1},k_{2}}\left((h_{2}^{2}+h_{2}-2k_{1})(h_{2}+k_{1})\right)G_{\mathcal{L}^{h_{1}}_{0,[0,0]},\mathcal{L}^{h_{2}}_{0,[0,1]}}^{k_{1},k_{2}}

(C.64)

\displaystyle g^{3}_{\mathcal{L}^{h_{1}}_{0,[0,0]},\mathcal{L}^{h_{2}}_{0,[0,1]}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k_{1},k_{2}}\frac{\left(h_{2}^{2}+h_{2}-2k_{1}\right)(h_{2}+k_{1}-1)}{\nu_{1}\nu_{2}}G_{\mathcal{L}^{h_{1}}_{0,[0,0]},\mathcal{L}^{h_{2}}_{0,[0,1]}}^{k_{1},k_{2}}

(C.65)

where

\displaystyle G_{\mathcal{L}^{h_{1}}_{0,[0,0]},\mathcal{L}^{h_{2}}_{0,[0,1]}}^{k_{1},k_{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\left((h_{1}-1)_{k_{2}}(h_{2}+1)_{k_{1}}(h_{1}+h_{2}+3)_{k_{2}}\right)}{(h_{2}-1)h_{2}(h_{2}+1)(2h_{1}+4)_{k_{2}}(2h_{2}+4)_{k_{1}}(h_{1}-h_{2}+k_{2})_{-k_{1}}}\frac{\nu_{2}^{h_{1}+k_{2}-2}(-\nu_{1})^{h_{2}+k_{1}-1}}{k_{1}!k_{2}!}

(C.66)

Appendix D
Effective Theories in AdS
2

This Appendix groups the Witten diagram computations in the first section of Chapter
5
. The first section (
D.1
) details the derivations for the odd-massless case and the
\Delta=2
case which were not included in
5.1
. Then, a list of results corresponding to contact diagrams is included. In section
D.2
, the case of the exchange diagram, presented in
2.3.1
and in
D.2
is gone through in detail.

D.1
Contact Diagrams

n
-odd Massless Scalars

The massless odd-
n
case can be solved with contour integration for the
x
and the
\chi
coordinates.
1
1

1

This is true for any convergent integral with odd
\sum_{i}\Delta_{i}
. The resulting correlator will thus be a rational polynomial in the cross-ratios, though it may not have a simple form.
Since the integrand and the residue of the pole in the
x
coordinates are antisymmetric under
z\rightarrow-z
, we can extend the region of integration of
z
to the entire real line. This is best seen in the trivial example of the conformal massless three-point function

\displaystyle\lim_{\Lambda\rightarrow\infty}\Lambda^{2}I(0,1,\Lambda)=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{dz}{z^{2}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}dx\frac{z^{3}}{(z^{2}+x^{2})(z^{2}+(x-1)^{2})}.

(D.1)

Since the integrand is antisymmetric under
z\rightarrow-z
, we need to compensate for the sign change when extending the range of the integral over
z

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{dz\textrm{sgn}(z)}{z^{2}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}dx\frac{z^{3}}{(z^{2}+x^{2})(z^{2}+(x-1)^{2})}.

(D.2)

When considering the
x
-contour integral (
D.2
), we are now faced with two situations for the contour integral; the first (
z<0
) is depicted on the left of Figure
D.1
, the second (
z>0
) is depicted on the right.

x-iz
x+iz
x\in\mathbb{C}
\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{R}}
\mathcal{C}_{\infty}
x+iz
x-iz
x\in\mathbb{C}
\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{R}}
\mathcal{C}_{\infty}

Figure D.1
:
The contour here is closed in the UHP (the same analysis holds for the LHP closing). However, since
z
is now defined on the entire real line, the pole enclosed in the given contour depends on the sign of
z
. The case where
z<0
(Left) will have a residue of the opposite sign when compared to that where
z>0
(Right).

The sign of the pole included in the contour cancels the sign contribution from equation (
D.2
). The range of
z
can be extended after having done the
x
-integral to obtain the same conclusion. In so doing, one obtains

\displaystyle\pi\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}dz\frac{1}{4z^{2}+1}=\frac{\pi^{2}}{2}.

(D.3)

This reasoning holds for a general
n
-point function whose integrand is antisymmetric under
z\rightarrow-z
. In one of the examples in subsection
5.1.3
, we will see the case of topological operators where the polynomial dependence on the external coordinates is just a constant (up to a sign-dependent factor).

We have an analytic integral over the entire real
z
line.

\displaystyle I(x_{i})

\displaystyle=\frac{2\pi i}{2}\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}dzz^{n-2}\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}\frac{1}{2iz\Pi_{i\neq j}(x_{ij}^{2}+2izx_{ij})}

(D.4)

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi}{2}\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}dzz^{n-3}\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}\frac{1}{\Pi_{i\neq j}(x_{ij}^{2}+2izx_{ij})}.

(D.5)

The integrand has a good large-
|z|
behaviour, and we can analytically continue
z
and evaluate this integral by contour integration, neglecting the vanishing contribution from the contour at
\infty
. There are poles at positions

\displaystyle z^{*}=-\frac{x_{ij}}{2i},

(D.6)

with residues

\displaystyle\hskip-5.69046pt\!Res_{z=z^{*}}\left(\frac{z^{n-3}}{\Pi_{k\neq j}(x_{kj}^{2}+2izx_{kj})}\right)=\frac{x_{ij}^{n-4}}{(2i)^{n-4}\Pi_{k\neq j\neq i}x_{kj}x_{ki}}.

(D.7)

We close the contour in the UHP, where only the poles where
x_{i}-x_{j}>0
contribute.
This gives the final result for odd-
n
and ordered
x_{i}

\displaystyle I_{n\,odd}(x_{i})

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi^{2}}{2(2i)^{n-3}}\sum_{i>j}^{n}\frac{(x_{i}-x_{j})^{n-4}}{\Pi_{k\neq j\neq i}x_{kj}x_{ki}}.

(D.8)

This formula agrees with the canonical case of
n=3
, and the explicit results for
n=5
and
n=7
are given in Appendix
D.1
.

Derivation of
\Delta=2

One may, of course, be interested in higher
\Delta
. This is where this method loses some of its power. While it is mighty in the generic
n
regime, it quickly increases complexity when
\Delta
increases. However, the complexity will only be combinatorial and not intrinsic.
The integrand of

\displaystyle I(x_{i})=\int dzz^{2n-2}\int dx\frac{1}{\Pi_{i=1}^{N-1}(z^{2}+(x-x_{i})^{2})^{2}}

(D.9)

only has double poles (and single poles from the expansion around these poles) at the position
x=x_{i}+iz
with residue

\displaystyle\underset{x=x_{i}+iz}{Res}\left(\!\frac{1}{\Pi(z^{2}+(x-x_{i})^{2})^{2}}\right)\!=\!\partial_{x}\left(\frac{1}{(x-(x_{i}-iz))^{2}}\frac{1}{{\displaystyle\prod_{j,j\neq i}}(z^{2}+(x-x_{j})^{2})^{2}}\right)\Biggr|_{x=x_{i}+iz}

(D.10)

\displaystyle=-\sum_{i}\frac{1}{4z^{2}}(\frac{1}{iz}+\partial_{x_{i}})\left(\frac{1}{\Pi_{j\neq i}x_{ij}^{2}(2iz+x_{ij})^{2}}\right),

where the residue was massaged into a more usable form. As in the massless case, these can be integrated using partial fraction decomposition. By comparison of simple and double poles, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{i}\frac{z^{2n-n_{0}}}{\prod_{k}(z+a_{ik})^{2}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\neq j}\frac{c_{ij}}{(z+a_{ij})^{2}}-\sum_{i\neq j}\frac{\partial_{a_{ij}}c_{ij}}{(z+a_{ij})}

(D.11)

\displaystyle=-\sum_{i\neq j}\partial_{a_{ij}}\left(\frac{c_{ij}}{(z+a_{ij})}\right),

(D.12)

\displaystyle c_{ij}

\displaystyle=\frac{(-a_{ij})^{2n-n_{0}}}{\prod_{k\neq j}(a_{ki}-a_{ji})^{2}}.

(D.13)

as long as the
n_{0}>-1
. This is integrated by sight

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\Lambda}dz\left(\sum_{i\neq j}\frac{c_{ij}}{(z+a_{ij})^{2}}-\sum_{i}\frac{\partial_{a_{ij}}c_{ij}}{(z+a_{ij})}\right)=\sum_{i\neq j}\partial_{a_{ij}}\left(c_{ij}\ln(a_{ij})\right)+(\partial_{a_{i}}c_{ij})\ln(\Lambda).

(D.14)

Explicitly, we have

\displaystyle\int dzz^{2n-2}\frac{-1}{4z^{2}}\sum_{i}\partial_{x_{i}}\left(\frac{1}{\prod_{j\neq i}x_{ij}^{2}(2iz+x_{ij})^{2}}\right)

(D.15)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}\sum_{j\neq i}\partial_{x_{i}}\frac{1}{4(2i)^{2n-3}x_{ij}^{2}}\partial_{x_{j}}\left(\frac{x_{ji}^{2n-4}}{\prod_{k\neq j,k\neq i}x_{kj}^{2}x_{ik}^{2}}\ln\frac{x_{ji}}{2i}\right),

(D.16)

and

\displaystyle\int dz\frac{z^{2n-5}}{4i}\left(\frac{1}{\prod_{j\neq i}x_{ij}^{2}(2iz+x_{ij})^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}\sum_{j\neq i}\frac{1}{4i(2i)^{2n-4}x_{ij}^{2}}\partial_{x_{j}}\left(\frac{x_{ji}^{2n-5}}{\prod_{k\neq j,k\neq i}x_{kj}^{2}x_{ki}^{2}}\ln\frac{x_{ji}}{2i}\right).

(D.17)

As in the massless case, the divergent terms cancel, and the logarithm is a well-defined function with a branch cut on the negative real axis.
This gives the result

\displaystyle I_{\Delta=2,n}(x_{i})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}\sum_{j\neq i}\frac{-\pi}{2(2i)^{2n-4}x_{ij}^{2}}\partial_{x_{j}}\left(\frac{x_{ji}^{2n-5}}{\prod_{k\neq j,k\neq i}x_{kj}^{2}x_{ki}^{2}}\ln\frac{x_{ji}}{2i}\right)

\displaystyle+\sum_{i}

\displaystyle\sum_{j\neq i}\partial_{x_{i}}\frac{-\pi}{(2i)^{2n-2}x_{ij}^{2}}\partial_{x_{j}}\left(\frac{x_{ji}^{2n-4}}{\prod_{k\neq j,k\neq i}x_{kj}^{2}x_{ik}^{2}}\ln\frac{x_{ji}}{2i}\right).

(D.18)

\displaystyle\Big(\left(\chi_{2}-1\right)\left(\chi_{3}-1\right)\left(\chi_{2}-\chi_{4}\right)\chi_{1}^{2}

\displaystyle+\chi_{2}\left(\chi_{3}^{2}+\chi_{4}\left(\chi_{3}+\chi_{4}-2\right)\chi_{3}-\chi_{4}-\chi_{2}\left(\chi_{3}-1\right)\left(\chi_{3}+\chi_{4}+1\right)\right)\chi_{1}
