We also find agreement between the pinching of the
2n
-point function of massless correlators and the
n
-point function of
\Delta=2
correlators up to
n=8
. Still, these were omitted from the text since they are bulky and not elucidating.
Notice that the prefactor (
1.19
) has no neighbouring terms of type
x_{i,i+1}
except for the
x_{n-1,n}
term and the
n=3
cases so that no single pinching will lead to divergences in the prefactor. In general, one expects the divergences appearing in the pinching to be physical divergences that need to be regularised, not artefacts of this method.

D
-functions

The quartic contact diagrams with external conformal dimensions
\Delta_{i}
are expressed in terms of
D
-functions
[
194
,
56
,
97
]
, defined for the general case of
\text{AdS}_{d+1}
as

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!D_{\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}}(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4})=\!\!\int\!\!\frac{dzd^{d}x}{z^{d+1}}\tilde{K}_{\Delta_{1}}\!(z,x;x_{1})\tilde{K}_{\Delta_{2}}\!(z,x;x_{2})\tilde{K}_{\Delta_{3}}\!(z,x;x_{3})\tilde{K}_{\Delta_{4}}\!(z,x;x_{4})\,

(D.32)

For vertices with derivatives, the following identity is useful

\displaystyle g^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\tilde{K}_{\Delta_{1}}(z,x;x_{1})\ \partial_{\nu}\tilde{K}_{\Delta_{2}}(z,x;x_{2})

(D.33)

\displaystyle\qquad=\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\left[\tilde{K}_{\Delta_{1}}(z,x;x_{1})\tilde{K}_{\Delta_{2}}(z,x;x_{2})-2x_{12}^{2}\tilde{K}_{\Delta_{1}+1}(z,x;x_{1})\tilde{K}_{\Delta_{2}+1}(z,x;x_{2})\right]\ \,,

(D.34)

where
g^{\mu\nu}={z^{2}}\delta^{\mu\nu}
and
\partial_{\mu}=(\partial_{z},\partial_{r})
,
r=0,1,2,...,\text{d}-1
.

To make explicit the covariant form of the correlator, it is useful to introduce the “reduced” functions
\bar{D}

[
97
]
, defined as (
\Sigma\equiv\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i}\Delta_{i}
)

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!D_{\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}}=\frac{\pi^{d\over 2}\Gamma\left(\Sigma-{d\over 2}\right)}{2\,\Gamma\left(\Delta_{1}\right)\Gamma\left(\Delta_{2}\right)\Gamma\left(\Delta_{3}\right)\Gamma\left(\Delta_{4}\right)}\frac{x_{14}^{2(\Sigma-\Delta_{1}-\Delta_{4})}x_{34}^{2(\Sigma-\Delta_{3}-\Delta_{4})}}{x_{13}^{2(\Sigma-\Delta_{4})}x_{24}^{2\Delta_{2}}}\bar{D}_{\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}}(u,v)

(D.35)

which depends only on the cross-ratios
u=\frac{x_{12}x_{34}}{x_{13}x_{24}}\,,v=\frac{x_{14}x_{23}}{x_{13}x_{24}}
. Their explicit expression in terms Feynman parameters integral is

\bar{D}_{\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}}(u,v)=\int d\alpha d\beta d\gamma\ \delta(\alpha+\beta+\gamma-1)\ \alpha^{\Delta_{1}-1}\beta^{\Delta_{2}-1}\gamma^{\Delta_{3}-1}\frac{\Gamma\left(\Sigma-\Delta_{4}\right)\Gamma\left(\Delta_{4}\right)}{\big(\alpha\gamma+\alpha\beta\,u+\beta\gamma\,v\big)^{\Sigma-\Delta_{4}}}\,.

(D.36)

In d=1 as usual they only depend on the single variable
\chi

\displaystyle u=\chi^{2},\quad v=(1-\chi)^{2}

(D.37)

\displaystyle\bar{D}_{\Delta\Delta\Delta\Delta}(\chi)=\frac{\Gamma(\Delta)^{4}}{\Gamma(2\Delta)}(1-\chi)^{-2\Delta}\!\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\!d\tau\,e^{-\tau}{}_{2}F_{1}\big(\Delta,\Delta,2\Delta,\textstyle-\frac{4\chi}{(1-\chi^{2})}\cosh^{2}\frac{\tau}{2}\big)\,.

(D.38)

Some explicit expression for
\bar{D}
-functions read

\displaystyle\bar{D}_{1111}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{2\log(1-\chi)}{\chi}-\frac{2\log(\chi)}{1-\chi}\,,

(D.39)

\displaystyle\bar{D}_{2222}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{2\left(\chi^{2}-\chi+1\right)}{15(1-\chi)^{2}\chi^{2}}+\frac{\left(2\chi^{2}-5\chi+5\right)\log(\chi)}{15(\chi-1)^{3}}-\frac{\left(2\chi^{2}+\chi+2\right)\log(1-\chi)}{15\chi^{3}}\,,

(D.40)

\displaystyle\bar{D}_{3333}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\left(8\chi^{4}-36\chi^{3}+64\chi^{2}-56\chi+28\right)\log(\chi)}{105(\chi-1)^{5}}+\frac{\left(-8\chi^{4}-4\chi^{3}-4\chi^{2}-4\chi-8\right)\log(1-\chi)}{105\chi^{5}}

(D.41)

\displaystyle+\frac{-24\chi^{6}+72\chi^{5}-74\chi^{4}+28\chi^{3}-74\chi^{2}+72\chi-24}{315(\chi-1)^{4}\chi^{4}}\,.

Further expressions can be found through the identities in
[
97
]
. Useful relations between
\bar{D}
-function of consequent weight are

\displaystyle\Delta\,\bar{D}_{\Delta\Delta\Delta\Delta}

\displaystyle=\bar{D}_{\Delta\Delta\Delta+1\Delta+1}+\bar{D}_{\Delta\Delta+1\Delta\Delta+1}+\bar{D}_{\Delta+1\Delta\Delta\Delta+1}\,,

(D.42)

\displaystyle(\Delta_{2}+\Delta_{4}-\Sigma)\,\bar{D}_{\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}}

\displaystyle=\bar{D}_{\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}+1\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}+1}-\bar{D}_{\Delta_{1}+1\Delta_{2}\Delta_{3}+1\Delta_{4}}\,,

(D.43)

\displaystyle(\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{4}-\Sigma)\,\bar{D}_{\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}}

\displaystyle=\bar{D}_{\Delta_{1}+1\Delta_{2}\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}+1}-(1-\chi)^{2}\bar{D}_{\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}+1\Delta_{3}+1\Delta_{4}}\,,

(D.44)

\displaystyle(\Delta_{3}+\Delta_{4}-\Sigma)\,\bar{D}_{\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}}

\displaystyle=\bar{D}_{\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{3}+1\Delta_{4}+1}-\chi^{2}\bar{D}_{\Delta_{1}+1\Delta_{2}+1\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}}\,,

(D.45)

\displaystyle\bar{D}_{\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}}

\displaystyle=(1-\chi)^{2(\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{4}-\Sigma)}\bar{D}_{\Delta_{2}\Delta_{1}\Delta_{4}\Delta_{3}}\,,

(D.46)

\displaystyle=\bar{D}_{\Sigma-\Delta_{3}\Sigma-\Delta_{4}\Sigma-\Delta_{1}\Sigma-\Delta_{2}}\,,

(D.47)

\displaystyle=\chi^{2(\Delta_{3}+\Delta_{4}-\Sigma)}\bar{D}_{\Delta_{4}\Delta_{3}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{1}}\,.

(D.48)

D.2
Exchange Diagrams

This section contains complementary material relating to the derivation and interpretation of the exchange diagrams. First, details on the conformal Casimir and its relation to the equation of motion in AdS are given. Then the derivation of the relationship between exchange and contact diagrams from
[
120
,
95
]
is reviewed. This is then applied to the toy model of
\phi^{3}
interaction in AdS
2
, which gives insight into Polyakov blocks whose perturbative strong coupling structure is shown explicitly.

Quadratic Casimir

The
n
-Casimir of the boundary conformal group is given by

\displaystyle C^{(n)}_{i_{1}..i_{n}}=\frac{1}{2}\{\sum_{k=1}^{n}L_{i_{k}}^{(0)},\sum_{k=1}^{n}L_{i_{k}}^{(0)}\}-\frac{1}{2}[\sum_{k=1}^{n}L_{i_{k}}^{-\alpha},\sum_{k=1}^{n}L_{i_{k}}^{\alpha}],

(D.49)

where
L^{(0)}
are elements of the Cartan and the others are the simple roots. Explicitly for a d=1 conformal boundary, the differential expression of the operators is:

\displaystyle D

\displaystyle=L_{0}=\Delta+x\partial_{x}

\displaystyle P

\displaystyle=L_{-1}=-\partial_{x}

\displaystyle K

\displaystyle=L_{+1}=-2\Delta x-x^{2}\partial_{x}

(D.50)

This leads to a linear Casimir

\displaystyle C^{(1)}_{a}

\displaystyle=\Delta(\Delta-1),

(D.51)

which is the mass-squared of the bulk operator and a quadratic Casimir:

\displaystyle C^{(2)}_{x,y}

\displaystyle=2(x-y)(-\Delta_{1}\partial_{y}+\Delta_{2}\partial_{x})-(y-x)^{2}\partial_{x}\partial_{y}+(\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}-1)(\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}).

(D.52)

The quadratic Casimir of the AdS
2
isometries is the Laplacian of AdS,
this is best seen in flat embedding coordinates where the generators are given by

\displaystyle J_{AB}=-i(X_{A}\partial_{B}-X_{B}\partial_{A}),

(D.53)

where
X_{A}X^{A}=1
. The Quadratic Casimir of the AdS isometries in embedding coordinates is then

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{L}_{a}\mathcal{L}^{a}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}J_{AB}J^{AB}

(D.54)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}(X_{A}\partial_{B}-X_{B}\partial_{A})(X^{A}\partial^{B}-X^{B}\partial^{A})

(D.55)

\displaystyle=X_{A}X^{A}\partial_{B}\partial^{B}+(1-d)X_{B}\partial^{B}

(D.56)

\displaystyle=\partial_{A}\partial^{A},

(D.57)

which is the coordinate-independent Laplacian.

A case of interest here is when we have
\Delta_{1}=\Delta_{2}
. The conformal quadratic Casimir then simplifies further to

\displaystyle C^{(2)}_{z}=2\Delta(2\Delta-1)f(z)-z\left((z-1)zf^{\prime\prime}(z)+(2\Delta(z-2)+z)f^{\prime}(z)\right),

(D.58)

where we have made a change of variable

\displaystyle z=1-\frac{x}{y}.

(D.59)

Such changes of variables can be made to reduce this differential equation into a single variable differential equation for each of the
(s,t,u)
exchange channels.

Relating the exchange and contact diagrams

We review the analysis from
[
255
,
94
]
, which goes through a detailed computation of the exchange diagram and the
z
integral. However, they specialise in the case where the resulting exchange correlator can be written in terms of a finite sum of
D
functions. The toy model does not satisfy the conditions needed for such a simplification, but it is still useful to illustrate the Polyakov blocks.

-\lambda
-\lambda
\Delta_{E}
x_{1}
x_{4}
x_{3}
x_{2}

Figure D.2
:
Exchange Witten diagram for four external insertions of identical scalars of weight
\Delta_{\phi}
exchanging a scalar of weight
\Delta_{E}
in the
t
channel.

The integral we are interested in, corresponding to the Witten diagram (
D.2
), is

\displaystyle J^{(t)}(x_{1},...,x_{4})

\displaystyle=\int\frac{d^{2}w}{w_{0}^{2}}I(w,x_{2},x_{3})K_{\Delta_{1}}(x_{1},w)K_{\Delta_{4}}(x_{4},w),

(D.60)

\displaystyle I(w,x_{2},x_{3})

\displaystyle=\int\frac{d^{2}z}{z_{0}^{2}}G_{\Delta_{E}}(w,z)K_{\Delta_{2}}(x_{2},z)K_{\Delta_{3}}(x_{3},z).

(D.61)

The integral
I(z_{a},x_{a};x_{2},x_{3})
is a boundary-boundary-to-bulk three-point function and has conformal symmetry.

a
\Delta_{E}
x_{2}
x_{3}
\mathcal{L}_{a}a
\Delta_{E}
L_{3}x_{2}
L_{4}x_{3}

Figure D.3
:
The system is invariant under an infinitesimal transformation (
D.62
).

I(w,x_{2},x_{3})
in (
D.61
) is invariant under global transformations generated by
\mathbf{\mathcal{L}}_{a}+\vec{L}_{2}+\vec{L}_{3}
, where the first term generates the isometries of
\text{AdS}_{2}
and the other two generate the conformal transformation of the boundary.
2
2

2

We write the transformations under the conformal group as vectors
\vec{L}_{2}=(L_{-1},L_{0},L_{1})
acting on point 2.
As such, we can write

\displaystyle(\mathbf{\mathcal{L}}_{a}+\vec{L}_{2}+\vec{L}_{3})I(z_{a},x_{a};x_{2},x_{3})=0,

(D.62)

and can therefore relate the Casimirs of the generators

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{\mathcal{L}}_{a}^{2}I(z_{a},x_{a};x_{2},x_{3})

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}(\vec{L}_{2}+\vec{L}_{3})^{2}I(z_{a},x_{a};x_{2},x_{3})

(D.63)

\displaystyle=C^{(23)}_{2}I(z_{a},x_{a};x_{2},x_{3}).

(D.64)

In one dimension, the quadratic Casimir of the
\text{AdS}_{2}
isometries is the Laplacian of the bulk
D.2
. This Laplacian will allow us to eliminate the bulk-to-bulk propagator through the equation of motion (
1.40
). Linking the previous elements together, we obtain

\displaystyle(C_{(23)}^{(2)}-m^{2}_{E})I(z_{a},x_{a};x_{2},x_{3})

\displaystyle=(\nabla_{a}^{2}-m_{E}^{2})I(z_{a},x_{a};x_{2},x_{3})

(D.65)

\displaystyle=K_{\Delta_{\phi}}(z_{a},x_{a};x_{2})K_{\Delta_{\phi}}(z_{a},x_{a};x_{3}).

(D.66)

The quadratic Casimir acting on points 3 and 4 commutes with the other coordinates so that we can write a differential equation relating the full exchange diagram to the contact term

\displaystyle(C_{(23)}^{(2)}-m^{2}_{E})J(x_{1},x_{2},x_{2},x_{3})

\displaystyle=\int\frac{dz_{a}dx_{a}}{z_{a}^{2}}\Pi_{i=1}^{4}K_{\Delta_{\phi}}(z_{a},x_{a};x_{i})

(D.67)

\displaystyle=\frac{A}{(x_{13}x_{24})^{2\Delta_{\phi}}}\bar{D}_{\Delta_{\phi}\Delta_{\phi}\Delta_{\phi}\Delta_{\phi}}(\chi).

(D.68)

The same analysis holds for any two legs attached to a bulk-to-bulk propagator, though the final differential equation might depend on many variables.

Polyakov Blocks and Exchange Witten Diagrams

The computation of the exchange Witten diagram in the main text (
2.3.1
) is the sum of solutions to second-order differential equations. The three-point function and the symmetry of the correlator provide the integration constants. The example given in the main text, corresponding to the Polyakov block of external dimension
\Delta=1
and exchanged dimension
\Delta_{E}=1
, can be computed with the toy model of a massless scalar theory in AdS
2
with a
\phi^{3}
interaction. This corresponds to the action

\displaystyle S_{\phi^{3}}=\int\frac{dtdz}{z^{2}}\left(\partial_{\mu}\phi\partial^{\mu}\phi-\frac{\lambda}{3!}\phi^{3}\right).

(D.69)

GFF gives the leading order contribution. The NLO (
O(\lambda)
) correlators come from the constant vertex, giving the three-point function

\displaystyle<\phi(x_{1})\phi(x_{2})\phi(x_{3})>

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda}{\pi^{3}}\frac{\pi^{2}}{2x_{12}x_{23}x_{13}},

(D.70)

and the OPE coefficient

\displaystyle c_{111}

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda}{2\pi}.

(D.71)

The first sub-leading term (
O(\lambda^{2})
) in the four-point function is generated by the exchange diagram, which contributes

\displaystyle<\phi(x_{1})\phi(x_{2})\phi(x_{3})\phi(x_{4})>

\displaystyle=\sum_{s,t,u}\lambda^{2}J_{\Delta_{E}=1,\Delta=1}(x_{1},...,x_{4})

(D.72)

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda^{2}}{\pi^{5}}\frac{\pi}{4}\frac{1}{(x_{13}x_{24})^{2}}P^{(0)}_{(1,1)}(\chi),

(D.73)

where
P^{(0)}_{(1,1)}(\chi)
is written explicitly in equation (
2.3.1
) and has a small
\chi
expansion

\displaystyle<\phi(x_{1})\phi(x_{2})\phi(x_{3})\phi(x_{4})>

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(x_{13}x_{24})^{2}}\frac{\lambda^{2}}{4\pi^{4}}\left(\frac{c_{1}-6\zeta(3)}{\chi^{2}}+\frac{2\left(\pi^{2}-3c_{2}\right)}{3\chi}\right)+O\left(\chi^{0}\right),

(D.74)

where we have kept the integration constants from the differential equation.
The first term in the expansion is set to zero since it corresponds to a correction to the weight of the identity operator. The second term corresponds, in the conformal s-channel OPE, to

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(x_{13}x_{24})^{2}}\sum_{h}c_{11h}^{2}\chi^{h-2}{}_{2}F_{1}(h,h,2h,\chi)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(x_{13}x_{24})^{2}}\left(\frac{c_{111}^{2}}{\chi}+O\left(\chi^{0}\right)\right),

(D.75)

where by equating the expansions in the conformal and Polyakov blocks, this corresponds to
c_{\Delta_{E}\Delta\Delta}
in equation (
A.86
).
Equating equations
D.75
and
D.74
fixes the integration constants

\displaystyle c_{1}=6\zeta(3)

(D.76)

\displaystyle c_{2}=-\frac{\pi^{2}}{6}.

(D.77)

This provides the correct numerical factor for the Polyakov block computed in
2.3.1
and the maximally symmetric and Regge-bounded function.

Appendix E
Mellin Transform

The Mellin transform is a topic in itself so the following Appendix starts with an introduction in section
E.1
, before using the 1d Mellin transform defined in the main text in subsection
5.2.1
to consider the case of GFF in section
E.2
. Section
E.3
then tests the sum rules presented in
5.2.4
and addresses some of the issues linked to perturbations and poles. The final section
E.4
lists the expressions for the anomalous dimensions obtained in the model described in
5.2.5
.

E.1
3 Computations to Understand Mellin Formalism

The simple Mellin transform is the following transform, closely related to the Laplace transform

\displaystyle M[g](s)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{dz}{z}g(z)z^{s}

(E.1)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}du\,g(e^{u})\,e^{su},

(E.2)

where the anti-Mellin transform is done through a contour integral parallel to the imaginary axis.
As the Fourier and Laplace transform, the Mellin transform reveals and highlights specific theory elements, making it sometimes simpler to spot patterns and solve correlators. From its inception, it has revealed itself to be very useful in hypergeometric integrals and their generalisations. Given the omnipresence of hypergeometric functions in the context of CFTs, one could expect there to be applications of the Mellin transform to CFT. The founding work in
[
110
]
revealed the power of Mellin transforms for dimension-independent formulations of conformal correlators. Since then, it has been a major tool and a very active area of research whose focus has been perturbative and non-perturbative computations.
Further work
[
109
,
102
,
83
]
developed the tools for efficient strong-coupling computations in this setting, as well as motivation to consider the Mellin transform of correlators as amplitudes by linking them to flat-space scattering amplitudes. The literature over the past decade and more is quite extensive and detailed, so the goal of this section is to be specific rather than extensive to give a feel for the Mellin transform.
1
1

1

I refer the interested reader to
[
72
,
109
,
216
]
for a more exhaustive treatment of the subject.

In this line, I will present three key computations in the Mellin formalism, these are:

•

The Mellin amplitude of contact Witten diagram

•

From Mellin to position space

•

Scattering amplitudes

From this, one can take away the key idea that Mellin formalism is useful because the quantities’ simplicity underlines the theory’s important aspects. At strong coupling, contact interactions in AdS give constant amplitudes, and exchanges operators give poles, which allow for a ‘Feynman Rules’ approach. The main obstacle to the development of this formalism is that going from position to Mellin space and vice versa is relatively hard, even for the simplest quantities. This motivates the quest to understand how to compute quantities directly in Mellin space and relate them to physical observables, such as flat-space scattering amplitudes.

Mellin Amplitudes of Contact Witten Diagrams

We will approach this from the point of view of boundary correlators of operators propagating in a bulk theory on AdS
d+1
.
We introduce a redundant coordinate system called embedding lightcone coordinates (
X_{\mu}X^{\mu}=-R^{2}
)

\displaystyle X^{\mu}

\displaystyle=R\left(\frac{1-x^{2}-z^{2}}{2z},\frac{x^{\mu}}{z},\frac{1+x^{2}+z^{2}}{2z}\right)

\displaystyle P^{\mu}=\lim_{z\rightarrow 0}zX^{\mu}

(E.3)

which map AdS
d+1
to flat space in d+2 dimensions. Note that
P^{\mu}
parametrises the coordinates of the boundary CFT. These lead to easier integrals and simpler propagators. In these coordinates, for example, the bulk-to-boundary propagator is simply

\displaystyle G_{\Delta}(X,P)=\frac{C_{\Delta}}{\left(-2P\cdot X\right)^{\Delta}}

(E.4)

The
n-
point contact integral in this context is

\displaystyle I_{\Delta_{1},...,\Delta_{n}}(P_{1}^{\mu},...,P_{m}^{\mu})=\int_{\text{AdS}_{d+1}}dX\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{C_{\Delta_{i}}}{\left(-2P\cdot X\right)^{\Delta_{i}}}

(E.5)

which we can write as

\displaystyle I_{\Delta_{1},...,\Delta_{n}}(P_{1}^{\mu},...,P_{m}^{\mu})=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\left(\frac{C_{\Delta_{i}}}{\Gamma(\Delta_{i})}\right)\int_{0}^{\infty}[dt]\prod_{i}^{n}t_{i}^{\Delta_{i}-1}\int dXe^{2Q\cdot X}

(E.6)

where

\displaystyle[dt]=\prod_{i=0}^{n}dt_{i}Q^{\mu}=\sum_{i=0}^{n}t_{i}P_{i}^{\mu}

(E.7)

and we use the Schwinger parametrisation

\displaystyle\frac{1}{A^{p_{i}}}=\frac{1}{\Gamma(p_{i})}\int_{0}^{\infty}dt_{i}t_{i}^{p_{i}-1}e^{-t_{i}A}.

(E.8)

The boundary integral of AdS
d+1
is now easy:

\displaystyle\int dXe^{2Q\cdot X}=\pi^{\frac{d}{2}}\int\frac{dz}{z}z^{-\frac{d}{2}}e^{-z+\tfrac{Q^{2}}{z}}

(E.9)

where

\displaystyle Q^{2}=(\sum_{i}P_{i}t_{i})^{2}=\sum_{i>j}-(-2P_{i}\cdot P_{j})t_{i}t_{j}.

(E.10)

The total integral is now

\displaystyle I_{\Delta_{1},...,\Delta_{n}}(P_{1}^{\mu},...,P_{m}^{\mu})=\pi^{\tfrac{d}{2}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\left(\frac{C_{\Delta_{i}}}{\Gamma(\Delta_{i})}\right)\int_{0}^{\infty}[dt]\prod_{i}^{n}t_{i}^{\Delta_{i}-1}\int\frac{dz}{z}z^{-\frac{d}{2}}e^{-z-\sum_{i>j}P_{ij}\tfrac{t_{i}t_{j}}{z}}

(E.11)

where
P_{ij}=-2P_{i}\cdot P_{j}=|x_{i}-x_{j}|^{2}
. By rescaling the variables
s_{i}=\frac{t_{i}}{\sqrt{z}}
, the
z-
integral is simply a gamma function.

\displaystyle I_{\Delta_{1},...,\Delta_{n}}(P_{1}^{\mu},...,P_{m}^{\mu})

\displaystyle=\pi^{\tfrac{d}{2}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\left(\frac{C_{\Delta_{i}}}{\Gamma(\Delta_{i})}\right)\int_{0}^{\infty}[ds]\prod_{i}^{n}s_{i}^{\Delta_{i}-1}e^{-\sum_{i>j}P_{ij}s_{i}s_{j}}\int\frac{dz}{z}z^{\frac{\sum\Delta_{i}-d}{2}}e^{-z}

(E.12)

\displaystyle=A\int_{0}^{\infty}[ds]\prod_{i}^{n}s_{i}^{\Delta_{i}-1}e^{-\sum_{i>j}P_{ij}s_{i}s_{j}}

(E.13)

where

\displaystyle A=\pi^{\tfrac{d}{2}}\Gamma\left(\tfrac{\sum\Delta_{i}-d}{2}\right)\prod_{i=1}^{n}\left(\frac{C_{\Delta_{i}}}{\Gamma(\Delta_{i})}\right)

(E.14)

Since the conformal symmetry fixes the integral up to
n=3
, we separate the cases
n=1,2,3
from the others to make some symmetry explicit. This is also where the Mellin transform comes into play; we introduce the Mellin transform of the exponential function

\displaystyle\exp^{-P_{ij}s_{i}s_{j}}=\int\frac{d\gamma_{ij}}{2\pi i}\frac{\Gamma(\gamma_{ij})}{(P_{ij}s_{i}s_{j})^{\gamma_{ij}}}

(E.15)

to write

\displaystyle I

\displaystyle=A\int_{0}^{\infty}[ds]\prod_{i}^{n}s_{i}^{\Delta_{i}-1}e^{-s_{i}\sum_{j>1}P_{1j}s_{j}+s_{2}s_{3}P_{23}}e^{-\sum_{j>3,i\geq 2}P_{ij}s_{i}s_{j}}

(E.16)

\displaystyle=A\int_{0}^{\infty}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\left(\frac{ds_{i}}{s_{i}}s_{i}^{\Delta_{i}}\right)\exp^{-\sum_{j>1}s_{1}s_{j}P_{1j}-s_{2}s_{3}P_{23}}\prod_{i\geq 2,j>3}^{n}\int\frac{d\gamma_{ij}}{2\pi i}\frac{\Gamma(\gamma_{ij})}{\left(P_{ij}s_{i}s_{j}\right)^{\gamma_{ij}}}

(E.17)

Collecting the
s_{i}
-dependent factor for
j\geq 4
, we can integrate them

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\tfrac{ds_{i}}{s_{i}}s_{i}^{\Delta_{i}}\exp^{-s_{1}s_{i}P_{1i}}s_{i}^{-\sum_{j=2}^{i-1}\gamma_{j,i}-\sum_{j=i+1}^{n}\gamma_{i,j}}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\tfrac{ds_{i}}{s_{i}}\exp^{-s_{1}s_{i}P_{1i}}s_{i}^{\gamma_{1i}}

(E.18)

\displaystyle=\frac{\Gamma(\gamma_{1i})}{(s_{1}P_{1i})^{\gamma_{1i}}}

(E.19)

where we have defined redundant variables through the following

\displaystyle\gamma_{ii}=-\Delta_{i}

(E.20)

\displaystyle\gamma_{ij}=\gamma_{ji}

(E.21)

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n}\gamma_{ij}=0.

(E.22)

Therefore, integrating
s_{j}
(
j\geq 4
), we obtain

\displaystyle I

\displaystyle=A\int\prod_{j>i>3}^{n}\frac{d\gamma_{ij}\Gamma(\gamma_{ij})}{\left(P_{ij}\right)^{\gamma_{ij}}}\prod_{j=4}^{n}\frac{\Gamma(\gamma_{1j})}{\left(P_{1j}\right)^{\gamma_{1j}}}\frac{\Gamma(\gamma_{2j})}{\left(P_{2j}\right)^{\gamma_{2j}}}\frac{\Gamma(\gamma_{3j})}{\left(P_{3j}\right)^{\gamma_{3j}}}I_{s}

(E.23)

\displaystyle I_{s}

\displaystyle=\int\frac{ds_{1}ds_{2}ds_{3}}{s_{1}s_{2}s_{3}}s_{1}^{\gamma_{12}+\gamma_{13}}s_{2}^{\gamma_{12}+\gamma_{23}}s_{3}^{\gamma_{13}+\gamma_{23}}\exp^{-s_{1}s_{2}P_{12}-s_{1}s_{3}P_{13}-s_{2}s_{3}P_{23}}

(E.24)

\displaystyle=\int\frac{dt_{1}dt_{2}dt_{3}}{t_{1}t_{2}t_{3}}t_{1}^{\gamma_{23}}t_{2}^{\gamma_{13}}t_{3}^{\gamma_{12}}\exp^{-t_{3}P_{12}-t_{2}P_{13}-t_{1}P_{23}}

(E.25)

\displaystyle=\frac{\Gamma(\gamma_{12})}{\left(P_{12}\right)^{\gamma_{12}}}\frac{\Gamma(\gamma_{13})}{\left(P_{13}\right)^{\gamma_{13}}}\frac{\Gamma(\gamma_{23})}{\left(P_{23}\right)^{\gamma_{23}}}

(E.26)

This gives us the final result

\displaystyle I=\pi^{\tfrac{d}{2}}\Gamma\left(\tfrac{\sum\Delta_{i}-d}{2}\right)\prod_{i=1}^{n}\left(\frac{C_{\Delta_{i}}}{\Gamma(\Delta_{i})}\right)\int\prod_{j>i}^{n}\frac{[d\gamma_{ij}]}{2\pi i}\frac{\Gamma(\gamma_{ij})}{\left(P_{ij}\right)^{\gamma_{ij}}}.

(E.27)

Here, the integral is over the non-redundant variables
\gamma_{ij}
satisfying the relations in
E.20
. If one defines a truncated Mellin transform or Mellin amplitude where

\displaystyle f(P_{ij})=\int\prod_{j>i}^{n}\frac{[d\gamma_{ij}]}{2\pi i}\frac{\Gamma(\gamma_{ij})}{\left(P_{ij}\right)^{\gamma_{ij}}}M(\gamma_{ij})

(E.28)

this is the claim that contact Witten diagrams give a constant Mellin amplitude.

From Mellin to Position Space

As expected, doing the Mellin integral (or anti-Mellin transform) is trivial for the cases
n=1,2,3
:

•

n=1

\displaystyle I_{n=1,\Delta}=\pi^{\tfrac{d}{2}}\Gamma\left(\tfrac{\Delta-d}{2}\right)\left(\frac{C_{\Delta}}{\Gamma(\Delta)}\right)\delta_{\Delta,0}

(E.29)

where
\sum_{i}\gamma_{1i}=-\Delta=0
was used

•

n=2

\displaystyle I_{n=2}(\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2},P_{12}=|\vec{x}_{1}-\vec{x}_{2}|^{2})=\delta_{\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2}}\pi^{\tfrac{d}{2}}\Gamma\left(\tfrac{2\Delta-d}{2}\right)\left(\frac{C_{\Delta}}{\Gamma(\Delta)}\right)^{2}\frac{\Gamma{(\Delta)}}{\left(P_{12}\right)^{\Delta}}

(E.30)

where
\gamma_{12}=\Delta_{1}=\Delta_{2}

•

n=3

\displaystyle I=\pi^{\tfrac{d}{2}}\Gamma\left(\tfrac{\sum\Delta_{i}-d}{2}\right)\prod_{i=1}^{n}\left(\frac{C_{\Delta_{i}}}{\Gamma(\Delta_{i})}\right)\frac{\Gamma(\gamma_{12})\Gamma(\gamma_{13})\Gamma(\gamma_{23})}{\left(P_{12}\right)^{\gamma_{12}}\left(P_{13}\right)^{\gamma_{13}}\left(P_{23}\right)^{\gamma_{23}}}

(E.31)

where

\displaystyle\gamma_{12}

\displaystyle=\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}-\Delta_{3}

(E.32)

\displaystyle\gamma_{13}

\displaystyle=\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{3}-\Delta_{2}

(E.33)

\displaystyle\gamma_{23}

\displaystyle=\Delta_{2}+\Delta_{3}-\Delta_{1}

(E.34)

For
n=4
, we obtain our first non-trivial integrals since solving the redundancy equations
E.20
leaves two variables free

\displaystyle\gamma_{ij}=\begin{pmatrix}-\Delta_{1}&\gamma_{12}&\Delta_{1}-\gamma_{12}-\gamma_{14}&\gamma_{14}\\
&-\Delta_{2}&\gamma_{14}+\tfrac{1}{2}\Delta_{23,14}&\tfrac{1}{2}\Delta_{124,3}-\gamma_{12}-\gamma_{14}\\
&&-\Delta_{3}&\gamma_{12}-\frac{1}{2}\Delta_{12,34}\\
&&&-\Delta_{4}\end{pmatrix}

(E.35)

where

\displaystyle\Delta_{a_{1}a_{2}..,b_{1}b_{2}..}=\sum_{i}\Delta_{a_{i}}-\sum_{j}\Delta_{b_{j}}

(E.36)

\displaystyle I_{n=4,\Delta_{i}}(P_{ij})

\displaystyle=\pi^{\tfrac{d}{2}}\Gamma\left(\tfrac{\sum\Delta_{i}-d}{2}\right)\prod_{i=1}^{n}\left(\frac{C_{\Delta_{i}}}{\Gamma(\Delta_{i})}\right)P_{13}^{-\Delta_{1}}P_{23}^{\frac{1}{2}\Delta_{14,23}}P_{24}^{-\frac{1}{2}\Delta_{124,3}}P_{34}^{\frac{1}{2}\Delta_{12,34}}\int\frac{d\gamma_{12}d\gamma_{14}}{(2\pi i)^{2}}M(\gamma_{12},\gamma_{14})

(E.37)

\displaystyle M(\gamma_{12},\gamma_{14})

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{P_{14}P_{23}}{P_{13}P_{24}}\right)^{-\gamma_{14}}\left(\frac{P_{12}P_{34}}{P_{13}P_{24}}\right)^{-\gamma_{12}}\prod_{j>i=1}^{4}\Gamma\left(\gamma_{ij}\right)

(E.38)

where the conformal cross-ratios

\displaystyle u

\displaystyle=\frac{P_{12}P_{34}}{P_{13}P_{24}}

(E.39)

\displaystyle v

\displaystyle=\frac{P_{14}P_{23}}{P_{13}P_{24}}

(E.40)

and conformal prefactors appear naturally in this formalism. Additionally, the crossing of operators is related to the exchange of the Mellin variables
\gamma_{ij}
. Additionally, each cross-ratio is in 1-1 correspondence with a non-redundant Mellin variable.

For simplicity, we will keep to the case where
\Delta_{i}=\Delta
, so we have

\displaystyle I_{n=4,\Delta_{i}}(P_{ij})

\displaystyle=\pi^{\tfrac{d}{2}}\Gamma\left(\tfrac{4\Delta-d}{2}\right)\left(\frac{C_{\Delta_{i}}}{\Gamma(\Delta_{i})}\right)^{4}P_{13}^{-\Delta}P_{24}^{-\Delta}\int\frac{d\gamma_{12}d\gamma_{14}}{(2\pi i)^{2}}M(\gamma_{12},\gamma_{14})

(E.41)

\displaystyle M(\gamma_{12},\gamma_{14})

\displaystyle=v^{-\gamma_{14}}u^{-\gamma_{12}}\Gamma\left(\gamma_{12}\right)^{2}\Gamma\left(\gamma_{14}\right)^{2}\Gamma\left(\Delta-\gamma_{12}-\gamma_{14}\right)^{2}

(E.42)

To solve this integral, one can relate the Mellin integral to an Euler-type integral through Hypergeometric functions (see
[
93
]
):

\displaystyle{}_{2}F_{1}(a,b,c,z)

\displaystyle=\frac{\Gamma(c)}{\Gamma(a)\Gamma(b)}\int\frac{ds}{2\pi i}\frac{\Gamma(s)\Gamma(1-s)\Gamma(b-s)}{\Gamma(c-s)}(-z)^{-s}

(E.43)

\displaystyle=\frac{\Gamma(c)}{\Gamma(a)\Gamma(b)}\int\frac{ds}{2\pi i}\frac{\Gamma(s)\Gamma(a-s)\Gamma(b-s)\Gamma(c-a-b+s)}{\Gamma(c-a)\Gamma(c-b)}(1-z)^{-s}

(E.44)

\displaystyle=\frac{\Gamma(c)}{\Gamma(b)\Gamma(c-b)}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{dt}{t(1-t)}t^{b}(1-t)^{c-b}(1-zt)^{-a}.

(E.45)

We can therefore rewrite the integral

\displaystyle\int\frac{d\gamma_{12}d\gamma_{14}}{(2\pi i)^{2}}

\displaystyle M_{n=4,\Delta}(\gamma_{12},\gamma_{14})

\displaystyle=\int\frac{d\gamma_{12}d\gamma_{14}}{(2\pi i)^{2}}\Gamma\left(\gamma_{12}\right)^{2}\Gamma\left(\gamma_{14}\right)^{2}\Gamma\left(\Delta-\gamma_{12}-\gamma_{14}\right)^{2}u^{-\gamma_{12}}v^{-\gamma_{14}}

(E.46)

\displaystyle=\int\frac{\gamma_{14}}{2\pi i}\Gamma\left(\gamma_{14}\right)^{2}\Gamma\left(\Delta-\gamma_{14}\right)^{2}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{dt}{t(1-t)}\left(\frac{t(1-t)}{1-(1-u)t}\right)^{\Delta-\gamma_{14}}

(E.47)

\displaystyle=\frac{\Gamma(\Delta)^{4}}{\Gamma(2\Delta)v^{\Delta}}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{dt}{t(1-t)}\left(\frac{vt(1-t)}{1-(1-u)t}\right)^{\Delta}{}_{2}F_{1}(\Delta,\Delta,2\Delta,1-\frac{vt(1-t)}{1-(1-u)t})

(E.48)

\displaystyle=\frac{\Gamma(\Delta)^{4}}{\Gamma(2\Delta)v^{\Delta}}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{dt}{t(1-t)}X^{\Delta}{}_{2}F_{1}(\Delta,\Delta,2\Delta,1-X).

(E.49)

In the familiar case
\Delta=1
this is

\displaystyle\int\frac{d\gamma_{12}d\gamma_{14}}{(2\pi i)^{2}}M_{n=4,\Delta}(\gamma_{12},\gamma_{14})

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}dt\left(\frac{1}{1-(1-u)t-vt(1-t)}\right)\log\left(\frac{vt(1-t)}{1-(1-u)t}\right)

(E.50)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}dt\left(\frac{1}{(1-t(1-\chi))(1-t(1-\bar{\chi}))}\right)\log\left(\frac{(1-t)t(1-\chi)(1-\bar{\chi})}{1-t(1-\chi\bar{\chi})}\right)

(E.51)

where

\displaystyle u

\displaystyle=\chi\bar{\chi}

\displaystyle v

\displaystyle=(1-\chi)(1-\bar{\chi})

(E.52)

and the remaining integral can be evaluated easily using partial fractions to give to usual box integral result

\displaystyle\int\frac{d\gamma_{12}d\gamma_{14}}{(2\pi i)^{2}}M_{n=4,\Delta=1}(\gamma_{12},\gamma_{14})=\frac{1}{2(\chi-\bar{\chi})}\left(\log(\chi\bar{\chi})\log(\frac{1-\chi}{1-\bar{\chi}})+2\textup{Li}(\chi)-2\textup{Li}(\bar{\chi})\right)

(E.53)

Explicitely computing the explicit Mellin and Anti-Mellin transform is often impractical, especially when considering more complex cases. Instead, using Mellin amplitudes directly to find quantities such as CFT data and scattering amplitudes is more convenient.

Scattering Amplitudes

The conditions on the Mellin variables in (
E.20
) can be written as the on-shell conserved momenta of
n
particles of mass
m_{i}^{2}=\Delta_{i}
:

\displaystyle\gamma_{ij}=p_{i}\cdot p_{j}

(E.54)

\displaystyle p_{j}\cdot\sum p_{i}=0

(E.55)

\displaystyle p_{i}^{2}=-m^{2}

(E.56)

The Mellin amplitudes can then be expressed in terms of Mandelstam variables

\displaystyle-(p_{1}+p_{2})^{2}=\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}-2\gamma_{12}=s

(E.57)

\displaystyle-(p_{1}+p_{3})^{2}=\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{3}-2\gamma_{13}=t

(E.58)

\displaystyle-(p_{1}+p_{4})^{2}=\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{4}-2\gamma_{14}=u

(E.59)

\displaystyle s+t+u=\sum_{i}\Delta_{i}

(E.60)

In
[
109
,
84
]
, the Mellin amplitudes for Witten diagrams were related to flat-space amplitudes in a certain limit:

\displaystyle\frac{\mathcal{T}_{n}(l_{s},k_{i})}{l_{s}^{n\tfrac{d-1}{2}-d-1}}=\lim_{\theta\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{\mathcal{N}}\int\frac{d\alpha}{2\pi i}\alpha^{\frac{d-\sum\Delta_{i}}{2}}\text{e}^{\alpha}M_{n}(\gamma_{ij}=\frac{\theta^{2}}{2\alpha}l_{s}^{2}k_{i}\cdot k_{j})\theta^{n\frac{d-1}{2}-d-1}

(E.61)

where
l_{s}
is a scattering length intrinsic to the theory,
k_{i}
are the external momenta,
\mathcal{N}
is a normalisation factor to match with flat-space amplitudes. The limit in
\theta=\frac{R}{l_{s}}
controls the flat-space limit and suppresses the disconnected diagram contribution naturally, finally the integral in
\alpha
, like the anti-Mellin transform is also on a contour parallel to the imaginary axis.
We will illustrate this formula in the following examples.

Disconnected Contributions

For disconnected contributions, the Mellin amplitude has no integration variables, so the replacement conditions for the Mellin variables in equations
E.20
give the momentum conservation condition expected from flat-space scattering.

Contact Interactions

The Mellin transform of a four-point function with a contact interaction
-\lambda_{4}\phi_{\Delta}^{4}
is

\displaystyle M_{n=4,\Delta}=\lambda_{4}\pi^{\tfrac{d}{2}}\Gamma\left(\tfrac{4\Delta-d}{2}\right)\left(\frac{C_{\Delta_{i}}}{\Gamma(\Delta_{i})}\right)^{4}P_{13}^{-\Delta}P_{24}^{-\Delta}\Gamma\left(\gamma_{12}\right)^{2}\Gamma\left(\gamma_{14}\right)^{2}\Gamma\left(\Delta-\gamma_{12}-\gamma_{14}\right)^{2}

(E.62)

The associated ‘truncated’ Mellin amplitude is

\displaystyle\hat{M}_{n=4,\Delta}

\displaystyle=\lambda_{4}\pi^{\tfrac{d}{2}}\Gamma\left(\tfrac{\sum\Delta-d}{2}\right)\left(\frac{C_{\Delta_{i}}}{\Gamma(\Delta_{i})}\right)^{4}

(E.63)

\displaystyle\hat{M}_{n,\Delta}

\displaystyle=\lambda_{n}\mathcal{N}\Gamma\left(\tfrac{\sum\Delta-d}{2}\right)

(E.64)

However, the interaction constant
\lambda_{4}
is a dimensionful parameter. To make it dimensionless, we define a new interaction constant through dimensional analysis by using the only dimensionful parameter of our theory: the radius
R
of AdS:

\displaystyle\tilde{\lambda_{n}}

\displaystyle=\lambda_{n}R^{d+1+n\frac{1-d}{2}}

(E.65)

\displaystyle=\lambda_{n}\left(\theta l_{s}\right)^{d+1+n\frac{1-d}{2}}

(E.66)

The scattering amplitude formula (
E.74
) then gives

\displaystyle\frac{\mathcal{T}_{n}(l_{s},k_{i})}{l_{s}^{n\tfrac{d-1}{2}-d-1}}

\displaystyle=\lim_{\theta\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{\mathcal{N}}\int\frac{d\alpha}{2\pi i}\alpha^{\frac{d-\sum\Delta_{i}}{2}}\text{e}^{\alpha}\tilde{\lambda}_{n}\mathcal{N}\Gamma\left(\tfrac{\sum\Delta-d}{2}\right)\left(\theta l_{s}\right)^{d+1+n\frac{1-d}{2}}\theta^{n\frac{d-1}{2}-d-1}

(E.67)

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{n}(l_{s},k_{i})

\displaystyle=\tilde{\lambda}_{n}\int\frac{d\alpha}{2\pi i}\alpha^{\frac{d-\sum\Delta_{i}}{2}}\text{e}^{\alpha}\Gamma\left(\tfrac{\sum\Delta-d}{2}\right)

(E.68)

\displaystyle=\tilde{\lambda}_{n}

(E.69)

Derivative Interactions

Though the preceding example may seem slightly artificial, derivative interactions are where this formalism starts showing some of its power (as well as exchange diagrams). Using the identity

\displaystyle\partial_{\mu}K_{\Delta_{1}}\partial^{\mu}K_{\Delta_{2}}=\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}(K_{\Delta_{1}}K_{\Delta_{2}}-2P_{12}K_{\Delta_{1}+1}K_{\Delta_{2}+1}),

(E.70)

the diagram corresponding to the Wick contraction such that the derivatives of an interaction such as
\lambda_{4,2}(\partial\phi_{\Delta})^{2}\phi_{\Delta}^{2}
are acting on the legs
1
and
2
gives the conformal integral

\displaystyle\lambda_{4}\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}(D_{\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}}-2P_{12}D_{\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{3}+1\Delta_{4}+1}).

(E.71)

Which gives the Mellin integral

\displaystyle\lambda_{4,2}\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\int[d\gamma_{ij}]\prod_{i<j}\frac{\Gamma(\gamma{ij})}{(P_{ij})^{\gamma_{ij}}}\left(1-2P_{12}\frac{\Gamma(\gamma_{12}+1)\Gamma\left(\tfrac{\sum\Delta+2-d}{2}\right)\Gamma(\Delta_{1})\Gamma(\Delta_{2}))}{\Gamma(\gamma_{12})P_{12}\Gamma\left(\tfrac{\sum\Delta-d}{2}\right)\Gamma(\Delta_{1}+1)\Gamma(\Delta_{2}+1)}\right)

(E.72)

and the truncated Mellin amplitude

\displaystyle M_{\partial^{2},n=4}

\displaystyle=\tilde{\lambda_{4,2}}\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\left(\theta l_{s}\right)^{d+1+n\frac{1-d}{2}-2}\left(1-2\frac{\gamma_{12}}{\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}}\tfrac{\sum\Delta+2-d}{2}\right)\mathcal{N}\Gamma\left(\tfrac{\sum\Delta-d}{2}\right)

(E.73)

Notice that the effective interaction term
\tilde{\lambda}_{4,2}
is additionally suppressed due to the derivative interactions, so the constant term above will vanish in the
\theta\rightarrow\infty
limit. The other term gives

\displaystyle\frac{\mathcal{T}_{\partial^{2},n=4}(l_{s},k_{i})}{l_{s}^{n\tfrac{d-1}{2}-d-1}}

\displaystyle=-2k_{1}\cdot k_{2}\tilde{\lambda_{4,2}}\Gamma\left(\tfrac{\sum\Delta-d}{2}+1\right)l_{s}^{d+1+n\frac{1-d}{2}}\int\frac{d\alpha}{2\pi i}\alpha^{\frac{d-\sum\Delta_{i}}{2}}\text{e}^{\alpha}\frac{1}{2\alpha}

(E.74)

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{\partial^{2},n=4}(l_{s},k_{i})

\displaystyle=-\tilde{\lambda_{4,2}}k_{1}\cdot k_{2}

(E.75)

Which corresponds to the contribution from the equivalent Wick contractions on a Feynman diagram with the same interaction term. It is easy to see that the
\theta
-suppression means that for a generic interaction term, only the term with the highest power of
\gamma_{ij}
survives so that for a product of fields
\phi_{i}
pairwise contracted with a derivative power
\alpha_{i,j}
, the resulting flat-space amplitude will be

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{n}=\lambda_{n}\prod_{i,j}\left(k_{i}\cdot k_{j}\right)^{\alpha_{i,j}}

(E.76)

E.2
Generalized Free Field Theory

Let us consider the simplest possible example of 1d CFT. The GFF correlator for four identical scalars of dimension
\Delta_{\phi}
reads

f^{\text{GFF}}(t)=1+t^{2\Delta_{\phi}}+\left(\frac{t}{1+t}\right)^{2\Delta_{\phi}}.

(E.77)

Of course, in this case, we know very well the spectrum of exchanged operators, which includes the identity, “two-particle” operators of the schematic form

\displaystyle[\phi\phi]_{n}\sim\phi\,\square^{n}\phi,

(E.78)

and their conformal descendants. The conformal primary operators have dimension
\Delta_{n}=2\Delta_{\phi}+2n
for
n\geq 0
. As usual, the fact that the dimensions of these operators are separated by integers creates huge degeneracies. In particular, we should be worried by the coincidence of left and right poles in (
5.79
) and (
5.80
). This happens for
s=0
, where the first right pole (associated to the exchange of the identity in the
s
-channel) coincides with the second left pole (associated to the exchange of
\phi^{2}
in the
t
-channel) and at
s=2\Delta_{\phi}
for the crossing symmetric case. The situation is depicted in Figure
E.1
.

s
2\Delta_{\phi}
s
\mathcal{C}
\epsilon
2\Delta_{\phi}
2\Delta_{\phi}\!\!+\!\!\epsilon

Figure E.1
:
Left
: The configuration of the poles for GFF. The left and right poles coincide in
s=0
and
s=2\Delta_{\phi}
.
Right
: the right poles are slightly shifted to the right so that the contour
\mathcal{C}
can run in the middle.

Coincident poles generate a problem in the realization of the contour
\mathcal{C}
, which was defined precisely in such a way as to separate the left and right poles. To avoid this issue, one can slightly split the poles by shifting the right (or left) poles by a small amount, as shown in Figure
E.1
. The result of the procedure outlined in subsection
5.2.1
leads to a Mellin transform which depends on the shift
\epsilon

M^{\text{GFF}}_{\epsilon}(s)=\frac{1}{\Gamma(s)\Gamma(2\Delta_{\phi}-s)}\left(\frac{\Gamma(s)\Gamma(2\Delta_{\phi}+\epsilon-s)}{\Gamma(2\Delta_{\phi})}+\frac{1}{s-\epsilon}+\frac{1}{s-2\Delta_{\phi}-\epsilon}-\frac{1}{s}-\frac{1}{s-2\Delta_{\phi}}\right)\,.

(E.79)

Taking the inverse Mellin transform (
5.75
) with the contour
\mathcal{C}
depicted in Figure
E.1
and then taking the limit
\epsilon\to 0
, one recovers (
E.77
). Taking the limit
\epsilon\to 0
before integrating leads to the straightforward result
M_{0}(s)=\frac{1}{\Gamma(2\Delta_{\phi})}
; however, the contour
\mathcal{C}
is not well-defined and one cannot consistently take the inverse Mellin transform.
2
2

2

However, the correction to this is of order
M_{\epsilon}(s)-M_{0}(s)=\frac{\epsilon}{s^{2}}+\frac{\epsilon}{(s-2\Delta_{\phi})^{2}}
which is only non-negligible in the
s\rightarrow 0
and
s\rightarrow 2\Delta_{\phi}
limit. Therefore, for all other purposes (for example, for comparisons at perturbative level or sum rule results), we can effectively take the
M^{GFF}_{0}(s)
result.
Notice that this procedure is simply the limit in the weak sense normally considered in distribution theory. In particular, the identity

\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\int_{p-i\infty}^{p+i\infty}\frac{ds}{2\pi i}\left(\frac{1}{s-p-\epsilon}-\frac{1}{s-p+\epsilon}\right)t^{s}=t^{p}

(E.80)

tells us that the function
\frac{1}{s-p-\epsilon}-\frac{1}{s-p+\epsilon}
in the limit
\epsilon\to 0
defines a delta function
\delta(s-p)
for Mellin integration.
3
3

3

Attentive readers will probably have noticed that the individual terms in
\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\int_{p-i\infty}^{p+i\infty}\frac{ds}{2\pi i}(\frac{1}{s-p-\epsilon})
diverge and one cannot use the residue theorem. However, the combination does not have this problem since
\left(\frac{1}{s-p-\epsilon}-\frac{1}{s-p+\epsilon}\right)=\frac{2\epsilon}{(s-p)^{2}-\epsilon^{2}}
which ensures convergence at large
s
.
In this sense, we can say that the Mellin transform for GFF is a distribution rather than a function.
This issue is caused by the exchange of the identity operator and by the degeneracy typical of GFF.
An alternative route for obtaining the Mellin transform of the GFF correlator would be to use equations (
5.77
) and (
5.78
) with the well-known expressions of the GFF OPE coefficients

c^{(0)}_{n}=\frac{2\Gamma(2n+2\Delta_{\phi})^{2}\Gamma(2n+4\Delta_{\phi}-1)}{\Gamma(2\Delta_{\phi})^{2}\Gamma(2n+1)\Gamma(4n+4\Delta_{\phi}-1)}

(E.81)

and summing over
\Delta_{n}=2\Delta_{\phi}+2n
. Doing this, one finds the result
M(s)=\frac{1}{\Gamma(2\Delta_{\phi})}
which is consistent with the fact that equations (
5.77
) and (
5.78
) were obtained under the assumption of non-coincident poles. Also, in this case, one could slightly shift the position of the poles in
\psi_{0}(s)
(or
\psi_{\infty}(s)
) and recover an expression which coincides with
M_{\epsilon}^{GFF}
in the weak limit
\epsilon\to 0
.

E.3
Sum Rules for the 1d Mellin Transform

Testing the sum rules (
5.106
) or (
5.104
) on a fully nonperturbative spectrum is a task which is momentarily out of reach. Therefore, we start by testing it on the simplest possible 1d CFT, i.e. generalised free field theory and perturbations thereof.

Generalized free theories

One may immediately raise several objections to our attempt to apply the sum rules (
5.104
) to GFF theories. First of all, as discussed in section
E.2
, the definition of the Mellin amplitude for GFF requires the inclusion of a cut-off to regulate the exchange of the identity operator. Furthermore, a crucial assumption in deriving the sum rules was that
M(s)
must have zeros for
s=-k
and
s=2\Delta_{\phi}+k
. Still, GFF is precisely the example where two-particle operators with dimension
2\Delta_{\phi}+2n+k
are exchanged; therefore, those zeros are absent. We will see that these issues can be avoided by considering the function

\displaystyle F_{p}(s)=\frac{1}{(2\Delta_{\phi}+2p-s)(2\Delta_{\phi}+2p-s+1)}\,.

(E.82)

Since this function has two poles and no residue at infinity, we have the property

\displaystyle\text{Res}_{s=2\Delta_{\phi}+2p}(F_{p}(s))+\text{Res}_{s=2\Delta_{\phi}+2p+1}(F_{p}(s))=0\,.

(E.83)

Furthermore, one can easily check from (
E.79
) that
M_{\epsilon}^{\text{GFF}}(2\Delta_{\phi}+2p)=M_{\epsilon}^{\text{GFF}}(2\Delta_{\phi}+2p+1)
for
p\in\mathbb{N}
. Combining these two properties, it is clear that the last term in (
5.102
) vanishes even though
M(s)
has no zeros at the positions of the poles of
F_{p}(s)
. We are then left with a sum over the residues of
M(s)
. For GFF, the position of the poles in principle depends on the regulator
\epsilon
, but the regulator’s role in (
E.79
) is to separate the left and right poles. This is precisely what we have done to go from (
5.102
) to (
5.103
). Therefore, equation (
5.106
) can be used with
\epsilon\to 0
and, inserting the GFF spectrum
\Delta=2\Delta_{\phi}+2n
we end up with the following sum rule

\displaystyle\sum_{n,k}c^{(0)}_{n}\tfrac{(-1)^{k+1}\Gamma(4(\Delta_{\phi}+n))\Gamma(2\Delta_{\phi}+2n+k)}{\Gamma(2\Delta_{\phi}+2n)^{2}\Gamma(4(\Delta_{\phi}+n)+k)\Gamma(-2n-k)\Gamma(k+1)}\tfrac{2(2\Delta_{\phi}+4p+1)(\Delta_{\phi}+k+2n)}{(k+2n-2p-1)(k+2n-2p)(2\Delta_{\phi}+k+2n+2p+1)(k+2(\Delta_{\phi}+n+p))}=0\,.

(E.84)

Notice that the
\Gamma(-2n-k)
factor in the denominator kills all the terms in this sum that are not compensated by a pole in the second ratio. The sum over
k
then receives only two contributions at
k=2p-2n
and
k=2p-2n-1
, which are present only for
p\geq n
. We are then left with a finite sum over
n

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{p}c_{n}^{(0)}\frac{\Gamma(2p+1)\Gamma(4(n+\Delta_{\phi}))\Gamma(2(p+\Delta_{\phi}))\left(\Delta_{\phi}-2n^{2}-4\Delta_{\phi}n+n+2\Delta_{\phi}p\right)}{\Gamma(2(n+\Delta_{\phi}))^{2}\Gamma(2(p-n+1))\Gamma(2(n+p+2\Delta_{\phi}+1))}=0\,.

(E.85)

Each value of
p
leads to an equation for
c_{p}^{(1)}
in terms of all the other OPE coefficients with a lower index. In other words, this sum rule can be solved recursively for
c^{(0)}_{n}
determining everything in terms of
c^{(0)}_{0}
, which sets the overall normalization. Doing so, one easily finds that the unique solution to this sum rule is provided by (
E.81
). The same strategy can be used to determine the OPE coefficients for a fermionic GFF and a GFF with
O(N)
symmetry. For these theories, sum rules are obtained inserting (
E.82
) into (
5.100
) and using the GFF spectrum, and read

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\sum_{\Delta,k}c_{\Delta}\frac{(-1)^{k+1}\Gamma(2\Delta)\Gamma(\Delta+k)}{\Gamma(\Delta)^{2}\Gamma(2\Delta+k)\Gamma(2\Delta_{\phi}-\Delta-k)\Gamma(k+1)}(\frac{1}{\Delta+k+p}-\frac{1}{2\Delta_{\phi}-\Delta-k+p})=0\,.

(E.86)

The GFF spectrum
\Delta=2\Delta_{\phi}+n
for exchanged operators has the effect of truncating the sum above because of the factor of
\Gamma(2\Delta_{\phi}-\Delta-k)
in the denominator. The example of bosonic GFF in section
E.3
illustrates the case of even
n
, namely
\Delta=2\Delta_{\phi}+2n
. Here we extend this to odd integers, namely
\Delta=2\Delta_{\phi}+2n+1
, in covering the free fermionic model. We also consider the free bosonic and fermionic models with O(
N
) symmetry.

In the case of a free fermion theory, the spectrum
\Delta=2\Delta_{\phi}+2n+1
of exchanged operators leads to the sum rule

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{p}c_{n}\frac{2\Gamma(2p+1)\Gamma(2(p+\Delta_{\phi}))\left(\Delta_{\phi}-2\Delta_{\phi}p+2n^{2}+4\Delta_{\phi}n+n\right)\Gamma(4n+4\Delta_{\phi}+2)}{\Gamma(2p-2n+1)\Gamma(2n+2\Delta_{\phi}+1)^{2}\Gamma(2(p+n+2\Delta_{\phi}+1))}=0.

(E.87)

Just like in the bosonic case, see (
E.85
), this is a recursive relation for the OPE coefficients, whose general solution is

\displaystyle c_{n}^{(0)}=\frac{2\Gamma(2n+2\Delta_{\phi}+1)^{2}\Gamma(2n+4\Delta_{\phi})}{\Gamma(2\Delta_{\phi})\Gamma(2\Delta_{\phi}+1)\Gamma(2n+2)\Gamma(4n+4\Delta_{\phi}+1)}\,.

(E.88)

This is confirmed by the vanishing of (
E.87
). As usual, this is true up to an overall scaling, and the choice in (
E.88
) is set by requiring
c_{0}^{(0)}=1
.

Consider a bosonic four-point function with
O(N)
symmetry. We write the Mellin amplitude as a sum of the singlet, antisymmetric and traceless symmetric contributions

\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{M}}^{1234}(s)=\hat{\mathcal{M}}^{S}\delta^{12}\delta^{34}+\hat{\mathcal{M}}^{A}(\delta^{13}\delta^{24}-\delta^{14}\delta^{23})+\hat{\mathcal{M}}^{T}(\frac{\delta^{13}\delta^{24}+\delta^{14}\delta^{23}}{2}-\frac{\delta^{12}\delta^{34}}{N}),

(E.89)

with scalar coefficient functions
\hat{\mathcal{M}}^{S}(s)
,
\hat{\mathcal{M}}^{A}(s)
and
\hat{\mathcal{M}}^{T}(s)
.
In these channels, exchanged operators will be of the form
\phi_{i}\partial^{2n}_{x}\phi^{i}
,
\phi^{[i}\partial^{2n+1}_{x}\phi^{j]}
,
\phi^{(i}\partial^{2n}_{x}\phi^{j)}
respectively, with same spectra of exchanged operators previously seen (
\Delta=2\Delta_{\phi}+2n
,
\Delta=2\Delta_{\phi}+2n+1
,
\Delta=2\Delta_{\phi}+2n
respectively). Therefore we get the corresponding OPE coefficients

\displaystyle c_{n}^{S}

\displaystyle=c^{S}_{0}\frac{2\Gamma(2n+2\Delta_{\phi})^{2}\Gamma(2n+4\Delta_{\phi}-1)}{\Gamma(2\Delta_{\phi})^{2}\Gamma(2n+1)\Gamma(4n+4\Delta_{\phi}-1)}\,,

(E.90)

\displaystyle c_{n}^{A}

\displaystyle=c^{A}_{0}\frac{2\Gamma(2n+2\Delta_{\phi}+1)^{2}\Gamma(2n+4\Delta_{\phi})}{\Gamma(2\Delta_{\phi})\Gamma(2\Delta_{\phi}+1)\Gamma(2n+2)\Gamma(4n+4\Delta_{\phi}+1)}\,,

(E.91)

\displaystyle c_{n}^{T}

\displaystyle=c^{T}_{0}\frac{2\Gamma(2n+2\Delta_{\phi})^{2}\Gamma(2n+4\Delta_{\phi}-1)}{\Gamma(2\Delta_{\phi})^{2}\Gamma(2n+1)\Gamma(4n+4\Delta_{\phi}-1)}\,,

(E.92)

up to a normalisation factor which can be easily found by looking at the first identity contribution in the different channels. This gives the known result (see
[
49
]
)

\displaystyle c_{n}^{S}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\frac{2\Gamma(2n+2\Delta_{\phi})^{2}\Gamma(2n+4\Delta_{\phi}-1)}{\Gamma(2\Delta_{\phi})^{2}\Gamma(2n+1)\Gamma(4n+4\Delta_{\phi}-1)}\,,

(E.93)

\displaystyle c_{n}^{T}

\displaystyle=\frac{2\Gamma(2n+2\Delta_{\phi})^{2}\Gamma(2n+4\Delta_{\phi}-1)}{\Gamma(2\Delta_{\phi})^{2}\Gamma(2n+1)\Gamma(4n+4\Delta_{\phi}-1)}\,,

(E.94)

\displaystyle c_{n}^{A}

\displaystyle=-\frac{2\Gamma(2n+2\Delta_{\phi}+1)^{2}\Gamma(2n+4\Delta_{\phi})}{\Gamma(2\Delta_{\phi})^{2}\Gamma(2n+2)\Gamma(4n+4\Delta_{\phi}+1)}\,.

(E.95)

The same procedure for free fermions with O(
N
) symmetry leads to

\displaystyle c_{n}^{S}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\frac{2\Gamma(2n+2\Delta_{\phi}+1)^{2}\Gamma(2n+4\Delta_{\phi})}{\Gamma(2\Delta_{\phi})\Gamma(2\Delta_{\phi}+1)\Gamma(2n+2)\Gamma(4n+4\Delta_{\phi}+1)}\,,

(E.96)

\displaystyle c_{n}^{T}

\displaystyle=\frac{2\Gamma(2n+2\Delta_{\phi}+1)^{2}\Gamma(2n+4\Delta_{\phi})}{\Gamma(2\Delta_{\phi})\Gamma(2\Delta_{\phi}+1)\Gamma(2n+2)\Gamma(4n+4\Delta_{\phi}+1)}\,,

(E.97)

\displaystyle c_{n}^{A}

\displaystyle=-\frac{2\Delta_{\phi}\Gamma(2n+2\Delta_{\phi})^{2}\Gamma(2n+4\Delta_{\phi}-1)}{\Gamma(2\Delta_{\phi})^{2}\Gamma(2n+1)\Gamma(4n+4\Delta_{\phi}-1)}\,.

(E.98)

Perturbative Sum Rules

In this section we further test our sum rules by using known CFT data for a class of perturbations around GFF. These perturbations, which will be treated in great details in subsection
5.2.5
, are constructed by introducing an effective field theory in AdS
2
background and considering the 1d boundary conformal field theory through the usual holographic dictionary. In particular, we will be interested in quartic contact interaction with derivatives. A classification of these independent contact interactions is given e.g. in
[
55
]
, where the authors find there is a one-parameter family labelled by
L
, where
4L
is the number of derivatives in the schematic interaction
(\partial^{L}\Phi)^{4}
. As we mentioned at the end of subsection
5.2.3
, single terms in the perturbative expansion of the correlator may have a worse Regge behaviour than the general bound (
5.85
). In particular, let us consider a perturbed correlator

f(t)=f^{\text{GFF}}(t)+g_{L}f_{L}^{(1)}(t)+O(g_{L}^{2}),

(E.99)

where
L
labels the maximum number of derivatives in the quartic interaction (i.e. the interaction term may involve a combination of terms with
\ell\leq L
derivatives) and
g_{L}
is the associated coupling. The Regge behaviour of this correlator is determined by the term with the maximum number of derivatives, and it reads
[
55
]

f_{L}^{(1)}(z)\sim z^{2\Delta+2L-1}\qquad z\to\frac{1}{2}+i\infty.

(E.100)

According to our discussion in subsection
5.2.4
, the associated Mellin amplitude will behave as

M^{(1)}_{L}(s)\sim|s|^{2L-1}\qquad|s|\to\infty,

(E.101)

and we need to choose a function
F_{p}(s)
which vanishes at infinity faster than
|s|^{-2L}
. Here we will derive and check the sum rules for the cases
L=0
and
L=1
. The strategy is the following. We use equation (
5.104
) to write down nonperturbative sum rules with a specific function
F_{p}(s)
, which will be chosen to decay sufficiently fast at
|s|\to\infty
at a given value of
L
. We then expand the CFT data as

\displaystyle\Delta

\displaystyle=2\Delta_{\phi}+2n+g_{L}\gamma_{L,n}^{(1)}+\mathcal{O}(g_{L}^{2})\,,

(E.102)

\displaystyle c_{\Delta}

\displaystyle=c^{(0)}_{n}+g_{L}c^{(1)}_{L,n}+\mathcal{O}(g_{L}^{2})

(E.103)

and derive perturbative sum rules for
\gamma^{(1)}_{L,n}
and
c^{(1)}_{L,n}
. We then check that these sum rules are satisfied by the
L=0,1
results obtained in
[
55
]
, which read

\displaystyle\gamma_{0,n}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)_{n}\left((\Delta_{\phi})_{n}\right){}^{2}\left(2\Delta_{\phi}-\frac{1}{2}\right)_{n}}{(1)_{n}\,(2\Delta_{\phi})_{n}\left(\left(\Delta_{\phi}+\frac{1}{2}\right)_{n}\right){}^{2}}\,,

(E.104)

\displaystyle\gamma_{1,n}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=A_{\Delta_{\phi}}^{-1}\gamma_{0,n}^{(1)}\frac{2n(4\Delta_{\phi}+2n-1)}{(\Delta_{\phi}+n-1)(2\Delta_{\phi}+2n+1)}(16\Delta_{\phi}^{5}-13\Delta_{\phi}^{3}-3\Delta_{\phi}^{2}+16\Delta_{\phi}n^{4}+8n^{4}+64\Delta_{\phi}^{2}n^{3}

\displaystyle+16\Delta_{\phi}n^{3}-8n^{3}+96\Delta_{\phi}^{3}n^{2}+8\Delta_{\phi}^{2}n^{2}-24\Delta_{\phi}n^{2}-2n^{2}+64\Delta_{\phi}^{4}n-28\Delta_{\phi}^{2}n-2\Delta_{\phi}n+2n)

(E.105)

where the result for
\gamma^{(1)}_{1,n}
differs from
[
55
]
by an overall factor

A_{\Delta_{\phi}}\equiv\frac{\Delta_{\phi}(\Delta_{\phi}+1)(\Delta_{\phi}+2)(4\Delta_{\phi}-1)(4\Delta_{\phi}+1)^{2}(4\Delta_{\phi}+3)}{(2\Delta_{\phi}+1)^{2}(2\Delta_{\phi}+3)}\,,

(E.106)

which we introduced to normalize the anomalous dimension as
\gamma_{1,1}^{(1)}=1
. Notice that
\gamma_{1,0}^{(1)}=0
. This is equivalent to a choice of basis for the set of independent interactions that can be built with up to one derivative. We discuss this issue in detail in subsection
5.2.5
. The OPE coefficients
c^{(1)}_{n}
are given by the relation

c^{(1)}_{L,n}=\partial_{n}(\gamma^{(1)}_{L,n}c^{(0)}_{n})\,.

(E.107)

Let us start with the case
L=0
. In that case, the Regge behaviour is better than the GFF case, so we could even choose the function

F_{p}(s)=\frac{1}{s+p}\,,

(E.108)

which gives the sum rules

\displaystyle\sum_{\Delta,k}c_{\Delta}\frac{(-1)^{k+1}\Gamma(2\Delta)\Gamma(\Delta+k)}{\Gamma(\Delta)^{2}\Gamma(2\Delta+k)\Gamma(2\Delta_{\phi}-\Delta-k)\Gamma(k+1)(\Delta+k+p)(-2\Delta+\Delta+k-p)}=0\,.

(E.109)

Notice that we can also use the function (
E.108
) as a building block from which we can construct more suppressed functions of the class (
5.105
), for example

\displaystyle F_{p,p+1}(s)=F_{p}(s)-F_{p+1}(s)=\frac{1}{(s+p)(s+p+1)}\,.

(E.110)

As we discussed below (
5.105
), the crossing-antisymmetric part of this function decays as
\frac{1}{s^{3}}
at large
s
and for this reason, we will also use it for the case
L=1
.
Inserting the expansions (
E.102
) and (
E.103
) into the sum rule (
E.109
), we get two contributions: a finite sum from the terms where
k=p-2n

\displaystyle\tilde{\omega}_{p}

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{[\frac{p}{2}]}c_{n}^{(1)}\frac{\Gamma(p+1)\Gamma(4(n+\Delta_{\phi}))\Gamma(p+2\Delta_{\phi})}{(p-2n)!\,\Gamma(2(n+\Delta_{\phi}))^{2}\Gamma(2n+p+4\Delta_{\phi})}

(E.111)

\displaystyle+c_{n}^{(0)}\gamma_{n}^{(1)}\frac{\Gamma(p+1)\Gamma(4(n+\Delta_{\phi}))\Gamma(p+2\Delta_{\phi})}{2(\Delta_{\phi}+p)(p-2n)!\,\Gamma(2(n+\Delta_{\phi}))^{2}\Gamma(2n+p+4\Delta_{\phi})}\eta(\Delta_{\phi},n,p)\,,

(E.112)

where

\displaystyle\eta(\Delta_{\phi},n,p)=1+2(\Delta_{\phi}+p)(-2\psi_{2n+2\Delta_{\phi}}+2\psi_{4n+4\Delta_{\phi}}-2\psi_{2n+p+4\Delta_{\phi}}+\psi_{p+2\Delta_{\phi}}+\psi_{p+1})

(E.113)

with
\psi_{n}=\frac{\Gamma^{\prime}(n)}{\Gamma(n)}
, and an infinite sum

\tilde{\omega}^{\prime}_{p}=\sum_{n,k\neq p-2n}c_{n}^{(0)}\gamma_{n}^{(1)}\frac{2(k+2n)!(\Delta_{\phi}+k+2n)\Gamma(4(n+\Delta_{\phi}))\Gamma(k+2n+2\Delta_{\phi})}{k!(k+2n-p)\Gamma(2(n+\Delta_{\phi}))^{2}\Gamma(k+4(n+\Delta_{\phi}))(2\Delta_{\phi}+k+2n+p)}\,.

(E.114)

This last term does not allow us to follow the same strategy we used for GFF to extract the value of
\gamma^{(1)}_{L,n}
. This is consistent with the fact that these sum rules are not as constraining as in the GFF case, and we may expect more than one solution. Therefore, instead of using the sum rules to derive the CFT data, we limit ourselves to numerically checking the validity of the equation

\omega_{p}=\tilde{\omega}_{p}+\tilde{\omega}^{\prime}_{p}=0

(E.115)

by inserting the data (
E.104
), (
E.3
) and (
E.107
). In Figure
E.2
, we show our results for the case
p=0
both for the functional
\omega_{0}
and for the combination (
E.110
), i.e. the functional
\omega_{0}-\omega_{1}
. It is very clear from the plot that the latter shows a faster convergence reflecting the better asymptotic behaviour at
|s|\to\infty
. In Figure
E.3
, we show the analogous plot for the
L=1
case, where we used the function (
E.110
) since we needed a large
s
behaviour at least
\frac{1}{s^{3}}
.

Figure E.2
:
L=0
sum rules for external dimensions
\Delta_{\phi}=1,\Delta_{\phi}=2
and different functionals
\omega_{0}
and
\omega_{0}-\omega_{1}
. The plot shows the value of the finite sum truncated after
N
terms in a logarithmic scale. The better convergence is seen for the
\omega_{0}-\omega_{1}
functional, due to a better large
s
behaviour.

Figure E.3
:
Sum rule applied for the
L=1
case for
\Delta_{\phi}=1,2,3
, truncated after summing over N terms seen on a logarithmic scale. The slower convergence compared to
L=0
is to be expected from the worse Regge behaviour.

We conclude with a couple of remarks. Since the finite sum in (
E.111
) always ranges up to
[\frac{p}{2}]
, we can always find a combination of functionals
\omega_{k}
such that the contributions proportional to
c^{(1)}_{L,n}
are all cancelled, and we can write down a sum rule which only involves the anomalous dimensions
\gamma^{(1)}_{L,n}

\sum_{k=0}^{2p+1}\frac{(-1)^{1+k}\,\Gamma(2p+2)}{\Gamma(k+1)\,\Gamma(2p+3-k)}\,\omega_{k}=0\,.

(E.116)

The simplest case is
p=0
where (
E.116
) gives
\omega_{0}-2\omega_{1}=0
. Even after doing that, however, we are left with an infinite sum with alternating signs, which we did not manage to use constructively to extract a solution. Nevertheless, notice that once the anomalous dimensions are known, we can use our sum rules at fixed
p
to determine recursively the value of the OPE coefficients
c^{(1)}_{L,n}
following the same strategy as in the GFF case. This provides a very convoluted way to rederive the relation (
E.107
).

Poles and Series

In this Appendix, we address some subtleties in the perturbative expansion of the Mellin amplitudes in the context of the analytic sum rules. In all the following, we will consider a function
\hat{F}(s)
defined nonperturbatively and its expansion in a small parameter
\lambda
, as well as the functional

\displaystyle\omega_{K}[\hat{F}]=\oint_{\mathbb{C}|_{\infty}}\frac{ds}{2\pi i}\hat{F}(s)K(s),

(E.117)

defined as the contour integral of
\hat{F}(s)
over the circle at
\infty
in the complex plane with an integration kernel
K(s)
. This functional is well defined and vanishing for meromorphic functions
\hat{F}(s)
and
K(s)
such that

\displaystyle\hat{F}(s)K(s)\underset{|s|\rightarrow\infty}{\sim}s^{-1-\alpha}\qquad\alpha>0.

(E.118)

Nonperturbative Zeros and Perturbative Poles

The nonperturbative Mellin function
\hat{M}(s)
has zeros at positions
s=2\Delta+n
, while the perturbative expansion has poles in those positions. The reason for this and the subtleties of evaluating the sum rule will be illustrated with an example.

Let
f(s)
be a well-behaved function
4
4

4

By this, we mean that
f
is meromorphic, has no essential singularities and behaves as
s^{-\alpha}
at large s, where
\alpha
is a positive parameter. For simplicity, we take
f(s)
to be regular without poles. However, including these poles (different to those of
\hat{F}
) is straightforward and does not change the conclusions.
. We define the
\lambda
-dependent function

\displaystyle\hat{F}(s)=\frac{(s-1)f(s)}{s-1-\lambda}.

(E.119)

Upon expanding
F(s)
in the parameter
\lambda
, we obtain the geometric series

\displaystyle\hat{F}(s)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\left(\frac{\lambda}{s-1}\right)^{k}f(s),

(E.120)

whose radius of convergence is
\lambda<|s-1|
.
This perturbative series cannot be evaluated at finite
\lambda
in
s=1
. However, several features are noteworthy. At
s=1
, we have poles of increasing degree at each order in the
\lambda
expansion, while the nonperturbative expression has a simple zero at that point. Let us evaluate the contour integral

\displaystyle\omega_{1}[\hat{F}]=\oint\frac{ds}{2\pi i}\frac{\hat{F}(s)}{s-1}

(E.121)

in three different ways. Firstly, by inserting the nonperturbative
\hat{F}

\displaystyle\oint\frac{ds}{2\pi i}\frac{\hat{F}(s)}{s-1}=\oint\frac{ds}{2\pi i}\frac{f(s)}{s-1-\lambda}=f(1+\lambda).

(E.122)

This method is the most intuitive way of evaluating (
E.121
) and is perfectly well-defined. However, it requires the knowledge of the full nonperturbative function, which is generally unknown.

Secondly, we can separately consider the poles of the integration kernel
\frac{1}{s-1}
from those of the function
\hat{F}
:

\displaystyle\oint\frac{ds}{2\pi i}\frac{\hat{F}(s)}{s-1}

\displaystyle=\textrm{Res}(\frac{1}{s-1})\hat{F}(1)+\sum_{s^{*}}\frac{\textrm{Res}(\hat{F})(s^{*})}{s^{*}-1}=f(1+\lambda).

(E.123)

This is not identical to the previous method since it assumes more (notably that the poles of
K(s)
and those of
\hat{F}
,
s^{*}
above, are distinct) and requires less information from the nonperturbative function; only the residues of
\hat{F}(s)
and the value of
\hat{F}(s_{i})
at a finite number of points are required to evaluate (
E.121
).

Finally, one can use the series expansion of
\hat{F}(s)

\displaystyle\omega_{1}[\hat{F}]

\displaystyle=\oint\frac{ds}{2\pi i}\left(\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{\lambda^{k}}{(s-1)^{k+1}}f(s)\right)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{\lambda^{k}f^{(k)}(1)}{k!},

(E.124)

which gives the
\lambda
-expansion of
f(1+\lambda)
. Under the assumption that
f
is analytic near 1, we can then resum the Taylor series to obtain
f(1+\lambda)
and obtain the same result as the previous two methods. In this specific example, the only operation which is not allowed is to evaluate
\hat{F}(1)
using the nonperturbative series since the latter has a vanishing radius of convergence for
s=1
. In the next subsection, we will explore a setting where the perturbative evaluation of the functional is problematic and requires the truncation of the series at a fixed order.

Bad Regge Behaviour

We now consider another pathological case where the perturbative expansion of the correlator makes the large
s
behaviour worse than that of the nonperturbative expression. To illustrate this, we take the example

\displaystyle\hat{F}(s)

\displaystyle=\frac{f(s)}{1-\lambda s^{2}},

(E.125)

and evaluate the functional

\displaystyle\omega_{3}[\hat{F}]

\displaystyle=\oint_{\mathbb{C}|_{\infty}}\frac{ds}{2\pi i}\frac{\hat{F}(s)}{s(s^{2}-1)}.

(E.126)

The convergence of the integral (
E.126
) imposes a bound on the large
s
behaviour of
f(s)
in (
E.125
)
5
5

5

As in the previous case, we consider that
f(s)
is meromorphic in
s
and ignore its poles since they act as spectators in the comparison between the perturbative and nonperturbative case.

\displaystyle f(s)

\displaystyle\underset{s\rightarrow\infty}{\sim}s^{4-\alpha}

\displaystyle\alpha

\displaystyle>0.

(E.127)

The functional (
E.126
) can then be evaluated explicitly as

\displaystyle\omega_{3}[\hat{F}]=-f(0)+\frac{f(1)+f(-1)}{2(1-\lambda)}-\frac{\lambda}{2(1-\lambda)}\left(f\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lambda}}\right)+f\left(-\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lambda}}\right)\right).

(E.128)

The perturbative expansion of
\hat{F}(s)
is

\displaystyle\hat{F}(s)=f(s)+\lambda s^{2}f(s)+\lambda^{2}s^{4}f(s)+O(\lambda^{3}).

(E.129)

In this perturbative setting, even with a stricter condition on the large
s
behaviour of
f(s)

\displaystyle f(s)

\displaystyle\underset{s\rightarrow\infty}{\sim}s^{-\alpha}

\displaystyle\alpha

\displaystyle>0,

(E.130)

we must truncate the series at order
\lambda
in order to evaluate the functional

\displaystyle\omega_{3}[f(s)+\lambda s^{2}f(s)]

\displaystyle=-f(0)+\frac{f(1)+f(-1)}{2}+\lambda\frac{f(1)+f(-1)}{2}.

(E.131)

Comparing (
E.131
) to the small-
\lambda
expansion of (
E.128
)

\displaystyle\omega_{3}[\hat{F}]

\displaystyle=-f(0)+\frac{f(1)+f(-1)}{2}+\lambda\frac{f(1)+f(-1)}{2}-\frac{\lambda}{2}(f(\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lambda}})+f(-\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lambda}}))+o(\lambda),

(E.132)

we see that the condition of convergence (
E.130
) is exactly what is needed to get rid of the final terms and find agreement between the results

\displaystyle-\frac{\lambda}{2}(f(\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lambda}})+f(-\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lambda}}))

\displaystyle\underset{\lambda\rightarrow 0}{\sim}\lambda^{1+\frac{\alpha}{2}}\underset{\lambda\rightarrow 0}{=}o(\lambda).

(E.133)

We therefore have agreement between the functional of the truncated
\lambda
-expansion of
\hat{F}(s)
and the truncated
\lambda
-expansion of the functional of
\hat{F}(s)

\displaystyle\omega_{3}[\hat{F}|_{\lambda}]=\left(\omega_{3}[\hat{F}]\right)|_{\lambda},

(E.134)

where the order at which we are required to truncate is controlled by the large
s
behaviour of
K
(which can always be chosen to satisfy the convergence condition (
E.118
) at a given order of expansion).

E.4
Anomalous Dimensions for Higher Derivative Interactions

This section lists the various results for the polynomial part of the anomalous dimension in equation (
5.136
). The
Mathematica
notebook attached to the arXiv submission of
[
2
]
has values of
L
ranging from
L=0
to
L=8
as well as a function
FindBootstrapPolynomial[L,
\Delta

to compute
\mathcal{P}_{L,n}(\Delta_{\phi})
for arbitrary
L
(the function gets slower and slower at higher
L
, but in principle, it works for any
L
).

Translations

To pay hommage to the four languages I have used during this PhD, there is some text in English, French, Italian and German. The English translations are as follows:

‘Will there be sky above me,//and will the stars

// enfold my eyes//with their maternal light?’

(T.1)

’By other routes, by other ports // you will reach the shore, not here for crossing

lighter ship must carry you’

(T.2)

‘A thousand-beat waltz//alone gives lovers//

three hundred and thirty-three times// the time to write a story.’

(T.3)
